# National Security Power and Constitutional Imbalance

Edition label: `2026-11`

## Executive Summary

Post-Watergate reforms expanded intelligence oversight, yet disclosure disputes, negotiated access, and limited individual sanctions recur. This paper offers a descriptive synthesis: a vocabulary, *constrained negotiated oversight*, and a coding scheme for how executive information control interacts with congressional incentives and uneven enforcement. It makes no predictive claim.

Four historical episodes (Pike and Church, Iran-Contra, National Security Agency bulk collection, and the Central Intelligence Agency Interrogation Program) are coded on eight process indicators developed with those cases; their fit is not independent validation and no cutoff is applied. In each, observations count against executive absorption of disclosure duties, and in none does the bargaining description fit where partisan and self-correction accounts do not. A Watergate tapes contrast, scored on the Senate committee's own demand, shows the same early conduct and a failed committee suit; the tapes came through a prosecutor insulated by regulation, under a holding that reserved congressional demands.

The Epstein Files Transparency Act (EFTA) supplies a contemporary congruence assessment using a frozen August 30, 2026 corpus of 1.38 million documents measured in twelve released data sets, with separately dated litigation updates.[^es-corpus-guidance] It is included for executive custody and a documented, unresolved intelligence question (Appendix D). The analysis identifies disclosure and accounting disputes, not an overall noncompliance percentage. Preliminary judicial compulsion in *Phang v. Blanche* shows an Administrative Procedure Act route in use; its jurisdictional basis is on appeal. Unified party control and the Oversight Committee's party-line 19–24 rejection of a substitute recommending a House-directed suit to enforce its Bondi subpoena leave partisan explanations open.

The contribution is a bounded descriptive account and conditional reform proposals. Explicit access rights, protected judicial review, collectible remedies, and a civil action for congressional subpoenas could strengthen enforcement, but their effectiveness depends on standing, sovereign-immunity waivers, constitutional limits, and protection of victims.

## Introduction

In the half-century since the Pike and Church committees of 1975–76, the United States has built an elaborate architecture of intelligence oversight: the Hughes-Ryan Amendment, the Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Act (FISA), permanent intelligence committees in both chambers, the Inspector General Act, the Privacy and Civil Liberties Oversight Board, the USA FREEDOM Act, and the anti-torture provisions of the National Defense Authorization Act (NDAA) for fiscal year 2016.[^intro1] Yet selective disclosure, disputes over what compliance requires, uneven congressional escalation, and external rupture recur across decades, alongside reforms whose reach is contested.

Institutional scholarship already identifies electoral incentives, fragmented jurisdiction, secrecy, and executive control of access, alongside staffing and expertise.[^lit4] These explanations are not reducible to incapacity. By 2014 the Senate intelligence committee had produced a study of more than 6,700 pages, but only its executive summary and its findings and conclusions became public; the Central Intelligence Agency (CIA) had searched committee staff computers and referred staff conduct to the Department of Justice (DOJ).[^intro2] The episode illustrates a distinction this paper develops: substantial investigative capacity can coexist with restricted publication and weak individual accountability. It also produced policy and institutional consequences that a blanket failure verdict would conceal.

This paper offers a descriptive synthesis of those structural explanations, organized as a bargaining vocabulary. Information asymmetry, incentive misalignment, weak enforcement capacity, and limited expertise can shift oversight from compelled into negotiated compliance. Selective disclosure becomes an executive offer, while non-escalation can function as acceptance. This does not establish shared motives to stage an empty check: the same behavior can reflect disagreement, privacy constraints, anticipated legal defeat, or a strategic decision to secure partial gains. *Constrained negotiated oversight* names the recurring configuration to be described, not an assumed absence of substantive oversight.

The paper codes four historical episodes: Pike and Church (1975–76), Iran-Contra (1986–91), National Security Agency (NSA) bulk collection (2001–15), and the CIA Interrogation Program (2002–14). It adds the Watergate tapes dispute (1973–74) as a contrast and a contemporary congruence assessment of the Epstein Files Transparency Act (EFTA).[^intro3] The evidence concerns U.S. constitutional institutions; extension to parliamentary or other national systems would require a different account of executive-legislative relations.[^intro-scope]

The contribution is twofold. First, eight process indicators organize observable bargaining conduct, bracketed by the source of disclosure and individual-accountability outcomes.[^intro4] Second, EFTA permits the paper to distinguish released-record measurements from legal compliance, and congressional action from judicial enforcement. The cases suggest that expanding information duties alone may leave important enforcement dependencies intact. They do not show that resources never help, that every negotiated settlement fails, or that structural remedies are sufficient. The recommendations therefore specify conditional institutional changes whose effects remain to be tested.

---

## Literature Review

The underlying problem is old: inter-branch checking depends on information, and one branch holds it.[^lit1] The Federalist authors recognized the executive's advantage in secrecy and intelligence (Federalist Nos. 64 and 70) but did not theorize how asymmetric control of the *information* on which checking depends would affect the checks themselves. Covert-action authority under the National Security Act of 1947's “other functions and duties” clause (§ 102(d)(5)), later constrained by Hughes-Ryan (1974), sits there. Senator Frank Church put the point this way in transmitting his committee's report:

> The root cause of the excesses which our record amply demonstrates has been failure to apply the wisdom of the constitutional system of checks and balances to intelligence activities . . . . The founding fathers foresaw excess as the inevitable consequence of granting any part of government unchecked power. This has been demonstrated in the intelligence field where, too often, constitutional principles were subordinated to a pragmatic course of permitting desired ends to dictate and justify improper means. Our recommendations are designed to place intelligence activities within the constitutional scheme for controlling government power.[^church-ii]

The closest literature supplies structural explanations the present framework inherits. Institutional histories trace Congress's evolving oversight arrangements; Johnson describes a repeated pattern of shock, intense oversight, remedial legislation, and a return to weak patrolling.[^lit2][^lit3][^lit-intel-secondary] Zegart's account centers on rational political incentives that generate collectively weak oversight: committee arrangements and electoral rewards make intelligence oversight unattractive even where members possess formal powers.[^lit4] She attributes weak oversight to information, incentives, and institutions regardless of party; the increment claimed here is the sequence in which those factors operate within one episode and the separate recording of access, policy, institutional, and sanction outcomes. Kibbe likewise asks how oversight arrangements themselves can become part of the problem, and Clark shows how restricted briefings can inhibit consultation and make silence appear to endorse executive choices.[^lit5] On the legal side, Koh traced the Iran-Contra affair to executive initiative, congressional acquiescence, and judicial tolerance, and a separate literature treats negotiated accommodation over information as the ordinary and usually adequate mode.[^lit-accommodation] *Constrained* adds a narrower condition: the record is access-restricted within Congress, limiting whom its recipients may consult and what they may verify or publish; custody alone marks every information dispute, the Watergate contrast included.

Four adjacent frameworks bear on the argument. McCubbins and Schwartz's "police patrols versus fire alarms" shows oversight rationally substitutes reactive monitoring for active patrol; fire alarms presuppose rules that let outsiders see agency conduct and complain about it, which classified domains largely lack, a limit Zegart identified.[^lit6] Michael Glennon argues that a permanent "Trumanite" security network operates with effective independence from the elected "Madisonian" institutions that supply legitimacy; the bargaining vocabulary describes how the two interact.[^lit7] Kathleen Clark's account of limited-notification practice is the most direct existing statement of the bargaining mechanism.[^lit8] Heidi Kitrosser's analysis of information “funneling” (non-public disclosure to small congressional groups, marked by a “near absence of public accountability”) supplies the distinction between formal and substantive accountability used here.[^lit9]

A substantive rival holds that law, press, courts, and inspectors general can constrain national-security power even without complete transparency or prosecutions. The summaries the project holds associate this view with Goldsmith's *Power and Constraint*; no argument or page is attributed to the book itself.[^lit10] Withdrawal of legal opinions, altered surveillance authority, and anti-torture legislation can count as correction on that view, and this paper cannot refute it merely by finding residual executive discretion after reform. The discriminating question is whether identified constraints alter access or conduct and endure.

**Limited-source engagement for this edition.** Six books that bear on the argument were not read in full: Goldsmith's *Power and Constraint*, Johnson's *A Season of Inquiry Revisited* and *Spy Watching*, Banks and Raven-Hansen's *National Security Law and the Power of the Purse*, Schwarz and Huq's *Unchecked and Unbalanced*, and Rudenstine's *The Age of Deference*. The project holds them only as summaries drawn from publisher descriptions and reviews, and for Goldsmith an Internet Archive lending-library copy protected by Adobe digital-rights management, which it could not read; no argument or page is attributed to their text.[^lit-limited] Full-text engagement is deferred to a later edition.

These literatures motivate the synthesis. Incentives and information restrictions may explain non-escalation; legal remedies may offer partial correction; bargains may establish durable institutions. Congress can obtain real gains through negotiation, threatened sanctions, appropriations, or litigation, and such gains count against any absolute incapacity claim.[^lit11]

---

## Conceptual Framework

*Constrained negotiated oversight* is a descriptive label for a recurring configuration in which executive information control makes formal oversight powers operate through bargaining. This section supplies a vocabulary and a coding scheme, not a theory with predictions.[^tf-formalism] Recurrence in selected cases does not establish an invariant equilibrium or identify the causal contribution of any condition.

Congressional *constraint* operates on four dimensions. Congress can **compel** testimony and records by subpoena, but the records sit in executive custody, so production depends on the executive's own search and review. It is constrained in what it can **disclose**, since the most sensitive classified material reaches only the members briefed, under restrictions on consulting staff or counsel that can make silence look like endorsement.[^constraint-access] It is constrained in **enforcement**. Criminal contempt and the false-statement and perjury statutes are ordinarily prosecuted by DOJ; the independent-counsel statute, in force from 1978 to 1992 and from 1994 to 1999, was the exception and produced the Iran-Contra pleas for withholding information from Congress. Neither House appears to have used its inherent contempt power since the 1930s. The Senate's civil-enforcement statute excludes any subpoena to an executive officer acting in an official capacity unless the refusal rests on a personal privilege; the House has no such statute, and whether it has a cause of action is unsettled in the D.C. Circuit (Appendix C, Table C.3).[^constraint-enforcement] And it is constrained in **knowledge**, since the executive controls classification, redaction, and release, so Congress may not know what records exist to demand.[^constraint-redaction] Enforcement is the firmest of the four, though most routes need a chamber vote and the executive or a court.

Our framework's primary scope is oversight structures with four recurring scope conditions.

**Table 1.** **Conditions Defining the Framework's Primary Scope**

| Scope condition | Why it defines our framework's primary scope |
|---|---|
| Scope #1: Executive control over operational architecture **[defining]** | The executive controls the records, systems, and procedures through which oversight must operate. |
| Scope #2: High operational secrecy or technical complexity | The underlying activity is difficult for Congress or the public to observe directly. |
| Scope #3: Legally delegated rather than self-enforcing congressional authority | Congress's oversight power depends on rules and reporting obligations rather than direct structural control. |
| Scope #4: Structurally constrained access to independent domain-specific expertise | Congress cannot freely draw on the staff, counsel, or outside expertise needed to evaluate the domain independently. |

Scope #1 bounds the cases; restricted access within Congress (Scope #4 and the disclosure constraint) separates constrained from ordinary accommodation. Whether Scope #1, or the preferences of congressional majorities, is the nearer constraint in a given episode is an empirical question that the coding records separately.

Scope is assigned from conditions preceding the disputed oversight outcome: custody of records, secrecy rules, governing access law, and the available expertise. A case stays in scope if Congress or a court subsequently breaks executive control; successful enforcement must not be excluded by redefining the outcome as absence of Scope #1. Table 2 records the initial conditions (✓ present, ◑ partly relaxed, ✗ materially relaxed).[^tf4]

**Table 2. Scope Conditions Across the Cases**

| Case | Scope #1 **[defining]** | Scope #2 | Scope #3 | Scope #4 | Partisan\* | Investigating chamber's majority relative to the President |
|---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|---|---|
| Pike and Church (1975–76) | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | Divided | Opposition (both chambers) |
| Iran-Contra (1986–91) | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | Divided | Opposition (House throughout; Senate from January 1987) |
| NSA bulk collection (2001–15) | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ (2001–07: briefings by White House approval, beginning with the intelligence committees' chairs and ranking members; by 2009–11: four committees held the court orders, other members were limited to a classified summary, and most had no cleared staff) | Varied | By sub-period: opposition in the Senate committee in 2001–02 and 2007–08, same party in 2003–06 and 2009–14; the 2008 provider immunity passed an opposition Congress and the 2015 statute an opposition Congress |
| CIA Interrogation Program (2002–14) | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ◑ (to September 6, 2006: information restricted to the committee's chairman and vice chairman; afterward: full-committee access and staff review of more than six million pages) | Varied | By sub-period: opposition in the Senate committee in 2002 and 2007–08 (the review began in December 2007), same party in 2003–06 (access limited to the chairman and vice chairman) and 2009–14 (the study of a program begun under the other party) |
| EFTA (2025–) | ✓ | ◑ (most production unclassified) | ◑ (Section 2(a) mandates publication and Section 2(c)(2) requires justifications to be submitted to Congress) | ◑ (committees have substantive prior knowledge; Members may view the unredacted material only personally, by arrangement through Judiciary Committee leadership, under confidentiality undertakings) | Unified | Same party |
| Watergate tapes (1973–74), contrast | ✓ (tapes in presidential custody) | ◑ (confidential, not classified) | ◑ (committee subpoenas, plus grand-jury and trial subpoenas under ordinary criminal process) | ✗ (no classification barrier to staff or counsel) | Divided | Opposition |

\* **Partisan configuration:** Divided = the opposition party controls at least one chamber of Congress; Unified = the president's party controls Congress; Varied = both occurred within the period. By case: Pike and Church (Democratic Congress, Ford); Iran-Contra (Democratic House and Republican Senate in 1986, Democratic Congress from January 1987, Reagan); NSA bulk collection and the CIA Interrogation Program (unified in 2003–06 and 2009–10, divided in 2001–02, 2007–08 and from January 2011); Watergate tapes (Democratic Congress, Nixon); EFTA (Republican House and Senate, Trump). The last column codes the chamber that did the investigating, the level at which the literature on divided government and investigations finds its effect.

EFTA partly relaxes Scope #2–#4 while preserving initial executive custody, so it is informative about transport beyond classified programs but is not a controlled comparison.

### Hypothesised Preconditions

Table 1 defines scope; Table 3 lists conditions hypothesised to sustain bargaining within it. Information asymmetry tracks Scope #1 and #2, weak enforcement Scope #3, and limited expertise Scope #4; incentive misalignment has no scope counterpart. The four historical cases show all four; EFTA relaxes three (Table 2).

**Table 3. Hypothesised Structural Preconditions**

| Structural condition | Role in our framework |
|---|---|
| Information asymmetry | Congress cannot verify what it is not shown; the post-1947 classification architecture, the classified-opinion framework of the Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Court (FISC), and EFTA's agency-controlled processing practices can each sustain asymmetry. |
| Incentive misalignment | Oversight of secret domains yields limited political reward, while escalation yields visible political risk. The McCubbins-Schwartz fire-alarm model relies on citizens and organized groups to sound alarms; where secrecy denies outsiders information and few organized groups exist, alarms come late, an extension Zegart makes.[^tf2] |
| Weak enforcement capacity | Criminal prosecution ordinarily depends on executive prosecutors; civil litigation, appropriations, chamber discipline, and negotiated concessions have different dependencies. Walsh's Iran-Contra Final Report names the gap: *"congressional oversight alone cannot correct the deficiencies that result when an attorney general abandons the law-enforcement responsibilities of that office and undertakes, instead, to protect the President."*[^tf3] |
| Limited expertise | Intelligence experience is rare in Congress (in the 116th Congress, eighteen of 535 members had ever worked in an intelligence agency);[^tf-expertise] the limited-notification architecture isolates informed members from the staff, counsel, and expertise needed to challenge what they receive. |

Two of the ten indicators bracket each episode: the source of the triggering disclosure and the individual-accountability outcome. Eight process indicators describe the intervening demand, offer, and response. They are coding questions, not independent observations, and no cutoff is applied to them. Mixed and uncertain observations stay visible instead of being rounded into affirmative scores. The surviving Helms plea and fine already defeat a universal absence-of-accountability claim.[^meth-scope]

**Table 4. Observable Indicators of Negotiated Oversight, by Phase**

| Phase | Indicator | What it looks like in practice |
|---|---|---|
| 1 — The scandal | Exogenous rupture *(bracket)* | The triggering disclosure breaks from outside the oversight channel (investigative press, leak, or litigation), not from Congress's own oversight machinery; the fire alarm rings externally. |
| 2 — Congressional request | Demand posture | Congress reacts to the scandal with an investigation whose demand is bounded by what it can compel; the posture ranges from negotiated access to compelled production. |
| 3 — Executive offer | Selective disclosure | Disclosure is partial enough to preserve the appearance of oversight without enabling full checking. |
| 3 — Executive offer | Justification proliferation | Categories of withholding or redaction expand beyond the statute's clear terms. |
| 3 — Executive offer | Delayed compliance | Disclosure arrives long after the demand, or not at all; the codebook dates it (Appendix B). |
| 3 — Executive offer | Executive self-definition | The executive states its own reading of what compliance requires and acts on it. |
| 4 — Congressional response | Majority declines an available remedy | A committee or chamber majority does not use a remedy open to it (a contempt vote, a civil action, a disclosure procedure, legislation). Evidence: recorded votes and unused procedures; merely considering a legally contested route does not establish its availability. |
| 4 — Congressional response | Remedy attempted and defeated outside Congress | A remedy that Congress or its members invoked fails elsewhere (a court ruling, a declination to prosecute, a reversal). Evidence: the congressional initiator and request, the outside decision-maker, the requested result, and the observed disposition; an acknowledged request alone is not partial success. |
| 4 — Congressional response | Informal negotiation | Oversight proceeds through tacit bargaining rather than formal enforcement. |
| 5 — Accountability outcome | Limited individual accountability *(bracket)* | Individual accountability for the underlying abuse of power is limited: it is deferred, achieved and then narrowed or nullified by reversal or pardon, or declined at the outset. Surviving sanctions are recorded separately as counterevidence (Table 7 codes mixed cases ◑; Table 6 states how surviving sanctions are recorded). (Accountability of private principals for ordinary crimes, where it occurs, is a separate question this indicator does not track.) |

In every historical case the triggering disclosure came from outside any access or standing rule Congress had set up: a press report, a leak, a shootdown.[^tf-leaks] That is the fire-alarm model working without its usual infrastructure (Johnson had already called the post-1975 scandals fire alarms),[^lit3] and it may account for the timing of oversight more than for its outcome. Alarms were also raised inside the channel: the Church Committee found and disclosed a concealed interception program, which counts against the bracket under Table 6; the CIA resisted Vice Chairman Rockefeller's inquiry into its interrogation program;[^hf14] and Senators Wyden and Udall warned publicly within classification limits.

Phase 3 carries a dynamic the single-indicator rows do not capture: the *graduated offer*. The executive offer is rarely one release. It is a sequence in which a limited first disclosure is enlarged only under continued pressure, each increment described as fuller transparency. It runs through the Phase 3 indicators and adds none of its own.

**Table 5. What the Vocabulary Describes at Each Phase (descriptions, not predictions)**

| Phase | Pattern the label describes |
|---|---|
| Triggering disclosure | The disclosure that forces the conduct into view originates outside the oversight channel (press, leak, or litigation), not from Congress's own investigative machinery. |
| Congressional request | Congress reacts with an investigation whose demand is bounded by what it can compel, ranging from negotiated access to compelled production. |
| Executive response | The executive preserves formal compliance while narrowing substantive accountability through selective disclosure, delay, self-definition, and expanded justification. |
| Congressional response | A committee or chamber majority objects within constrained channels, escalates weakly or late, or declines an available remedy; remedies pursued elsewhere fail. |
| Reform outcome | Reform follows; whether it alters the bargaining conditions is recorded case by case (Table 6). |
| Accountability outcome | Durable individual accountability for the underlying abuse is limited and often narrowed by reversal, pardon, or declination. Each surviving sanction against an identified official is recorded as counterevidence (Table 6). |

The comparison is exploratory and descriptive. The indicators were developed with these episodes, so their fit cannot validate anything, and no cutoff is applied. Several cells moved when the hearing record was read more fully or the codebook was revised (Appendix B): firm marks track the depth of reading as well as the record. The Watergate tapes dispute of 1973–74 is coded on the same rows as a contrast (Table 7), scored like the others on Congress's own demand, the Senate committee's subpoenas for the tapes. Its early indicators fall within the other episodes' range: the subpoenas were refused and edited transcripts replaced the tapes; the committee's suit was later dismissed without production.[^tf-watergate] The tapes came instead through a Special Prosecutor whom a Justice Department regulation insulated, on a criminal trial subpoena in a matter without classification barriers, and the Supreme Court reserved congressional demands and national-security secrets.[^tf-watergate-insulation] The contrast therefore points to the enforcement channel and the secrecy conditions, not to the early indicators, as what separated this outcome; it is not court-enforced congressional oversight. It is one episode coded by the same author, and a prospectively selected case remains for a later edition.

**Table 6. Observations that would count against the description**

| Claim | Observation against it | Decision rule and limit |
|---|---|---|
| Recurring process configuration | An in-scope episode in which the process indicators are mostly absent or contrary, or are present while the response and outcome rows show access enforced on Congress's demand and durable sanctions | No cutoff is applied and no tally is used as a test. The Watergate tapes column of Table 7 is the one contrast coded: scored on the Senate committee's demand, its early indicators are present; its committee sued after judging statutory and inherent contempt against the President unseemly, and lost; and its outcome came through a prosecutor's criminal process. On this rule the contrast does not count against the description, because its response row shows the committee's own remedy defeated and its accountability bracket is mixed; read to include access enforced for a prosecutor, it might. |
| Executive absorption of disclosure duties (historical cases and EFTA) | Court-ordered or congressionally induced access to identified disputed records, or a durable change to an identified withholding practice | Count concrete production or policy change, including concessions under a credible threat; do not require conviction or unilateral congressional control. Applied to the historical cases, Church's publication over presidential objection, the 1991 notification statute, the USA FREEDOM Act, and the 2015 anti-torture statute count against absorption, and the 2005 statute counts in part, since only a temporary suspension of the interrogation techniques followed it. |
| EFTA timing | Full responsive production, subject to lawful exclusions, by December 19, 2025 | The deadline was missed; this tests timing, not motive or the legality of every exclusion. |
| EFTA justification | An adequate, reviewable accounting of disputed redactions under the governing legal construction | Marker frequency and counts of old guidance are not legal compliance tests. The court has not decided the notice's substantive adequacy. |
| Partisan versus structural explanations | Changes in escalation and access after an actual change of party control | Observe both willingness to invoke a remedy and the remedy's efficacy. No election outcome is assumed. |
| External-disclosure bracket | Congress discovers concealed abuse before an external disclosure | A congressional discovery counts against the claim even if subsequent publication is negotiated. |
| Individual-accountability bracket | Durable penalties for identified official misconduct attributable to oversight | Count surviving sanctions separately from access, policy change, and institution building. |

These rules were written in September 2026, after the observations discussed here; they are not a preregistration. Preliminary judicial compulsion in *Phang* already qualifies any claim that enforcement outside DOJ prosecution is unavailable.[^tf-falsifiability]

---

## Methodology

The design pairs an exploratory historical comparison with contemporary process tracing.[^meth1][^meth3] The four historical episodes were selected for temporal coverage, differing demand postures, and primary-source availability, not sampled from a defined population; the Watergate tapes dispute was added as a contrast because it is widely regarded as oversight that worked. It, and EFTA for its released record, relax the access restrictions that distinguish constrained from ordinary accommodation (Table 2), so the vocabulary should apply to them least; Watergate's process rows did not bear that out (Table 7). Because the concepts were developed using these cases, the recurring pattern is descriptive. It does not establish that executive control caused any outcome or that the vocabulary outperforms an institutional rival.[^meth-design] One author developed the indicators, chose the cases, and coded them; Appendix B gives the codebook, and no second coder has scored them.

Within each episode, the analysis asks who possessed the records, who requested access, what was offered or withheld, what remedy was attempted, and what changed. A defeated enforcement motion is evidence about political willingness; a failed suit is evidence about the remedy invoked and the court's reasons. Successful negotiated concessions count as outcomes. Access, policy change, durable institutions, and individual sanctions are recorded separately, so a gain on one dimension does not disappear because another remained limited.

EFTA extends the comparison to a mostly unclassified disclosure mandate. Enactment by a 427–1 House vote and Senate unanimous consent, enumerated withholding grounds, and an observable production make the case useful, but do not make noncompliance uniquely diagnostic of bargaining.[^meth2] Unified party control, agreement or deference among pivotal members, ordinary administrative burden, legitimate privacy protection, contested statutory interpretation, and case-specific incentives can predict parts of the same sequence. The analysis compares those explanations against dated documents and procedural events; it does not assign a probability to the structural hypothesis or treat an inherited framework marker as proof of an unlawful redaction.

Process tracing distinguishes what each source establishes from what would connect it to a mechanism.[^meth-pt-collier] The March 21, 2025 training establishes an earlier instruction, not its continued use after enactment, and DOJ's privilege construction is documented while its legality is disputed, not measured by a text classifier. The source-to-inference table in the EFTA discussion presents such alternatives directly.

Corpus figures are frozen at August 30, 2026. Litigation is updated separately through the September 16 orders. No September corpus rerun is claimed. Detector performance is measured on its tuning set, with uncertainty and transport limits in Appendix A. The corpus figures rest on unpublished local analysis files, available on request, and are not publicly reproducible; historical findings and publicly filed orders can be examined independently.

The limits are substantial: selection favors visible controversies; withheld content remains unknown; retrospective indicators risk fitting the chosen cases; one author coded them; partly shared sources reduce independence; and EFTA implementation differs from national-security policymaking. The source base is uneven. The NSA case rests mainly on two senators' letters, an inspector-general draft, and a later oversight-board report, and the CIA case on the Senate committee's own summary, one party's account of a two-party dispute. The paper does not identify the causal contribution of oversight failures to trust decline.


---

## Historical Cases

The four cases illustrate different forms of bargaining. Case 1 (Pike and Church, 1975–76) is one disclosure dispute in two chambers: both committees bargained and both defied the executive, and the outcomes diverged where the parent chambers did. Case 2 (Iran-Contra, 1986–91) shows investigation after exposure, with records withheld or destroyed and a four-year statutory fight. Case 3 (NSA bulk collection, 2001–15) shows constraint built into the program's access rules, followed by statutory change. Case 4 (the CIA interrogation program, 2002–14) combines executive counter-pressure and continued secrecy with substantial investigation and anti-torture restrictions.

### Case 1 — Pike and Church: One Disclosure Dispute in Two Chambers (1975–1976)

**Phase 1: The scandal.** Both committees were established in early 1975 (the Senate on January 27, the House in February) after a disclosure from outside the oversight channel: Seymour Hersh's December 1974 *New York Times* report on CIA domestic surveillance, which covered many of the subjects in the CIA's internal "Family Jewels" compilation and first revealed its existence.[^hf1]

**Phase 2: The congressional request.** The two committees ran simultaneously against the same executive counterparty with equivalent formal powers, but under different resolutions, rules, leadership, disclosure procedures, and contempt paths. Church relied mainly on negotiated access and Pike mainly on subpoenas, though each committee used both.[^hf1][^hf2][^hf3]

**Phase 3: The executive offer.** To Church the executive offered negotiated, incremental disclosure, answered the committee's first inquiries about a telegram-interception program only after four months, and pre-empted the proposed statutory charter with Ford's Executive Order 11905 of February 18, 1976, issued 68 days before the April 26, 1976 date on Church's Book I (executive self-definition).[^hf3] To Pike the executive responded with a September 1975 embargo on classified material, loans of documents on condition that the President arbitrate any release, an executive-privilege claim against one of the subpoenas behind the Kissinger contempt resolutions, and a White House briefing in December in place of delivery (delayed and conditional disclosure).[^hf5][^hf-pike-bargain]

**Phase 4: The congressional response.** Both committees bargained and both defied; the outcomes diverged where the parent chambers did. Church negotiated access but did not defer on publication. It disclosed the SHAMROCK interception program on November 6, 1975 after a 7–3 committee vote and over President Ford's request for secrecy, and it published its assassination report over the administration's objection after a closed Senate session recorded no disapproval.[^hf-church-defiance] Its legacy followed: the Senate Select Committee on Intelligence (SSCI, May 1976), the House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence (HPSCI, July 1977), FISA (October 1978), and the Intelligence Oversight Act of 1980; the proposed statutory charter never passed.[^hf-church-cluster] Pike bargained and then contested the bargain's scope. After Ford suspended its access to classified material in September 1975, the committee voted 10–3 on October 1 to accept documents on condition that disputed releases go to the President for written certification, and in December it declared substantial compliance on its contempt subpoenas after a White House briefing.[^hf-pike-bargain] When it moved to release its final report without that review, the House enforced the certification term against its own committee: on January 29, 1976 it voted 246–124, with 127 Democrats joining 119 Republicans, to bar release of a report containing classified material unless restricted to authorized recipients or certified by the President.[^hf2] That was the decision of a cross-party House majority; whether executive classification claims moved those votes is not shown by this record. The findings reached the public through Daniel Schorr's leak to the *Village Voice*.[^hf-schorr-leak]

**Phase 5: Individual accountability.** Accountability was limited and partly reversed. Former Director of Central Intelligence Richard Helms pleaded nolo contendere in 1977 to two misdemeanors of not testifying fully to the Senate about CIA covert action in Chile; the plea, suspended sentence, and fine stood. Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) officials W. Mark Felt and Edward S. Miller, convicted in 1980 for the 1972–1973 warrantless break-ins, were pardoned by President Reagan in April 1981 while their appeal was pending.[^hf-accountability]

### Case 2 — Iran-Contra: Investigation after Exposure (1986–1991)

**Phase 1: The scandal.** The operation broke into public view through two external disclosures a month apart. On October 5, 1986 a cargo plane carrying military supplies to the Contras was shot down over Nicaragua, and its captured survivor, Eugene Hasenfus, said the flight was a CIA-assisted resupply operation.[^hf-hasenfus] On November 3, 1986 the Lebanese magazine *Ash-Shiraa* reported an Iranian faction's account of a secret U.S. spare-parts deal with Iran, and Attorney General Meese's November 25 press conference linked the two.[^hf6]

**Phase 2: The congressional request.** Senate and House select committees, established on January 6 and 7, 1987, held joint hearings and issued a joint report, alongside Walsh's Office of Independent Counsel. The conduct at issue was evasion of statutory appropriations restrictions: the December 1982 Boland limitation barred CIA and Defense support for overthrowing Nicaragua's government, the October 12, 1984 rider cut off funds for fiscal 1985, and National Security Council staff under National Security Advisers Robert McFarlane and John Poindexter, with Lt. Col. Oliver North, channeled covert support through the "Enterprise," on a reading of the rider the Administration disputed and the committees' majority rejected.[^hf6]

**Phase 3: The executive offer.** The offer enlarged only as pressure mounted, from initial denials to the President's review board (the Tower Commission, reporting February 26, 1987). Meanwhile contemporaneous records were destroyed or withheld. Poindexter, North, and others shredded documents from November 21, 1986, as the Attorney General's inquiry began; the notes of Weinberger and Regan, and those dictated by Shultz, reached the Independent Counsel only in 1991 and 1992, after the committees reported; and in December 1992 he learned that Bush had failed to produce a diary with contemporaneous notes (selective disclosure and delayed compliance; the 1992 events are sequels).[^hf-ic-withheld] The immunized testimony was Congress's act, not an executive offer: on the committees' application, with timing negotiated with the Independent Counsel, the district court issued immunity orders, and North and Poindexter testified publicly on July 7–14 and July 15–21, 1987, which bounded what any prosecution could use.[^hf-immunity] The executive also advanced a doctrine of presidential discretion over when to notify Congress of covert action, in the 1986 Cooper opinion, National Security Decision Directive 286 (NSDD-286), which also barred retroactive findings, and President Bush's 1989 letter to Senator Boren, and the committees' minority report endorsed it (justification proliferation and executive self-definition).[^hf9][^hf9-olc-audit]

**Phase 4: The congressional response.** The select committees exposed the misconduct, and Congress then pressed for notice of covert-action findings within 48 hours, written findings, and a ban on retroactive findings. The administrations would compromise on the last two but not on the deadline, and the impasse lasted almost four years, through one vetoed bill, until the 1991 compromise: written, non-retroactive findings and a statutory definition of covert action were enacted, "timely" notice stayed in the statute, and the President's commitment to notify within a few days was recorded beside his reservation of constitutional authority to withhold longer.[^hf-1991-act] The discretion claim survived; the statutory floor moved. Separately, the immunity orders led the D.C. Circuit to set aside the North and Poindexter convictions under *Kastigar*.

**Phase 5: Individual accountability.** Fourteen persons were charged and eleven convicted, by an Independent Counsel appointed by a court at the Attorney General's request; accountability was partial and some consequences were undone. The convictions of North and Poindexter were set aside on use-immunity grounds (North's vacated and remanded for a *Kastigar* hearing, charges later dropped; Poindexter's reversed, in part also because the obstruction statute could not constitutionally reach some of the charged lying to Congress).[^hf7] On December 24, 1992 President Bush pardoned six defendants, including Caspar Weinberger, who had been indicted on June 16, 1992 and was awaiting trial.[^hf8]

### Case 3 — NSA Bulk Collection: Constraint Built into the Program (2001–2015)

**Phase 1: The scandal.** In this case objection inside the oversight channel stayed muted for years, and the disclosures that changed the program came from outside. Bulk collection of telephone records began in late 2001 under presidential authorization and moved under Section 215 of the USA PATRIOT Act by court order in May 2006, the month a *USA Today* report described the collection. A December 2005 *New York Times* report had disclosed a companion program of warrantless interception, not the call-records collection.[^hf-nyt-2005] Edward Snowden's disclosures, first published in *The Guardian* on June 5, 2013, revealed the court order and its Section 215 basis.[^hf13]

**Phase 2: The congressional request.** Access rules changed across the period. From October 2001 briefings required White House approval and began with the chairs and ranking members of the intelligence committees.[^hf10-nsa-oig] By the 2010 and 2011 reauthorizations that was no longer the governing regime: the intelligence and judiciary committees held the court's orders, and a classified summary without legal analysis had been offered to all members, though the House committee did not circulate the 2011 paper. Notification remained limited in a narrower sense. Other members could not read the orders, and, as Senators Ron Wyden and Mark Udall told the Attorney General, most members had no staff cleared to read the documents. The two senators could not say publicly what they knew: their September 21, 2011 letter warned that the government relied on "secret" interpretations of surveillance law, and their March 15, 2012 letter pressed for declassification.[^hf11] Senate Resolution 400 also supplied subpoena authority and a classified-information disclosure procedure.[^hf10]

**Phase 3: The executive offer.** The executive controlled the architecture rather than negotiating disclosure. For the 2001–07 phase, the NSA Inspector General's working draft records that General Hayden gave over 49 briefings to members or staff and recalled that *"no one ever said that NSA should stop what it was doing."* That was Hayden's inference, not a finding of congressional consent (selective disclosure, executive self-definition).[^hf10-nsa-oig] On March 12, 2013, asked by Wyden in open session whether the NSA collected *"any type of data at all on millions, or hundreds of millions, of Americans,"* Director of National Intelligence James Clapper answered *"No, sir,"* then *"Not wittingly,"* an inaccurate answer; the transcript does not establish that he knew it was false.[^hf12] The executive claimed statutory authority, although the Second Circuit later held that Section 215 did not authorize this program. After June 2013 it released court opinions and orders in stages.[^hf13]

**Phase 4: The congressional response.** In 2008 Congress barred civil suits against providers that had assisted the President's program, on the Attorney General's certification.[^hf-nyt-2005] It extended Section 215 in 2010 and 2011 while four committees held the orders, which is consent under classification as much as denial of information. After the 2013 disclosures it legislated: the USA FREEDOM Act of 2015 ended bulk collection under Section 215 after a 180-day transition, passing a Republican-majority House 338–88 under a Democratic President, and left the Section 702 architecture intact.[^hf13] In the courts, the Second Circuit held the program statutorily unauthorized without reaching the constitutional issue; the D.C. Circuit vacated a district-court injunction on standing grounds; and in 2020 the Ninth Circuit held that the collection exceeded Section 215 and may have violated the Fourth Amendment, without suppressing evidence.[^hf-215-litigation]

**Phase 5: Individual accountability.** On April 8, 2014, Attorney General Holder acknowledged Representative Sensenbrenner's letter about Clapper's testimony and said DOJ was reviewing material supplied by members, without confirming an investigation. No charge against Clapper is established by the reviewed record, and the five-year limitation period for the testimony has run. A member request, a committee referral, an investigation, and a charge are distinct events; this exchange establishes only the first.[^hf12]

### Case 4 — The CIA Interrogation Program: Investigation under Counter-Pressure (2002–2014)

**Phase 1: The scandal.** Until September 6, 2006, when the President publicly acknowledged the program, the CIA restricted information about it to the Senate committee's chairman and vice chairman; the executive chose when access widened.[^hf14] The inquiry followed an external disclosure: in December 2007 the *New York Times* revealed that the CIA had destroyed videotapes of detainee interrogations, a destruction ordered in 2005 by Jose A. Rodriguez Jr., and the committee began its review on December 11, 2007, after the CIA Director briefed it that day.[^hf-cia-tapes]

**Phase 2: The congressional request.** The SSCI authorized its study on March 5, 2009. Staff reviewed more than six million pages of CIA documents before the committee approved the report in December 2012 by a 9–6 vote; the final report exceeded 6,700 pages, with approximately 38,000 footnotes.[^hf14] Its demand ran through negotiated declassification of the CIA's own document set rather than compelled release. Attorney General Holder's expansion of the Justice Department inquiry in August 2009 left CIA personnel unavailable for staff interviews.

**Phase 3: The executive offer.** The executive released a fixed fraction and resisted further demand. Only the Executive Summary (499 pages, released with the Findings and Conclusions as a 525-page document) was made public, on December 9, 2014, while the full report, more than 6,700 pages, stayed classified (selective disclosure, executive self-definition, delayed compliance). The White House withheld approximately 9,400 CIA documents from the committee pending a determination on executive privilege, and three written requests in 2013 went unanswered.[^hf14] Sen. Dianne Feinstein's March 11, 2014 floor statement described the counter-pressure: the removal in 2010 of documents already provided to committee staff; CIA searches of committee staff computers in January 2014; Director Brennan's January 15, 2014 emergency meeting; and a CIA criminal referral against committee staff. Feinstein named the stakes: "How this will be resolved will show whether the intelligence committee can be effective in monitoring and investigating our Nation's intelligence activities or whether our work can be thwarted by those we oversee."[^hf15]

**Phase 4: The congressional response.** The outcome was mixed. Within the period, statutes of 2005 and 2006 limited both cruel treatment and accountability for it; the first, and then *Hamdan v. Rumsfeld*, only paused the interrogation techniques.[^hf-dta-mca][^hf-dta-response] After the period, FY2016 NDAA Section 1045 codified anti-torture provisions, a real policy and institutional gain.[^hf16] The full report was not released: the committee sent only the summary for declassification, and in 2016 the D.C. Circuit held the full report a congressional record outside the Freedom of Information Act (FOIA).

**Phase 5: Individual accountability.** Accountability was declined at the outset. No senior official was prosecuted, and the Durham review closed with no charges. The Office of Professional Responsibility found professional misconduct by Yoo and Bybee; Associate Deputy Attorney General David Margolis declined to adopt those findings, treating the conduct as flawed judgment rather than misconduct, and the record reviewed shows no discipline. The computer-search dispute produced two referrals: the CIA Inspector General referred possible CIA personnel misconduct, and the acting general counsel filed a crimes report against committee staff.[^hf15][^hf16]

---

The four episodes illustrate constrained bargaining but do not establish it as an invariant property of U.S. constitutional architecture. Table 7 gives the exploratory coding, with the Watergate tapes dispute as a contrast column; the evidence, the codebook, and the separate Pike and Church profiles are in Appendix B.[^hf-accountability]

**Table 7. Exploratory per-indicator coding across the four historical cases and one contrast case, by phase[^tbl5a-pike] (indicators per Table 4; evidence and codebook in Appendix B; ✓ supported, ◑ mixed, ? insufficient evidence, ✗ contrary, n/a no congressional invocation documented)**

| Phase | Indicator | Pike and Church | Iran-Contra | NSA bulk collection | CIA Interrogation Program | Watergate tapes (contrast) |
|---|---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| 1 | Exogenous rupture *(bracket)* | ◑ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ◑ |
| 2 | Demand posture | ✓ | ✓ | ◑ | ✓ | ✓ |
| 3 | Selective disclosure | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| 3 | Justification proliferation | ◑ | ✓ | ◑ | ◑ | ◑ |
| 3 | Delayed compliance | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| 3 | Executive self-definition | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| 4 | Majority declines an available remedy | ✓ (House) / ◑ (Senate) | ✗ | ◑ | ◑ | ? |
| 4 | Remedy attempted and defeated outside Congress | n/a | n/a | ? | n/a | ✓ (the committee's own suit) |
| 4 | Informal negotiation | ✓ | ✓ | ◑ | ✓ | ◑ |
| 5 | Limited individual accountability *(bracket)* | ◑ | ◑ | ✓ | ✓ | ◑ |

**Historical outcome comparison (dimensions are not interchangeable)**

| Episode | Access | Policy change | Institutional durability | Individual sanctions |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Pike and Church | Extensive inquiry; official publication of Pike's report barred by the House; Church published substantial findings, twice over presidential objection | Reform followed the investigations | SSCI/HPSCI and FISA endured; causal attribution is shared with the broader reform coalition | Helms plea and fine survived; Felt and Miller pardoned |
| Iran-Contra | Extensive compelled, immunized testimony; contemporaneous notes withheld until 1991–92 and documents destroyed | 1991 Act: written, non-retroactive findings; covert action defined; "timely" notice kept with a recorded few-days commitment; statutory CIA inspector general (1989)[^hf-1991-act] | Investigation documented evasion; the statutory floor moved while the discretion claim survived | Fourteen charged, eleven convictions in Walsh's accounting; reversals and pardons had differing effects |
| NSA bulk collection | Member briefings constrained; public access expanded after leaks | USA FREEDOM ended Section 215 bulk collection | Statutory and judicial constraints were real though narrower than the whole surveillance system | Member requests concerning Clapper acknowledged; no charge established |
| CIA Interrogation Program | Six million pages reviewed; executive summary public; full study withheld; committee access widened only on the President's 2006 acknowledgment | Detainee Treatment Act of 2005, after which the interrogation techniques were only paused; sequel: FY2016 NDAA anti-torture restrictions | Study and statutory restrictions are durable outputs | Durham closed without charges; disciplinary findings reduced; a statutory defense for personnel (2005) and a retroactive redefinition of the war-crimes offense (2006) |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Nothing produced to the Senate committee, whose suit was dismissed; the tapes ordered produced on the Special Prosecutor's trial subpoena, under a Justice Department regulation that insulated him | Sequel: the Ethics in Government Act of 1978, with a court-appointed special prosecutor and the Senate's civil-enforcement statute (excluding subpoenas to federal officers or employees acting in an official capacity) | A holding that a generalized privilege claim yields to a demonstrated need for evidence in a criminal trial, which reserved congressional demands and national-security secrets[^tf-watergate] | President resigned and was pardoned; Haldeman, Ehrlichman, and Mitchell convicted and sentenced to imprisonment |

These outcomes qualify any failure thesis. Negotiation can produce effective oversight on access and policy without producing criminal sanctions, and the absence of prosecution does not cancel the other gains.

---

## Contemporary Congruence Assessment: EFTA

Corpus-derived figures in this section and Appendix A refer to the records measured as of August 30, 2026; event-specific dates below keep their narrower scope.

EFTA is a DOJ records-disclosure case. Two grounds bring it within this paper: executive custody of records and control of the initial release process, and a documented but unresolved question about an intelligence link. The record documents contacts with foreign officials and a credentialed analyst's assessment; it does not establish a service-directed operation. DOJ holds records bearing on the question; the reviewed record does not show whether decisive records exist or who holds them (Appendix D).[^efta-intel-link] The case therefore tries the bargaining vocabulary outside its original classified-policy setting, with the limits that extension entails.

EFTA imposes unusually express disclosure duties. Section 2(a) requires the Attorney General, "subject to subsection (b)," to publish within 30 days all unclassified records in the Department's possession on nine subjects, including internal communications about charging decisions (§ 2(a)(7)). Subsection (b), headed "Prohibited Grounds for Withholding," bars withholding, delay, or redaction "on the basis of embarrassment, reputational harm, or political sensitivity, including to any government official, public figure, or foreign dignitary" (§ 2(b)(1)). Section 2(c), headed "Permitted Withholdings," is permissive and limited: the Attorney General "may withhold or redact the segregable portions of records that" fall in five classes (§ 2(c)(1); Table 8). Section 2(c)(2) states that all redactions must be accompanied by a written justification published in the Federal Register and submitted to Congress; it sets no deadline and prescribes no Bates-level entries or segregability findings. Section 2(c)(4) requires publication of classification decisions after July 1, 2025. The itemized-accounting construction below is the author's analysis, not statutory language or a holding.

**Table 8. Permitted Withholdings Under EFTA Section 2(c)(1)**

| Section 2(c)(1) class | The Attorney General "may withhold or redact the segregable portions of records that"\* |
|---|---|
| (A) | personally identifiable information of victims, or victims' personal, medical, and similar files whose disclosure would be a clearly unwarranted invasion of personal privacy |
| (B) | records that depict or contain child sexual abuse materials (CSAM) as defined under 18 U.S.C. § 2256 and prohibited under 18 U.S.C. §§ 2252–2252A |
| (C) | material whose release would jeopardize an active federal investigation or ongoing prosecution, with withholding narrowly tailored and temporary |
| (D) | images depicting or containing the death, physical abuse, or injury of any person |
| (E) | information specifically authorized under criteria established by an Executive order to be kept secret in the interest of national defense or foreign policy and in fact properly classified pursuant to that order |

\* The word "that" can attach to "records" or to "portions." This table adopts the narrower reading, under which only the portions containing the listed matter may be withheld; the reviewed record does not show the Department claiming the broader one.

Section 3 requires a report to the House and Senate Judiciary Committees within 15 days of "completion of the release," listing the categories of records released and withheld, a summary of redactions with legal basis, and the government officials and "politically exposed persons" named in the released materials. The Act defines neither "completion" nor "politically exposed persons," and it has no enforcement or definitions section (Appendix C, Table C.1). It passed the House 427–1 and the Senate by unanimous consent.[^tt1]

### The Scandal

The Epstein matter reached EFTA through an external disclosure, as in the historical cases: Julie K. Brown's *Miami Herald* series "Perversion of Justice," whose first installment ran November 28, 2018, re-exposed the 2007–2008 federal non-prosecution agreement and its concealment from the victims. The Department's professional-responsibility office found that it "led to public outrage and media scrutiny"; the New York indictment followed in July 2019.[^tt-julie-brown] The House floor debate on EFTA (171 Cong. Rec. H4725–H4733, daily ed. Nov. 18, 2025) credited survivors' advocacy and did not mention the reporting.

### The Congressional Request

The request built in two waves. First, public pressure to release the Epstein files mounted through 2025, and the executive met it with the graduated offers traced below. Second, the demand became statutory, and through Congress's own machinery: the bill reached the floor after Representative Massie gathered 218 signatures on a discharge petition, which forces a vote the leadership has not scheduled, and EFTA followed on November 19, 2025.[^tt-discharge] That is escalation inside the channel.

EFTA reaches unclassified records "in the possession of the Department of Justice," including the FBI and U.S. Attorneys' Offices, whoever originated them. Records held only by the CIA or NSA fall outside it; Epstein's own earlier requests to those agencies drew "neither-confirm-nor-deny" (Glomar) responses in 2011 and 2014.[^tt-glomar-cycles]

### The Executive Offer

The offer was graduated. On February 27, 2025 the Department released a "first phase" of declassified files, binders labeled "The Epstein Files: Phase 1" handed to about fifteen online influencers at the White House; the contents were largely already public. The Attorney General framed the release as one that *"begins to provide"* accountability and, the same day, wrote the FBI Director demanding the *"full and complete"* files with *"no withholdings or limitations"*: a low first offer presented as an installment.[^tt-binder-etp] The FBI's internal Epstein Transparency Project (ETP) redaction pipeline followed in March 2025. Then an undated, unsigned Department and FBI memorandum made public in July 2025 tried to close at a low offer, concluding there was no incriminating client list and that *"no further disclosure would be appropriate or warranted"*; backlash reopened the question. The compelled offer examined here is the latest concession, not an endstate.

DOJ says it identified more than six million pages as potentially responsive and released over three million, nearly 3.5 million including earlier productions. The release measured here, the twelve data sets as counted through Bates EFTA02731789, contains 1,378,393 documents and, by known page counts, 2,729,256 pages; DOJ has also published Bates-EFTA records above that number, including reports named in the June 25 *Phang* order that sit in its Data Set 12 folder, and those are outside the counts.[^tt2][^tt-released-count] Different inclusions, duplicates, and nonresponsive overcollection prevent subtraction from identifying a responsive withheld population. The December 19, 2025 deadline was missed; production continued through January 30, 2026, and the Section 3 Report followed February 14. The January 30 letter describes approximately 200,000 pages redacted or withheld based on privileges, not a disjoint withheld-only population, and the report supplies no count reconciliation. These are accounting limits, not a measured percentage of unlawful withholding.

Six aspects of implementation organize the following assessment: processing instructions, inherited markings, privilege claims, compliance reporting, completion claims, and official responsibility. Each rests on a statutory opening, an undefined term or a trigger the obligor controls (Table C.1). The label "self-defined compliance" describes agency choices under a disputed mandate; it does not pre-adjudicate their legality or infer a concealment motive.

#### Self-defined Compliance #1 — Earlier processing instructions

Full-corpus classification recovered nine copies of two review-guidance documents, including an FBI training deck dated March 21, 2025, eight months before enactment (EFTA00172446, pp. 1, 6).[^tt-guidance-docs] Its instruction reads, "Do not add any exemptions; just redaction boxes", without a terminal period in the source.[^tt5] This establishes what that training told processors to place on pages then. It does not establish that reasons were never recorded, that the instruction governed the post-EFTA review, or that a justification log could not be reconstructed.

Continuity is an inference requiring a documentary bridge. DOJ's January 30 letter describes post-enactment review protocols, updated guidance, and FAQs, which is affirmative rival evidence.[^efta-letter2] One such document is in the court record: the September 16 *Phang* order describes DOJ's "EFTA First Level Review Protocol," cited there as dated January 4, 2026 although attached to the December 19 letter, which required redaction of personal email addresses under the Privacy Act and of government personnel names.[^phang-orders] The March 2025 training gave the same rule for personnel names, so the protocol cuts both ways: it repeats one rule and gives another a statutory basis. The protocol is not among the nine guidance copies, and record-level review histories would still be needed to date any decision.

The training's other instructions identify earlier processing categories, not automatically unauthorized post-EFTA redactions. One contested episode is on the record: Wexner's name was redacted in an FBI document and, by the Attorney General's hearing testimony, restored within 40 minutes after Representative Massie raised it.[^tt-wexner] Section 2(b)(1) is the provision that governs a public figure's name; the Section 3 Report states that no record was redacted on those grounds, and whether the provision was breached turns on the reason for the redaction, which no published record states. Bondi's May 29 interview (pp. 36, 74, 86–87) acknowledges victim-protection failures.[^tt5][^tt-bondi-format] Victim exposure and over-withholding require separate investigation.

#### Self-defined Compliance #2 — Inherited markings

The production carries four inherited record or redaction groupings: two proceedings and two FBI record or marking categories.[^tt-four-cases] DOJ states that third-party material retained its original redactions, while also stating that documents were loaded for review; retention does not establish that no review occurred.[^efta-letter2] As of August 30, 2026, eight phrase queries return 2,670 documents (Set A), of which the classifier assigns 569 to inherited non-EFTA framework markers and 314 to common-law-privilege language; across the whole corpus the same classes hold 571 and 444,298 documents, the latter almost all confidentiality-disclaimer boilerplate.[^tt-setA] Because most of the eight queries are the marker phrases themselves, the markers' share of the query results is not a prevalence and is not reported. The Report never explains how the inherited markings fit Section 2(c); see Appendix A.

#### Self-defined Compliance #3 — Disputed privilege construction

The Section 3 Report invokes deliberative-process, work-product, and attorney-client privileges, none enumerated in § 2(c). It rests on Federal Rule of Evidence 501 and a clear-statement presumption for common-law privileges, citing *Trammel*, *Bassett*, *Castro-Huerta*, and above all *FBI v. Fazaga*, for the proposition that a privilege "should not be held to have been abrogated or limited unless Congress has at least used clear statutory language."[^tt3][^efta-section3] The strongest DOJ reading is that Congress legislates against established privileges, that § 2(a) nowhere mentions privilege, and that enumerating disclosure exceptions does not displace them.

Privilege is one of several bases DOJ applied that § 2(c)(1) does not list: the Privacy Act, redaction of government personnel names and email addresses, names redacted for "law-enforcement sensitive purposes," statutes that its crosswalk groups as "other applicable law," and non-production of "substantially similar" records and of foreign-language and technically incompatible files (Appendix C, Table C.2).[^efta-crosswalk] The *Phang* court rejected two of these rationales (duplication and foreign-language impracticability), discharged its show-cause order for the personnel and email redactions, and reserved whether the Act permits Privacy Act redactions.

**Author's legal analysis.** The enacted text supports reading § 2(c)(1) as exclusive. The Act pairs "Prohibited Grounds for Withholding" with "Permitted Withholdings"; § 2(a)(7) makes internal Department communications about charging decisions covered records, which a full preservation of deliberative-process and work-product privilege would leave with little to reach; and § 2(c)(1) confines even permitted withholding to "segregable portions." In the December 9 *Maxwell* order a court applied structural reasoning of this kind at DOJ's request: it relied on the enumerated exceptions to hold that the Act covers grand-jury materials, and on the later-in-time and specific-over-general canons, with § 2(b)(1), to hold Rule 6(e) displaced for them. It did not address privileges.[^efta-interactions] DOJ's principal authority, *Fazaga*, stated its rule for the state-secrets privilege and called its own holding narrow; whether that rule reaches ordinary privileges under a disclosure mandate is undecided, and on DOJ's reading paragraph (7) still reaches communications that are unprivileged or segregable. The construction is contested, not adjudicated, and the release record does not reveal what privilege-withheld pages contain. The inference that privilege substituted for intelligence classification remains withdrawn.

Other legal regimes require separate treatment. FOIA exemptions govern FOIA and do not automatically migrate into EFTA. The Privacy Act bars disclosure without consent unless an exception applies, and its exception for disclosures "required under section 552" covers FOIA, not EFTA. 18 U.S.C. § 3509(d) separately protects child victims' identities. Housekeeping rules regulate handling, but 5 U.S.C. § 301 supplies no withholding authority. In *United States v. Maxwell*, the December 9, 2025 order held Rule 6(e) displaced for the covered grand-jury materials and modified the protective order with victim safeguards; the January 21, 2026 order in that case is discussed with the member-level routes below.[^efta-interactions]

Classification is a separate statutory ground: § 2(c)(1)(E), read with the declassification duty in § 2(c)(3) and the duty in § 2(c)(4) to publish each decision after July 1, 2025 to classify covered information. The Classified Information Procedures Act (CIPA) supplies criminal-case procedures, not a general EFTA exemption. The pending EFTA II bills would strike (E). That alone would not open classified records, because § 2(a) still reaches only "unclassified" records; § 2(c)(3) would lose the withholding it applies to, while § 2(c)(4) would survive as the principal published check on classifying a record out of § 2(a). The bills' separate State-officer access to classified records is in Table C.4. A reviewer must apply the governing protection, not presume every inherited label valid or void.

#### Self-defined Compliance #4 — Discretionary Compliance Reporting

DOJ initially supplied no Federal Register notice and republished its Section 3 Report there on August 27, 2026, with categorical explanations, not entries keyed to records or pages.[^tt-fr-aug2026] Under the author's construction, "all redactions" requires enough linkage to permit review of each disputed redaction. DOJ's December 19 letter said that "A privilege log will be produced in due course as required under the Act." Section 3(2) separately requires a "summary of redactions made, including legal basis," so a categorical summary under § 2(c)(2) would duplicate it. And the June 25 *Phang* opinion described the provision as requiring a "redaction log" due with the production, though on merits DOJ did not contest at that stage.[^efta-log] DOJ's strongest rival is that categorical justifications accompany every member of each class, that the provision sets no deadline, and that record-level entries are impracticable for roughly 1.8 million page-slots with an apparent redaction box (uncorrected). The September 16 order discharged the publication show-cause requirement and reserved substantive adequacy.

#### Self-defined Compliance #5 — Self-declared Completion

DOJ's January 30 letter declared the identification and review process complete while describing remaining reporting obligations.[^efta-letter2] Section 3 makes that declaration the trigger for the report, which followed February 14. A completion announcement is not a judicial determination of compliance. A proposed amendment should resolve the uncertainty by requiring the Attorney General to post, with each release and by a stated date, a machine-readable index giving for each redaction or withheld record a public identifier, the governing ground, a harm explanation where relevant, the segregability decision, the review date, and an expiry for temporary withholding, with notice of its availability in the Federal Register and a date-certain report. Protected details should go to a sealed annex accessible to the reviewing court; the public log must not identify victims or reveal that an identified record is tied to an open investigation whose subject is unaware of it.

#### Self-defined Compliance #6 — Official responsibility

The compliance letters and Section 3 Report bear Blanche's signature; Bondi's name appears on Letter #2 and the report without her signature. Signature allocation documents responsibility for those statements, not a design to evade it. Bondi's April removal and May 29 unsworn transcribed interview are political consequences distinct from a criminal sanction; whether any official incurred a durable legal penalty remains open.[^bondi-firing][^bondi-acting-ag][^tt-bondi-format]

Epstein's state guilty plea and Maxwell's federal conviction are real accountability for private crimes; they do not establish accountability for official oversight failures. The 2007–2008 non-prosecution agreement and its concealment from victims remain central, but the district court's Crime Victims' Rights Act finding was followed by *In re Wild*, 994 F.3d 1244 (11th Cir. 2021) (en banc), which held that the Act authorized no freestanding enforcement action before federal proceedings began.[^tt-epstein-npa][^wild-cvra] Maxwell's later prison transfer raises a separate question from the force of her conviction.[^tt-maxwell-asymmetry]

#### Release, Redaction, and Justification Findings

The three analyses answer different questions and do not yield a single compliance percentage (Tables 9 and 10).

**Table 9. What Each Measurement Establishes**

| Measurement | Supported interpretation | Limit |
|---|---|---|
| Release inventory | Pages represented in the measured release | Cannot derive a withheld responsive residual from DOJ's gross collection |
| Dark-box detector | Apparent flags and calibrated dark-box prevalence | Does not establish legal compliance or total redaction prevalence |
| Text classification | Assigned phrase and framework classes | Does not decide each redaction's operative legal basis |

**Table 10. Separate Measurements, as of August 30, 2026**

| Component | Result and denominator |
|---|---|
| Measured release (Data Sets 1–12, through EFTA02731789) | 2,729,256 pages (sum of known counts); not a share of a reconciled responsive universe |
| DOJ collection | More than 6 million potentially responsive pages, including duplication and overcollection |
| January 30 letter | Approximately 200,000 pages redacted or withheld based on privileges; no disjoint withheld-only share established |
| Text-marker join | 5,438 of 2,729,178 page-slots (0.199%); 3,186 visual-plus-marker and 2,252 marker-only |
| Dark-box census | 2,729,175 pages processed; 81 pages in 33 corrupt files excluded, plus a three-page duplicate removed |
| Corrected dark-box prevalence | 64.5%, conditional 95% calibration interval 62.0–66.6%; tuning and transport uncertainty excluded |
| Apparent image-only join | 1,842,147 of 2,729,178 page-slots (67.498%); uncorrected |
| Overall compliance | Not estimable from these noncomparable populations |

At the document level, the largest classifier assignment in the 2,670-document query set is a 1,576-document fallback class, which is not a verified population of unjustified redactions (Appendix A, Table 12).

The 64.5% dark-box estimate reuses the 400-page set used to tune the detector, its main weakness, so its 62.0–66.6% interval is conditional on that design; transport limits and unresolved white-box and media masking are set out in Appendix A, and neither this estimate nor the text counts supplies an overall compliance percentage.

#### The grand-jury motions

The July 2025 grand-jury unsealing motions were denied under the law then applied; after enactment, the December 9 *Maxwell* order changed access to the covered materials.[^tt-gj-unsealing-gambit][^efta-interactions] The sequence shows why contemporaneous legal constraints and later statutory changes must both be considered.

### The Congressional and Judicial Response

Congress escalated unevenly, while private litigation independently produced compulsory orders (*Phang v. Blanche*, discussed below). The two channels must be tracked separately: a committee majority's refusal to pursue a suit shows political non-use, not that the suit would fail.

#### Response to the withheld documents

On January 21, 2026, Rep. Summer Lee offered a substitute for the pending Clinton contempt report. It would have replaced that report with a recommendation that the House direct the Committee to seek declaratory judgments enforcing its August 5, 2025 subpoena to Bondi, with House counsel appearing on the Speaker's authorization. It failed 19–24 on a party-line vote: every aye a Democrat, every no a Republican. The Committee also rejected, 19–25, an amendment substituting civil enforcement for criminal contempt against Clinton, and then reported the Clinton criminal-contempt resolution 34–8.[^tt-committee-civil-motion] Because the Lee text displaced the Clinton report, the vote is evidence of a committee choice against this remedy but not a clean test of it; it is compatible with partisan protection, and adoption would still have required action by the House. Public pressure preceded additional interview-summary releases in March, while related notes remained disputed; under Table 6 such concessions count as gains.[^tt-53-pages][^tt-chain-reaction-withholding]

#### Response to witness avoidance

The witness record mixes subpoenas, negotiated appearances, depositions, and unsworn interviews; it should not be summarized as a uniform denial. Maxwell appeared virtually on February 9 and invoked the Fifth Amendment; Bondi's May 29 appearance was an unsworn transcribed interview; Gates's interview occurred June 10.[^tt-maxwell-asymmetry][^tt-bondi-format][^tt-subpoena-roster] A threatened contempt vote that secures testimony is a concrete gain, even if document access remains disputed.


#### Response to the Federal Register justification requirement

The private Administrative Procedure Act (APA) action *Phang v. Blanche* is substantive counterevidence to a categorical enforcement-monopoly account. Judge Emmet G. Sullivan's June 25 preliminary injunction (ECF 15, with opinion ECF 16) required production or a showing of cause for specified emails, documents, and interview notes; it separately required initiation of foreign-language review and publication or a showing of cause for the redaction log.[^phang-orders] It did not order immediate public release of every withheld record. The September orders show both the reach and limits of this route:

| Date / order | Compulsion and response | Evidentiary limit |
|---|---|---|
| June 25, ECF 15–16 | Preliminary APA relief, with July 2 obligations | Likelihood of success found on merits DOJ did not contest at the preliminary-injunction stage (Local Civil Rule 7(b)), so no exemption claim was adjudicated; the APA right of action, standing, and FOIA-adequacy rulings are contested and on appeal |
| July 25 / July 30; August 13 (recounted in ECF 43, pp. 6–8) | Court ordered in-camera submissions; DOJ submitted materials; further victim evidence requested | Judicial access and review occurred; not equivalent to full public production |
| September 16, ECF 42 | Answer compelled by September 24; stay of filing deadlines denied | Procedural enforcement, not sanctions for the underlying disclosure dispute |
