| September 16, ECF 43, pp. 9–16, 19–20 | Show cause discharged for victim names in five records, for private email addresses redacted under the Privacy Act, and for government-personnel names, the last two under DOJ's review protocol; notes and specified names required in camera; foreign-language review renewed for September 24, a date temporarily stayed on September 21 (ECF 49), when the court also denied DOJ's stay motion | Whether the Act permits Privacy Act redactions expressly reserved; the duplication and foreign-language rationales for non-production rejected; civil contempt declined as premature; appeal No. 26-5299 pending, and a second appeal, No. 26-5334, docketed September 21; new deadline not yet tested here |
| September 16, ECF 43, pp. 17–18 | Federal Register publication show-cause issue discharged | Adequacy of the notice's content expressly left for later proceedings |
| October 1, ECF 52 | DOJ moved for judgment on the pleadings, contesting among other things the right of action, standing, redactions, and foreign-language production | A motion, not a ruling; none seen. DOJ's September 24 answer (ECF 50, p. 11) had already pleaded no final agency action. The treatment of the merits as conceded concerns the preliminary injunction |
| October 9, ECF 57; October 16, ECF 59 | Show cause discharged for names in three records and for the notes behind one report; redacted notes behind three reports ordered in camera and submitted | In camera only. DOJ says the redacted copies "did not exist" earlier and asks for a stay if publication is ordered |
| October 28, D.C. Circuit Nos. 26-5299, 26-5334 | Stay pending appeal denied; motion to dismiss referred to the merits panel | The court noted that "production does not appear to be imminent"; no ruling on the merits of the appeal |

The September and October entries postdate the August 30, 2026 corpus cutoff. Together the orders show one district court exercising APA review and in camera inspection without DOJ choosing to prosecute; standing, the APA right of action, and the adequacy of FOIA as an alternative are before the D.C. Circuit. They do not establish comprehensive public release, a final privilege holding, or a collected penalty. The Inspector General's audit is another review channel, subject to the Attorney General's authority under 5 U.S.C. § 413(a) to prohibit an audit that requires sensitive investigative information, with notice to Congress.[^phang-oig-sequence]

#### Member-level routes

Three member-level routes are on the record. Representative Thomas Massie, an EFTA co-author, named three men DOJ should investigate on the House floor on February 24, 2026. The Speech or Debate Clause protects the floor statement, though not its republication outside Congress; the speech neither compels the withheld records nor alters the redaction process.[^tt-massie] Second, the January 30 letter invites any Member to view unredacted material by arrangement through Judiciary Committee leadership under confidentiality undertakings, and the Section 3 Report says some have done so. DOJ then told the *Phang* court that it "should not supplant Congress's acceptance" of its explanation for not reviewing foreign-language documents; the court found no authority that congressional silence amends the Act.[^tt-member-access] Third, when Massie and Representative Khanna, the Act's co-sponsors, moved to participate as amici in *Maxwell* and obtain a special master, the court held on January 21, 2026 that the criminal case gave it no authority to supervise compliance, without prejudice to a separate suit, and noted without deciding DOJ's argument that the members would lack standing.[^efta-interactions]

### The cumulative reading

The evidence supports a narrower cumulative reading: agency processing choices and contested interpretations shape disclosure, while political and judicial checks sometimes change that process. The dated training, inherited markers, and absence of an itemized public log do not jointly prove post-enactment continuity or unlawful privilege use. The following source-to-inference audit replaces decisive-test labels.[^tt-pt-table]

| Observation | What the source establishes | Rival or missing link |
|---|---|---|
| March 21 training | Earlier box-only page instruction | Updated post-enactment protocols; no demonstrated continuity |
| Inherited text markers | Presence of framework language in released records | Legacy markings, quotations, and incidental hits may not be operative reasons |
| Section 3 privilege account | DOJ's common-law-preservation construction | Author's contrary construction awaits adjudication |
| August 27 notice / September 16 order | Publication occurred; itemized adequacy remains contested | Categorical compliance reading remains live |
| Congressional and judicial action | Rep. Lee's substitute recommending a House-directed civil suit defeated on a party-line vote, 19–24; APA orders issued and in camera review undertaken | Political unwillingness and legal enforcement coexist; the substitute displaced another report, so the vote is not a clean test |
| Non-escalation by committee or chamber majorities | Recorded votes against an available remedy | Agreement or deference among pivotal members is a rival to executive information control as the nearer constraint |

This is congruence with parts of the bargaining account, not a discriminating confirmation against partisanship, administrative burden, privacy protection, or judicial self-correction.

**Table 11. What the bargaining vocabulary and two rival accounts would each expect, by episode (descriptive; not independent predictions)**

| Case | Observed result | Bargaining vocabulary | Partisan account | Fire-alarm or self-correction account | Do the accounts differ? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Pike and Church | Both investigated; the House barred official publication of Pike's report on a cross-party vote; Church published over presidential objection. Reform followed; SSCI/HPSCI and FISA endured. Helms's plea and fine survived; Felt and Miller were pardoned. | Conditional access and executive arbitration of release; a committee that publishes over executive objection is overridden where its chamber sides with the executive and sustained where it does not. | An opposition Congress escalates against the President; it does not expect a cross-party House majority to bind its own committee. | Outside alarm, investigation, publication, reform. | Partly. The House vote fits neither a simple partisan account nor executive control alone; publication and reform fit self-correction. |
| Iran-Contra | Extensive compelled, immunized testimony; records withheld or destroyed. The 1991 Act moved the statutory floor while the discretion claim survived. Fourteen charged and eleven convicted; reversals and pardons had differing effects. | Withheld records, negotiated concessions, and a settlement that leaves discretion claims standing. | An opposition Congress investigates and legislates. | Exposure, prosecution by an independent counsel, statute. | No discriminating observation on access and policy. On sanctions, reversals and pardons fit the bargaining description and surviving convictions fit self-correction. |
| NSA bulk collection | Member access constrained; objection muted under opposition and same-party Senates alike; provider immunity enacted by an opposition Congress (2008); public access expanded after leaks. USA FREEDOM ended Section 215 bulk collection. Member requests concerning Clapper acknowledged; no charge established. | Muted objection under classification; reform only after outside disclosure. | Escalation by the opposition Senates of 2001–02 and 2007–08 and little by the same-party Senates; no immunity from an opposition Congress for providers that assisted the President's program; a statute from an opposition Congress in 2015. | Leak, litigation, statute. | Partly. Muted objection under the opposition Senates and the 2008 immunity from an opposition Congress do not fit the partisan account; the bargaining description fits both as consent under classification, and the fire-alarm account fits the muted objection. |
| CIA Interrogation Program | More than six million pages reviewed and the executive summary public; full study withheld. FY2016 NDAA anti-torture restrictions. Durham closed without charges; disciplinary findings reduced. | Counter-pressure and restricted publication alongside a statute. | An opposition Senate opens the review (2007) and a later majority investigates a program begun under the other party; persistence against its own party's administration is not expected. | Study, summary, statute. | Mixed: initiation fits the partisan account and persistence against same-party resistance does not; no on outcomes. |
| EFTA | Missed deadline and contested privilege and reporting claims; post-enactment workflow continuity unproved; preliminary APA compulsion. | Delay and disputed definitions of compliance under executive custody. | A same-party committee declines to enforce; the one recorded vote was party-line. | A private suit compels through the courts. | No discriminating observation yet. A change of party control or the appellate rulings would supply one. |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Nothing produced to the Senate committee, whose suit was dismissed; tapes ordered produced on the Special Prosecutor's trial subpoena; impeachment inquiry; resignation and pardon; senior aides convicted. | Scored on the committee's demand, the early indicators are present and the committee's own remedy failed. | An opposition Congress escalates. | Prosecutor, court, and impeachment inquiry produce the records and sanctions. | On the congressional channel, no: all three fit. On outcome, yes: the records came through a regulation-insulated prosecutor and the courts, which fits the self-correction account better than the bargaining description. |

### Rival hypothesis: partisan alignment

Partisan incentives and institutional constraints are compatible explanations. Research on congressional investigation finds that party control affects investigative activity, most robustly in the House, and Levinson and Pildes state the rival in general form: the distinction between party-divided and party-unified government rivals, and often dominates, the distinction between the branches.[^tt-rival-partisan] EFTA arose under unified government, and the party-line defeat of the Lee substitute is the most direct evidence here for a willingness-based explanation, qualified by the fact that the substitute displaced another report. A unified-government implementation failure cannot by itself separate partisanship from the structural description.

Coded by investigating chamber and sub-period (Table 2), the episodes do not separate the accounts. Opposition chambers (Pike and Church; Iran-Contra, through 1991; the Watergate contrast) produced testimony, publication, suits, and statutory reform, though the House also bound its own committee in 1976. The CIA review began in an opposition Senate and targeted a program begun under the other party, as a partisan account expects; its persistence against a same-party administration's resistance does not fit that account. In the NSA case objection stayed muted under opposition and same-party Senates alike, an opposition Congress enacted the 2008 provider immunity, and an opposition Congress enacted the 2015 statute. In EFTA the same-party committee declined the civil remedy. A partisan account would expect most of this ordering, and these selected cases cannot test a probabilistic theory.[^tt-rival-intel] Non-use of a channel must still be distinguished from its unavailability: the civil route is open to the House in the district court on present authority, unsettled on appeal, and for the Senate outside its civil-enforcement statute and untested on any other basis (Appendix C, Table C.3).

If party control changes after the November election, subsequent escalation and production will supply further observations; no result is predicted here. Continued withholding would need its legal and factual grounds examined before being attributed to an institutional pattern.

*Phang* shows that one private plaintiff obtained preliminary APA compulsion in this setting, on an informational-injury theory and with the merits treated as conceded at that stage. Its public-production result, appellate treatment, and remedial durability remain unresolved. The paper therefore presents EFTA as a live congruence assessment with material counterevidence, not a completed causal test or a forecast that only external rupture can succeed.


---

## Implications

The deepest consequence is constitutional, and it is not that negotiation replaces checking. The Supreme Court reads Madison's design as an "ongoing relationship" of "rivalry and reciprocity" and records that disputes over congressional demands for presidential documents have historically been settled in the political process, not in court; the D.C. Circuit speaks of an "implicit constitutional mandate to seek optimal accommodation."[^imp1] The claim here is narrower. Accommodation presupposes that each branch can verify what the other offers and can escalate at a cost the other must weigh. In the classified domain the executive also restricts who in Congress may see the information, so verification is impaired, not merely costly: within each episode the evidentiary baseline is its own production, and the cases show checking that works through negotiation, with real gains and real limits.

The constraint has a second face. McCubbins and Schwartz pair fire-alarm oversight (decentralized monitoring triggered by outside complainants) with police-patrol oversight (Congress's own direct examination of agency activity). The argument so far has described the conditions under which alarms are scarce and late in the classified domain: the access and standing rules that let outsiders see agency conduct and complain about it are missing or barred, so alarms arrive from outside those rules, and Congress then bargains over a record the executive still holds. A distinct problem arises one step later, at enforcement: where the prosecutor who would act answers to the institution whose conduct is at issue, agencies holding jurisdiction and evidence may decline to act. That is not a police-patrol failure; it is the paper's separate enforcement-monopoly hypothesis.[^imp-enforcement]

When detection does occur, durable individual accountability meets several distinct obstacles, and the cases do not show one cause for all of them.[^hf7][^hf8][^hf12][^hf16][^wyden-bofa-aml] (a) Charging discretion: the Durham closure and the absence of a charge after member requests concerning Clapper's testimony are decisions by a prosecutor inside the executive. The Department stated that the admissible evidence was insufficient to obtain and sustain convictions in the two closed investigations; the record does not establish that institutional dependence caused that assessment. (b) Immunity: North and Poindexter were prosecuted by Independent Counsel and convicted; the convictions were set aside by judicial application of the Fifth Amendment to congressionally immunized testimony, not by prosecutorial refusal. (c) Clemency: the December 1992 pardons were a presidential act. (d) Professional discipline: the Office of Professional Responsibility's findings against Yoo and Bybee were not adopted by Associate Deputy Attorney General Margolis. (e) Classification control over trial evidence: the central conspiracy and theft charges against North were dismissed when the Reagan Administration refused to declassify information, and the Fernandez prosecution was dismissed when the Attorney General barred disclosure of classified evidence; Walsh concluded that this discretion lets an attorney general block almost any potentially embarrassing prosecution that requires declassification.[^hf-cipa] Surviving Iran-Contra convictions and pleas, and the civil compulsion in *Phang*, show that the obstacles are not absolute. The enforcement-monopoly hypothesis concerns (a) only. Its natural test compares periods with and without an available independent counsel: the Iran-Contra pleas for withholding information from Congress came under that statute, and the Helms plea came from an ordinary Department prosecution. Watergate is the earlier instance: a regulation-insulated prosecutor obtained what the Senate committee could not and charged senior aides, who were convicted.[^tf-watergate-charges] It remains a hypothesis to be tested against charging records, not a finding.

A possible reinforcing mechanism is doctrinal continuity: notification disputes generated opinions, directives, and minority views that later officials could invoke. The evidence supports continuity of an argument, not a citation chain or causation: the three audited Yoo documents cite none of the Iran-Contra notification materials, and Cheney himself pointed back to the minority report on December 20, 2005.[^hf9][^hf9-olc-audit]

A further, untested hypothesis runs outward to public trust. It has separate links, each with its own observable test: that the public notices non-punishment of documented misconduct; that noticing it lowers confidence in the checking institutions specifically; and that lower confidence later increases tolerance of norm-breaking.[^imp3] Public trust in government fell from 77% in 1964 to 17% in 2025, but the series is not monotone and combines surveys and modes; incumbency, economic performance, and issue salience are established rival influences, and this paper does not identify oversight's share of any change.[^imp2][^imp-trust-asymmetry] No causal claim about trust or backsliding is made.

A related hypothesis concerns public attention: a large partial release may satisfy some demands while leaving other disputes unresolved. This paper has not measured that mechanism.[^tt-clark-extension-public-inoculation] Substantive constraints can matter even when distrust persists, and reforms should be judged against their actual target: USA FREEDOM ended Section 215 bulk collection while separate Section 702 authorities persisted.

Where federal responses leave demands unresolved, the demand can reappear elsewhere. In February 2026 the New Mexico House of Representatives created a special committee, the Survivors' Truth Commission, by House Resolution 1, adopted 62 to 0, to investigate Epstein, his co-conspirators, and his facilitators in New Mexico. A state commission is not a substitute for federal oversight of federal agencies, and its interim report offers no final findings.[^nm-truth-interim]

---

## Recommendations

**Conditional leverage reforms.** Proposals 1 to 6 address the unclassified disclosure setting of one statute, Proposal 7 congressional subpoenas generally; the historical cases, where secrecy and standing doctrines blocked private suits, give no assurance that the first six would transfer. Proposals 1 to 3 build, for that statute, the access and standing rules whose absence makes alarms scarce. Records remain in agency custody, DOJ ordinarily conducts federal litigation (28 U.S.C. § 516), and courts must have jurisdiction. The APA route used in *Phang*, still contested on appeal, is the baseline. DOJ contests whether the Act carries a private right, whether FOIA forecloses APA review, and whether a journalist has standing; review proceeds on an administrative record, and in camera inspection rests on the court's inherent authority. An express cause of action would settle the first two questions and could prescribe de novo review, the agency's burden, in camera inspection, and fees for a plaintiff whose suit prompts release.[^remedy-apa-gap]

**Constitutional and remedial feasibility.** Each proposal regulates executive information control by statute. Where a refusal rests on a claimed exclusive power, Justice Jackson's third *Youngstown* category requires that power to be both exclusive and conclusive; a claim of executive privilege is instead balanced against Congress's need, as *Nixon v. Administrator of General Services* and *Mazars* describe, and that balance once defeated a Senate committee.[^remedy-youngstown] *Trump v. United States* identifies one exclusive power, direction of the Department's investigative and prosecutorial decisions; that limits any proposal to relocate charging authority and does not decide noncriminal disclosure duties. Standing differs by plaintiff. *TransUnion* bars suits by unharmed plaintiffs but distinguishes statutes that entitle the public to information, the theory accepted in *Phang*; a requester must still show adverse effects from the denial, a State must assert its own right, and a Member faces *Raines v. Byrd*. Relief differs by source: *Trump v. CASA* holds that universal injunctions likely exceed the equitable authority Congress has granted and reserves APA vacatur, and a new statute can specify its remedy within Article III limits. Any constitutional privilege, state-secrets claim, or sovereign-immunity bar must still be considered.[^remedy-design]

1. **Deadline-triggered access review.** A proposed amendment should impose the accounting duty described under Self-declared Completion directly, with a date, and give a person denied information it requires to be published an express cause of action in district court to compel it. A person who then identifies a logged record, and a victim whose own records are withheld or whose protected information is threatened with release, could challenge the entry. The court would review de novo, with the burden on the agency, and could inspect records in camera. Missing the accounting date would entitle a plaintiff to a schedule order, not automatic release or liability; a fixed date to lodge the accounting, not the word "expedited," gives the timetable its force. Refusal of an order would be enforced by civil contempt against a named officer, which the *Phang* court has so far called premature. The amendment should state whether it applies to the production already made and to pending actions, and should specify fees.

2. **Protected judicial review of disputed exclusions.** Identified disputes and the unredacted record would go to the district court under secure procedures, with victim notice where feasible and an opportunity to object before access. A magistrate judge could assist only through a valid 28 U.S.C. § 636 referral, and a special master could be appointed under § 636(b)(2) or Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 53, with the statute saying who pays; neither is a freestanding disclosure tribunal.[^magistrate-review] The court would set a production schedule and review temporary grounds at intervals. A missing log would permit an order compelling the log, not forfeiture of victim privacy. The added value is systematic record-level review and a required segregability finding for each withholding: the enacted text confines withholding to "segregable portions" but requires no finding, explanation, or reviewer. Treatment of constitutional privileges, including the state-secrets privilege that *Fazaga* held a statutory in camera procedure did not displace, remains unresolved.

3. **Collectible civil remedies for defined injuries.** Congress could authorize compensatory or bounded statutory damages for an identified victim or requester with concrete injury from a specified violation, after notice and a reasonable opportunity to cure. A claim against the United States requires an express money-damages waiver, which 5 U.S.C. § 702 does not supply, and the waiver must say whether damages require proof of actual harm and whether non-pecuniary harm counts, because the Court has read the Privacy Act's damages waiver narrowly on both points. It must also name the source of payment: a judgment paid from the permanent Judgment Fund costs the Department nothing, so an incentive to comply requires payment from the agency's own appropriations, as Congress provided for FOIA fee awards. Intent, defenses, limitation periods, and caps must be defined. No current authority for EFTA damages is asserted.[^prv-action]

These first three proposals are minimal specifications, not operative statutory text. A complete annex covering definitions, jurisdiction, waiver, standing, service, deadlines, appeals, appropriations, effective date, application to the completed production and to pending actions, severability, and interaction clauses is deferred to a later edition. The statutory analogies show possible mechanisms, not that transplanting a label creates an effective remedy.[^env-citizen-suit][^vaughn]

Every consequence in the package except the reporting and notice duties of Proposals 4 and 5 needs a plaintiff and a court (Appendix C, Tables C.4 and C.5). Consequences that attach without litigation and without touching victim protection are available: lapse of a temporary or non-enumerated withholding that is not logged and renewed, a burden on the agency to justify each entry, sworn certification by a named official, payment of any award from the Department's own appropriations, and a rule that congressional silence does not ratify a compliance position. Their design is left to a later edition.

4. **Auditable compliance reporting.** An auditor with an identified appointment process, conflict rules, secure access, and a separately stated budget would inspect processing records and report to Congress. The proposal must say which branch houses the auditor. A legislative-branch auditor on the Government Accountability Office model fits the functions in aid of Congress that *Slaughter* left open, but could hold no executive power and would inherit the limits on the Comptroller General's access suit (31 U.S.C. § 716(d)); an auditor inside the Department is subject to the Attorney General's authority to prohibit an audit that requires sensitive investigative information (5 U.S.C. § 413(a)). The statute would need enforceable record-preservation and access duties, and protected annexes would keep victim identities out of the audit. The Sarbanes-Oxley analogy supplies a reporting model, not automatic independence.[^sox]

5. **IG and whistleblower protections after *Slaughter*.** *Trump v. Slaughter* (June 29, 2026) invalidated the Federal Trade Commission's removal restriction and overruled whatever remained of *Humphrey's Executor* beyond its observation about bodies that exercise no executive power, while stating that Congress may establish agencies to assist it with its own functions and reserving, in Part III-B, investigative and informative functions in aid of Congress and the fate of officials not before the Court.[^slaughter] That reasoning places for-cause inspector-general (IG) protection in doubt without deciding it. The more defensible fallback is enforceable notice and reasons, preservation of pending records, direct lawful transmission to Congress, and court access for independently cognizable injuries. A rule barring closure of IG inquiries would in practice amend 5 U.S.C. § 413(a), and judicial review of urgent-concern or reprisal decisions would require changing provisions that now bar it or leave enforcement to the President; each requires separate analysis.[^ig-wb][^reform-records]

6. **Congressional analytical capacity with governed access.** An analysis platform could help Congress inspect large productions within lawful access entitlements. It needs funding, secure custody, evaluation against independent samples, and human review of errors; the detector's tuning limits show that technology is itself a constraint. Contractors must have purpose-limited access and audit logs, and neither an artificial-intelligence result nor a framework-string match should release a record or establish a violation.

7. **Civil enforcement of congressional subpoenas.** None of the first six proposals changes Congress's position, the firmest constraint. A statute would have to supply a cause of action for either House, or a committee it authorizes by resolution, against an executive officer; jurisdiction reaching such officers, which for the Senate means amending its statute's exclusion; and a merits standard, which for a constitutionally based privilege remains the courts' balancing of the committee's need against the executive's interest.[^remedy-civil-enforcement] The House passed only procedural and remedial rules in 2017: expedition, an optional three-judge court with direct appeal, a discretionary waiver of privilege claims missing from a timely log, which a statute should confine to statutory and common-law grounds, and penalties on an agency head for willful noncompliance, payable without Government funds (Appendix C, Table C.3). For the House, which already has a contested judicial route, the gain would be a settled cause of action, that court, and penalties; it would not alone have changed the January 2026 outcome, which turned on the committee majority's choice. Two limits apply. The authorization step is at least a majority gate in either chamber, and no statute can supply a willing majority. The action changes the forum, not the law of privilege; personal penalties on an official following a presidential privilege instruction would meet the 1984 Office of Legal Counsel opinion's reasoning.

**Adverse users and privacy.** These mechanisms must work when the requester is a harasser, implicated associate, partisan state official or member, or a contractor seeking access beyond its assignment.[^adverse-users] A victim's identifying or intimate material must not become a penalty for agency delay, and the amendment should make withholding of victim-identifying material and child sexual abuse material mandatory; the enacted § 2(c)(1) only permits it. Protected submissions, independent pre-access review, victim notice and objections where feasible, restricted-use orders, and reviewable access decisions should precede disclosure. A route-by-route adverse-user test remains for a later edition.

**The pending EFTA II bills.** EFTA II (H.R. 9694; S. 5005) would bar common-law privilege as a withholding ground, change "All redactions" to "Each redaction" in § 2(c)(2), strike § 2(c)(1)(E), and add § 4 causes of action for state officers, victims, and individual Members of Congress.[^efta-ii-state] The privilege bar would resolve the construction dispute prospectively; "Each redaction" would not itself supply an identifier, ground, or deadline. A State may not sue the United States as parens patriae for its citizens, though it may assert its own federal rights, such as § 4(c)(1) access to withheld records for its investigations. There is no separate waiver clause or damages measure; expressly authorizing monetary relief against the Attorney General supports a waiver, whose scope needs clarification.[^efta-ii-memo] A Member's suit would rest on a personal access right, a standing basis the D.C. Circuit has not settled since *Raines v. Byrd*. Neither the constitutionality, adoption, nor effectiveness of these proposals follows from the historical pattern.


---

## Conclusion

The historical episodes document recurring constraints on congressional access and enforcement, alongside substantive gains in disclosure, policy, and institutional durability; observations in every case count against executive absorption of disclosure duties, and in none does the bargaining description fit where its rivals do not. *Constrained negotiated oversight* is a descriptive vocabulary and coding scheme for that pattern; the selected cases and indicators cannot validate an invariant equilibrium. The one contrast coded, scored on the Senate committee's own demand, shows the same early conduct and a failed committee suit; its outcome came through a prosecutor insulated by regulation, using criminal process without classification barriers.

EFTA shows agency choices, disputed legal constructions, and a party-line refusal of one enforcement route within a strong statutory mandate. It also shows preliminary judicial compulsion under the APA. The frozen corpus measures released pages, visual flags, and text classes; it cannot identify a responsive withheld residual or an overall compliance percentage. The March 2025 training proves an earlier instruction, not continuity after enactment. The September *Phang* orders prevent the paper from treating all enforcement as unavailable, while leaving substantive compliance and remedial durability open.

The reform implication is conditional: clearer access rights, protected review, enforceable remedies, and a civil action for congressional subpoenas may change bargaining incentives, but must survive standing, immunity, removal, privacy, and appropriations constraints. Full legal drafting, full-text reading of the six books named in the literature review, and a venue-specific version remain for a later edition.


---

## Appendix A — EFTA Document Redactions Quantitative Analysis

**Objective.** This appendix separates the measured release, visual redaction, and document-level text signals as of August 30, 2026. Section 2(c)(1) permits withholding or redaction of the segregable portions of records in five classes; Section 2(c)(2) requires written justifications for all redactions, published in the Federal Register and submitted to Congress. The measurements below do not establish a reconciled responsive universe or an overall compliance percentage.

Three terms are used throughout and kept distinct, because the source text blurs them. A **redaction marker** is the evidence that a redaction occurred, either a box drawn on the page or the bare word "REDACTED"; it says nothing about why. A **justification** is the text label stating the legal basis for a redaction, a Section 2(c) citation or an inherited framework marker such as a Privacy Act exemption or a "FOIA Exempt" stamp. A document is **redaction-marker-bearing** when its extracted text carries any redaction marker. The Justification Analysis classifies document-level text signals against a finite marker taxonomy; it does not adjudicate the legal basis for individual redactions or establish compliance with EFTA.

### A.1 Scope, Corpus, and Limits

The corpus analysed is the twelve-data-set release (DS1 through DS12) on the DOJ Disclosures page of the Justice Department's Epstein Library, as counted through Bates EFTA02731789: 1,378,393 Bates-numbered PDF documents, EFTA00000001 through EFTA02731789, delivered as Concordance load files with per-volume image trees.[^a-doccount] It is not everything DOJ has published under the Act. The project's metadata catalogue (content version 283ac296cb1a, the version of September 7, 2026, queried on September 30 and October 1, 2026) holds two further sets above that number, which share no Bates number and no file although their ranges interleave. One is a separately held court-records collection, filed by case: 4,001 PDFs (40,776 pages), EFTA02731941 through EFTA02805767. The other is 8,220 PDFs (85,660 pages), EFTA02739953 through EFTA02858497, listed in the project's fetch record of May 23, 2026, which has 8,240 rows because it logs 20 files twice; by file it places 8,124 in DOJ's Court Records folder, 60 in Freedom of Information Act (FOIA), 16 in Prior DOJ Disclosures, and 20 in Data Set 12 itself, including the FD-302 reports EFTA02858481, EFTA02858491, and EFTA02858495 named in the June 25 *Phang* order. The count is therefore incomplete even for Data Set 12, and those 12,221 PDFs (126,436 pages) are outside every figure reported here. For the 8,220, the fetch record, not a live inspection of the DOJ site, is the evidence for their location.

As of August 30, 2026, the released production this analysis covers sums to 2,729,256 pages, about 2.73 million, from known per-document page counts across Data Sets 1 through 12; three documents carry no recorded count. The full-population visual census, which walked the same production from its image inventory, counts 2,729,259 pages and successfully processed 2,729,175 of them, excluding 33 unprocessable corrupt files (81 pages, 0.003%) and dropping the three pages double-counted by the one duplicated three-page manifest row, the Data Set 8 duplicate.[^a-doccount] These figures differ by small amounts, and each analysis has its own denominator: 2,729,256 summed pages, 2,729,259 inventoried pages, 2,729,175 processed census pages, 2,729,178 join page-slots, and 2,729,246 coded pages in the separate white-box calculation. Compliance Letter #2 (p. 2) reports *"over 3 million"* pages released and *"nearly 3.5 million"* combined with prior productions, a wider population that includes media and earlier productions. The measured Bates-PDF total is lower than DOJ's reported release total. The available records do not reconcile the two counting universes, and the Section 3 report supplies no page-total reconciliation, so both the released total and the roughly 6-million identified total remain unaudited executive figures.

Two scope limits remain: image forms not tightly bounded by the dark-box detector, and unmeasured audio/video masking.

**The text-classification step reads text, not images.** The classifier cannot detect an image mask without a text signal. The visual census separately detects dark rectangles. The white-box recomputation, as of August 30, 2026, gives a point estimate near 0.1%, but its detector is not census-grade: precision is about 6%, three of 220 unique candidate pages are excluded as unclear, leaving 217 evaluable pages, and only twenty detector-negative pages form an unbiased miss sample. A prevalence of a few percent is not excluded.[^a32-whitebox-recompute] The inspected white-box pages were identified as legacy FBI FOIA-style records; this limited provenance finding does not date every redaction or establish the complete later workflow.[^a32-whitebox-provenance] The prior six- and twelve-span examples were generic visual annotations recoded as zero white-box spans, so they cannot establish white-box examples. The reported 64.5% estimator and interval remain dark-box-only; a separate white-box point lift near 0.1 percentage point is not incorporated into them.

**Audio, video, and images are released media this redaction analysis does not score for redaction.** DOJ reports more than 2,000 videos and 180,000 images. The project's June 2026 transcription run processed 557 on-disk media files; its status CSV records 546 `ok` and 11 `no-audio` entries. Of the 546 nominal successes, 471 have zero transcript characters and only 75 have nonempty transcripts. Recorded durations total 446.938 hours among the `ok` entries: 426.228 hours in the empty group and 20.710 hours in the nonempty group. A second accounting, regenerated June 15, 2026, covers 3,175 restored media files from Data Sets 9 through 11: 513 have transcript text, 906 have an audio stream with no speech recognized, and 1,756 have no audio stream. A successful process status is not intelligible-content coverage, and `no-audio` is not a listening-based finding of silence.[^tt-media-crosswalk] Neither accounting measures audio bleeping or video masking, which remain unquantified and excluded from redaction estimates.

### A.2 Methodology

The methodology has three parts, one for each analysis step.

**Release method.** The released-page denominator is the sum of known per-document page counts across all twelve data sets, cross-checked against the visual census's independent page inventory and against the public concordance. DOJ's more-than-six-million-page figure is a gross potentially responsive collection with duplicates and overcollection. It is not a valid denominator for the measured release or for a withheld responsive residual. The audio and video inventory and transcription pipeline is described in the project's media-accounting artifacts; it produced the file counts and transcription coverage cited in the Release Analysis.

**Redaction method (page-level visual census).** The August 30 accounting records 2,729,175 processed pages after corrupt-file exclusions and duplicate correction. A deterministic raster detector flags dark rectangles. Its six configurations were evaluated and tuned on the same 400-page adjudicated gold set; the selected confusion matrix is TP 261, FN 2, FP 14, TN 123, giving sensitivity 0.992 and specificity 0.898. These are tuning-set performance measures, not independent validation. The set contains DS9 (185), DS10 (141), DS11 (73), and DS12 (1), with no DS1–8 pages. The Rogan–Gladen correction transports those measured error rates to the census; bootstrap uncertainty is conditional on that transport and the selected detector.[^detector-tuning] Apparent flags are observable counts; the corrected estimate has no corresponding identified page set. White boxes are outside this detector's target.

**Justification method (text-marker classification).** As of August 30, 2026, to classify document-level redaction and framework-language signals, without measuring whether individual redactions carried a legally sufficient justification, each PDF's text layer was extracted with pdftotext -layout (optical character recognition used ocrmypdf and Tesseract, but nine manifest PDFs still lacked a text layer and had no completed OCR), and a deterministic, priority-ordered regular-expression rule-set assigned each document to one of nine clusters by the redaction-justification language in its text. The rule-set is reproducible: the same input text yields the same cluster every time, with no model or sampling dependence. Two populations were examined. Document Set A[^tt-setA] is the eight-phrase-query union, the 2,670 documents whose extracted text matches at least one of eight redaction-related phrase queries ("FOIA Exemption," "withheld pursuant to," "REDACTED," "Lugosch," "FOIA Exempt," "Tartaglione," "Privacy Act exemption," "proposed redactions"). Document Set B is the broader classifier-driven population, the 446,675 documents the classifier assigns to any cluster other than the no-signal cluster; Set A is the headline denominator. Set B was examined diagnostically, found dominated by confidentiality-disclaimer boilerplate, and retained to explain why a denominator comparison would mislead (A.3.3). The Set A and Set B tables reported here are those of the classification summary generated May 3, 2026. The June 24, 2026 runs classified all 1,378,393 documents in about seven minutes (426.677 seconds on ten workers) and then refined the privilege subclasses; their 444,298 privilege-language documents match the May summary.

**Reliability checks and classification provenance.** The nine processing-guidance items are nine copies of two documents: seven copies of a two-page guidance sheet and two scans of the March 21, 2025 training deck. Five copies were read in full and identified as guidance rather than records adjudicated for redaction legality. The privilege-language refinement is pinned to the June 24, 2026 privilege-subcluster classification run and its input hash.[^privilege-manifest] Its 444,298 documents comprise 435,453 `DISCLAIMER_ONLY` (98.01%), 346 `PRIV_REDACTION_CONTEXT`, 3,788 `PRIV_PRESENT_BUT_NOT_REDACTION_CONTEXT`, and 4,711 `PRIV_PRESENT_NO_DISCLAIMER_NO_CONTEXT`. The four classes sum exactly to the denominator. A context label still does not adjudicate a privilege claim; the intermediate classes must not be silently collapsed into a binary justification/no-justification result. The manifest and row-level inputs take precedence over stale overview counts.

**Reproduction.** The classification inputs, scripts, run manifests, and selected outputs are held with the local review materials and are available on request, subject to applicable access restrictions. No durable public archive, DOI, or publicly accessible analysis repository is established here. The notes identify versions and files where possible; published DOJ records and court orders are independently accessible. Reproducibility is therefore limited by access to the local analysis substrate, not presented as already satisfied by a public repository.

### A.3 Findings

#### A.3.1 Release Analysis

As of August 30, 2026, the measured released production sums to 2,729,256 pages by known page counts. DOJ's gross potentially responsive collection exceeds six million pages and includes duplicates and overcollection. The difference is not a population of responsive records withheld in full, and no unreleased share is established. The Section 3 Report supplies no page count reconciling the measured release with DOJ's reported release totals.[^tt2][^tt-released-count]

The released media are distinct from this PDF-page analysis. Of 557 on-disk files in the first transcription accounting, only 75 have nonempty transcript output; 471 nominal successes are empty and 11 report no audio. In the second accounting of 3,175 restored files, 513 have transcript text. The transcripts therefore cover a small, selected portion of recorded content and cannot support corpus-wide negative findings.[^tt-media-crosswalk] Media redaction remains unmeasured.

#### A.3.2 Redaction Analysis

How many released page-slots carry dark-box flags? The visual census counts those flags across all 2,729,175 processed pages; it does not verify every flagged page as redacted.

The **apparent** result, the exact pages the detector flagged, is that 1,845,330 pages (67.6%) carry at least one dark-box flag. The apparent rate counts dark-box flags only. White-box prevalence is a separate point estimate with a wide upper bound and is not incorporated into the corrected dark-box rate. Joining the census against the text-marker classification shows the joint distribution of flags and text markers: 1,842,147 pages (67.50%) carry a dark-box flag with no text marker, only 3,186 pages (0.12%) carry both a flag and a text marker, 2,252 pages (0.08%) carry a text marker with no detected box, and 881,593 pages (32.30%) carry neither a dark-box flag nor a text marker. These four categories sum to 2,729,178 rather than the 2,729,175 censused pages because the census-to-classification join universe is 2,729,178 page-slots: the one duplicated three-page manifest row (A.1) enters the join twice, double-counting three pages. The three-page difference is bookkeeping, not measurement.

The **corrected** dark-box estimate is 64.5% (95% calibration interval 62.0–66.6%), as of August 30, 2026. Its complement, 35.5%, estimates absence of dark boxes, not absence of all redaction. The image-only join cell is an uncorrected 67.498%; it has no separately calibrated 64% estimator. White-box prevalence remains incompletely bounded and is outside the dark-box estimator and interval.[^a32-whitebox-provenance]

The detector is computationally practical but its performance remains design-dependent. The 400-page gold set was also used to select among six configurations, so the 64.5% correction may inherit selection optimism. An independently described 120-page manual re-code returned 68.3% (95% interval 59.6–76.0%); overlap with a broad interval is not proof of transportability. Earlier vision-model estimates covered different populations or had incomplete recall and are not interchangeable validation studies.[^a32-validation-studies] A held-out, dataset-stratified audit would be needed to establish census-wide performance independently; this revision does not claim to have run one.

**Transport sensitivity (illustrations, not confidence bounds).** With apparent prevalence about 0.676, the Rogan–Gladen formula is `(apparent + specificity - 1) / (sensitivity + specificity - 1)`. Holding sensitivity at 0.992 and varying specificity from 0.84 to 0.96 gives approximately 62.0% to 66.8%; holding specificity at 0.898 and lowering sensitivity to 0.97 gives about 66.1%. These scenarios show how plausible error-rate shifts alter the estimate; they are not empirical DS1–8 rates or a substitute for a transport audit. Data Sets 1–8, absent from the tuning set, hold only 39,016 of 2,729,175 pages (1.43%), so transport error there can move the corrected estimate by about 1.4 points at most. The published 62.0–66.6% calibration interval excludes model selection, dataset transport, and unmeasured white-box error.

The join has 5,438 text-marker-bearing page-slots (0.199%) as of August 30, 2026: 3,186 with a visual flag and 2,252 without one. Most detector flags fall in the image-only cell, which is why the text-based Justification Analysis sees so little of the detected dark-box activity. This is a distribution of flags and markers, not a count of verified redactions or legally sufficient justifications.

#### A.3.3 Justification Analysis

As of August 30, 2026, Set A contains 2,670 documents selected by eight phrase queries, including incidental hits and 181 C0 no-signal documents. Table 12 reports classifier assignments, not adjudicated legal justifications. The 883 framework-language assignments and 1,576 fallback assignments do not establish statutory justification present versus absent.

**Table 12. Document Set A classifier assignments (N=2,670 phrase-query documents, as of August 30, 2026; shares are shares of the query results, not prevalence)[^tt-setA]**

| Count (share of query results, not a prevalence) | Category | Description and examples |
|---|---|---|
| 314 of 2,670 (11.76%) | Common-law-privilege language | C7 privilege-framework strings; not a count of the five statutory Section 2(c) bases. On the privilege-subclass refinement, 122 of the 314 are confidentiality-disclaimer boilerplate only, 100 carry privilege language outside a redaction context, and 92 carry it in a redaction context. |
| 569 of 2,670 (21.31%) | Inherited-framework markers | "Tartaglione" (Bureau of Prisons FOIA litigation exemptions); "Lugosch" (the Second Circuit sealing standard as applied in the Maxwell litigation); "Privacy Act exemption" (FBI investigative records under 5 U.S.C. § 552a(j)(2)); and FOIA-exemption cover/justification language. (FBI "FOIA Exempt / SBU" form-stamps, 2 documents, are counted in the Residual row.) These inherited frameworks are not themselves Section 2(c)(1) bases. The classifier does not establish that each marker was the operative basis for a DOJ redaction. |
| 1,576 of 2,670 (59.03%) | C7 fallback with no matched inline framework | A finite-rule fallback, not proof of no justification; no finer unpinned subdivision is asserted here. |
| 211 of 2,670 (7.90%) | Residual | Documents matching one of the eight phrase queries incidentally (181 no-signal documents: a legal citation, a policy discussion, a court opinion citing a redacted source, or an image-only redaction whose only textual hook is incidental), plus 19 internal DOJ FOIA processing emails, 2 FBI "FOIA Exempt / SBU" form-stamp documents, and the nine processing-guidance copies, which match phrases like "REDACTED" but are *meta-documents instructing how to redact* rather than redacted records. |
| **2,670** | **(all phrase-query documents)** | |

Three findings come from the justification analysis.

**The largest assignment is the fallback class.** Its 1,576 documents are rule assignments, not independently verified bare-marker redactions. The 314 common-law-privilege assignments and 569 inherited-marker assignments establish framework-language classes, not 883 legally justified documents, and 122 of the 314 are disclaimer boilerplate only. The classifier supplies no validated statutory-justification rate.

**The inherited-marker count is fixed by the query design.** Set A contains 569 inherited-marker documents among 2,670 phrase-query records; across the whole corpus the same clusters hold 571 documents, so 569 of the 571 fall inside Set A because they contain the phrases that were searched for. Their share of Set A therefore measures the query design, not prevalence. Set B, the 446,675 documents assigned to any signal cluster, is dominated by confidentiality-disclaimer boilerplate (435,453 of its 444,298 privilege-language documents are disclaimer-only), so it is not a useful robustness denominator and no rate against it is reported. Neither set is a random sample of legally disputed redactions.

**A pre-EFTA processing instruction is documented.** The March 21, 2025 training (EFTA00172446, pp. 1, 6) directs boxes without exemptions on the page. It predates the statute, but does not establish later continuity or the impossibility of reconstructing justifications. The January 30 account of updated guidance is a rival explanation requiring dated workflow evidence. The August 27 notice and September 16 court order establish publication while leaving substantive adequacy contested.[^tt5][^phang-orders]

A reader can inspect EFTA00172446 at the DOJ portal, using the document's first page for its date and sixth page for the instruction. The earlier unpaginated white-box examples are omitted because a document identifier without the relevant image page does not adequately identify the claimed mask. This removes examples, not the separately qualified calibration result.

The Section 3 Report separately invokes three common-law privileges and reports no withholding under § 2(c)(1)(E). Its preservation construction and the author's contrary reading are analyzed in the main text. The January 30 privilege-based approximately 200,000-page figure is not a count of documents assigned to a privilege-language cluster, nor a disjoint withheld-only total. No public itemized reconciliation is established.[^tt3]

### A.4 Conclusion

Section 2(c)(2) provides that all redactions "must be accompanied by a written justification published in the Federal Register and submitted to Congress." It does not expressly require every withholding to appear on that record or each justification to name a category, although DOJ's December 19 letter (p. 4) promised "an explanation for any redacted and withheld materials" under that provision. The enumerated withholding bases and the publication requirement are separate textual obligations.

As of August 30, 2026, the classifier assigns inherited-framework markers to 569 of the 2,670 phrase-query records, a share fixed by the query design and not a prevalence. The former 5% marker threshold is withdrawn: a marker need not state the operative withholding basis. Counts of pre-enactment guidance are likewise not a compliance test. DOJ's account of retained original redactions also describes review; the records do not establish categorical absence of reconsideration.

No overall compliance percentage is assigned. The earlier 84% noncompliance and 16% compliance estimates, along with their 54% floor and 46% ceiling, rested on incompatible denominators. DOJ's gross collection cannot identify the responsive withheld residual. The corrected 64.5% dark-box estimate cannot classify each page's legality or total redaction status. White-box prevalence remains incompletely bounded; audio and video masking is unmeasured. These gaps do not license a replacement compliance percentage.

The earlier training, the privilege construction, and the absence of public record-level entries identify questions for audit and adjudication. They must not be converted into proof of post-enactment workflow continuity, a settled privilege holding, or a numerical compliance judgment. The September judicial record demonstrates active enforcement while leaving material disputes unresolved.

### A.5 References

These references record where the analyzed data came from. Bates numbers refer to documents in the DOJ EFTA production.

**Governing statute.** Epstein Files Transparency Act, Pub. L. No. 119-38, 139 Stat. 656 (H.R. 4405), enacted November 19, 2025. Section 2(c)(1) permits withholding or redaction of the segregable portions of records in five classes; Section 2(c)(2) provides that all redactions "must be accompanied by a written justification published in the Federal Register and submitted to Congress"; Section 3 requires a report to the House and Senate Judiciary Committees listing the categories of records released and withheld, a summary of redactions with legal basis, and the government officials and politically exposed persons named in the released materials.

**Primary data source.** U.S. Department of Justice, Epstein Library, "DOJ Disclosures" (Epstein Files Transparency Act (H.R. 4405), Data Sets 1–12), https://www.justice.gov/epstein/doj-disclosures. The production was released in twelve data sets (Data Set 1 through Data Set 12). As counted here it comprises 1,378,393 Bates-numbered PDF documents, Bates EFTA00000001 through EFTA02731789, delivered as Concordance load files; A.1 describes Bates-EFTA records outside that count.

**DOJ compliance instruments.** The framework the production is measured against is DOJ's Section 3 Report and its two compliance letters: Letter #1 dated December 19, 2025, and Letter #2 dated January 30, 2026.

**Federal Register check.** DOJ's August 27, 2026 notice republishes the Section 3 Report without Bates-level entries. In *Phang*, ECF 43, pp. 17–18, the court discharged the publication show-cause requirement while reserving the notice's substantive adequacy. The author's itemization reading and DOJ's categorical reading are distinguished in the main text.

---


## Appendix B — Per-Indicator Coding Evidence (Historical Cases)

This appendix holds the codebook and the evidence for each per-indicator coding in Table 7, including the Watergate tapes contrast. Cells that add a fact not stated in the body carry their own notes.

Pike and Church are observed separately below. Their shared episode is not a controlled comparison: chambers, authorizing resolutions, leadership, disclosure rules, and enforcement paths differed. Both committees bargained and both defied the executive. Church's inquiry produced substantial public findings and durable institutions; Pike's obtained evidence and lost the fight over official publication when the House bound its committee. Neither outcome alone identifies posture, or the parent chamber's response, as the cause.

**Codebook (actor scored, and what earns each mark).** Each case is scored on Congress's own demand; for the Watergate contrast that is the Senate committee's subpoenas for the tapes, the demand that was litigated. The House Judiciary Committee's own subpoenas of 1974 are not scored: the President answered the first of them with edited transcripts, and the committee charged his failure to comply with all four in an article of impeachment.[^tf-watergate-house] The Special Prosecutor's trial subpoena is recorded in the outcome rows. Each cell is scored on the period in the case heading: evidence from before it is labelled as an antecedent and evidence from after it as a sequel, and outcomes may be scored after a period ends. The executive-offer rows score the executive's conduct at the time, not whether its position later stood, which the response and outcome rows record. Scored this way, delay and self-definition mark almost any executive that resists a demand, so those rows describe the exchange and do not by themselves separate episodes. One author wrote the codebook and applied it; no second coder has scored the cells.

| Indicator | Actor whose conduct is scored | ✓ supported | ◑ mixed | ✗ contrary |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Exogenous rupture *(bracket)* | Source of the triggering disclosure | The disclosure that prompts the inquiry comes from outside Congress | The trigger is external, but Congress itself discovers concealed conduct during the episode | Congress discovers the conduct first |
| Demand posture | Congress | A committee or chamber formally demands records or testimony | The demand is limited to letters or requests without formal process | No demand is made. The row records the form of the demand; it is present in any investigation and does not discriminate between episodes |
| Selective disclosure | Executive | Records are withheld, destroyed, conditioned, or released only in part | Disclosure is substantially complete with identified gaps | Full production |
| Justification proliferation | Executive | Grounds for withholding expand beyond the governing text | One ground is asserted, or the evidence concerns claimed authority and not withholding | No ground beyond the governing text |
| Delayed compliance | Executive | Production came ninety days or more after the demand, or never came | Production came after the date the demand set but within ninety days | Production by the date demanded |
| Executive self-definition | Executive | The executive states its own reading of what compliance requires and acts on it | It states a reading but defers to a congressional or judicial process before acting | It accepts the requester's reading |
| Majority declines an available remedy | A committee or chamber majority | Most of the considered remedies shown to be available are declined, by recorded vote or non-use | Some are used and others declined | Those available are used |
| Remedy attempted and defeated outside Congress | A court or prosecutor acting on a remedy Congress or its members invoked | The remedy fails | Partial success | The remedy succeeds (n/a when no congressional invocation is documented) |
| Informal negotiation | Congress and the executive | Documented bargaining over access or release, whether or not it produced terms; bargaining later overtaken by adjudication is scored here, and the adjudication in the remedy row | Terms are imposed or offered with limited bargaining, or the parties' accounts dispute that a bargain was struck | No bargaining is documented: access is sought only by demand and adjudication |
| Limited individual accountability *(bracket)* | Prosecutors, courts, and the President | No durable sanction for an identified official | Some sanctions survive and others are reversed or pardoned | Durable sanctions |

A mark of ? means insufficient evidence to apply the row: the sources may not separate the conduct, establish a requested remedy's disposition, resolve a considered route's legal availability, or date both demand and production. The ninety-day line is a coding convention, not a legal standard. The remedies considered are those named in each evidence cell: a contempt vote, a suit, a disclosure procedure, legislation, or impeachment. The majority-remedy mark describes route choice among possible substitutes, not weak escalation or enforcement effort. In the external-remedy row, n/a means no congressional invocation is documented in the sources read; ? means a request is documented but its disposition is not; ◑ requires partial success. These marks are distinct from ✗, a successful remedy.

**Separate eight-indicator profiles (✓ supported; ◑ mixed; ? insufficiently separated evidence; ✗ contrary)**

| Process indicator | Pike | Church | Basis |
|--------------|:-----:|:-----:|--------------------------------------------------------|
| Demand posture | ✓ | ✓ | Pike relied mainly on subpoenas and contempt resolutions, Church mainly on negotiated access; each used both |
| Selective disclosure | ✓ | ? | Pike: a September 1975 embargo, then documents loaned on condition that the President arbitrate release, and a briefing in place of delivery. Church: the only evidence read is a four-month delay in answering the first SHAMROCK inquiries, which the delay row scores; no withholding or conditioning is documented in the sources read[^hf-pike-bargain][^hf-church-defiance] |
| Justification proliferation | ◑ | ? | An executive-privilege claim against one Pike subpoena and classification as the ground for the release conditions; no separate Church evidence[^hf5] |
| Delayed compliance | ◑ | ✓ | Pike: subpoenas served November 7, 1975 and returnable November 11 were met by accommodations the committee accepted as substantial compliance on December 2 and 10, inside ninety days, after the September embargo had ended in the October 1 agreement. Church: inquiries of May 1975 answered in early September, about four months later[^hf5][^hf-pike-bargain][^hf-church-defiance] |
| Executive self-definition | ✓ | ✓ | For Pike the President was made final arbiter of release; for Church, Executive Order 11905 pre-empted the statutory charter[^hf3] |
| Majority declines an available remedy | ✓ | ◑ | Pike: the committee accepted presidential-certification terms 10–3 and did not take its contempt resolutions to the floor, and the House voted 246–124 to bar release absent restricted distribution or presidential certification. Church: the committee published twice over presidential objection and the Senate recorded no disapproval, but the statutory charter was not enacted[^hf2][^hf-church-cluster] |
| Remedy attempted and defeated outside Congress | n/a | n/a | None was attempted; the Pike committee reserved a right to seek judicial determination and did not use it |
| Informal negotiation | ✓ | ✓ | Pike bargained over release terms and over the contempt subpoenas; Church bargained over access throughout[^hf-pike-bargain] |

Counting firm ✓ marks only, as description: Pike five of eight, Church four, Iran-Contra six, NSA three, the CIA program five, and the Watergate contrast five. These counts are not a test and do not rank the episodes: the contrast ties Pike and the CIA program, exceeds Church and NSA, and falls below Iran-Contra. Against the previous edition, Watergate's majority-remedy mark changes from ✓ to ? because consideration of contempt against a sitting President does not establish its contested legal availability; its firm count falls from six to five. The external-remedy marks change from ◑ to n/a for Iran-Contra and the CIA program, because the cited proceedings lack a documented congressional invocation, and from ◑ to ? for NSA, because the request's disposition is unestablished. None of these three changes affects a firm-mark count. Pike and Church remain n/a on that row. No other mark changes; the episode-level union in Table 7 retains the combined Pike and Church column as supported on selective disclosure and delay. The successful institutional products of the Church inquiry remain positive outcomes.

| Case | Indicator | Coding | Evidence |
|----------|--------------|:-----:|----------------------------------------------------------|
| Pike and Church | Exogenous rupture | ◑ | The inquiries followed Hersh's December 1974 *New York Times* report, which covered many of the subjects in the "Family Jewels" compilation and first revealed its existence, a disclosure from the press and not from Congress's own oversight.[^hf1] Within the episode the Senate committee itself found and disclosed the SHAMROCK interception program, having first inquired in May 1975; under Table 6 a congressional discovery counts against the bracket.[^hf-church-defiance] |
| Pike and Church | Demand posture | ✓ | Both committees made formal demands. Pike relied mainly on subpoenas and contempt resolutions and Church mainly on negotiated access, though each used both.[^hf5][^hf-church-defiance] |
| Pike and Church | Selective disclosure | ✓ | The executive suspended the Pike committee's access to classified material in September 1975, then loaned documents on condition that the President arbitrate any public release, and met the subpoenas behind the Kissinger contempt resolutions with reading-room access and a briefing in place of delivery.[^hf-pike-bargain] To Church it answered the first SHAMROCK inquiries only after four months,[^hf-church-defiance] which the delay row scores; Church's own profile therefore marks this row ?. This cell no longer rests on the House vote of January 29, 1976, which was an act of the House. |
| Pike and Church | Justification proliferation | ◑ | Executive privilege was asserted against one Pike subpoena, and classification was the ground for the release conditions; the record read here does not show grounds multiplying beyond those two.[^hf5][^hf-pike-bargain] |
| Pike and Church | Delayed compliance | ✓ | Dated events: the September 1975 embargo on classified material; the October 1 agreement; the November 14 contempt resolutions; the accommodations of December 2 and 10. For Church, first inquiries about SHAMROCK in May 1975 were answered in early September, about four months later. Pike's subpoenas, served November 7 and returnable November 11, were met by accommodations accepted on December 2 and 10, inside ninety days, so Pike's own profile marks this row ◑.[^hf5][^hf-pike-bargain][^hf-church-defiance] |
| Pike and Church | Executive self-definition | ✓ | Ford's Executive Order 11905 (February 18, 1976) set an executive-branch charter 68 days before the April 26, 1976 date on Church's Book I, and the proposed statutory charter never passed; for Pike, the President was made final arbiter of release. Scored on conduct, each is the executive's own reading, acted on: the order was issued in place of a statutory charter, and from September 18, 1975 the executive withheld classified material from the Pike committee until, on September 26, Pike and the House leadership agreed, and on October 1 the committee voted 10–3, that the President would be final arbiter of what it would make public.[^hf3][^hf-pike-bargain] |
| Pike and Church | Majority declines an available remedy | ✓ (House) / ◑ (Senate) | Remedies considered: contempt, a suit, publication, and legislation. House: the committee accepted presidential-certification terms 10–3 on October 1, 1975 and declared substantial compliance on its contempt subpoenas in December instead of taking them to the floor; on January 29, 1976 the House voted 246–124, with 127 Democrats and 119 Republicans in the majority, to bar release of a report containing classified material unless restricted to authorized recipients or certified by the President.[^hf-pike-bargain][^hf2] Senate: the committee published SHAMROCK and the assassination report over presidential objection and the Senate recorded no disapproval, but the statutory charter was not enacted.[^hf-church-defiance][^hf-church-cluster] |
| Pike and Church | Remedy attempted and defeated outside Congress | n/a | None was attempted. The Pike committee reserved a right to seek judicial determination and did not use it, and its contempt resolutions were not taken to the House floor.[^hf-pike-bargain] The one prosecution in the episode for an offence against Congress, of Helms, ended in a plea and fine.[^hf-accountability] |
| Pike and Church | Informal negotiation | ✓ | Both committees bargained: Pike over release terms with the President (the October 1 agreement) and over the contempt subpoenas (the December compromise, of which Pike said "we got more than they wanted to give us"); Church over access throughout.[^hf-pike-bargain][^hf3] |
| Pike and Church | Limited individual accountability | ◑ | Accountability was limited and partly reversed. Former Director of Central Intelligence Richard Helms pleaded nolo contendere in 1977 to two misdemeanors of not testifying fully to the Senate about CIA covert action in Chile, drawing a suspended sentence and a $2,000 fine; FBI officials W. Mark Felt and Edward S. Miller, convicted in 1980 of conspiracy to violate citizens' rights through 1972–1973 warrantless break-ins, were pardoned by President Reagan while their appeal was pending, as he announced on April 15, 1981. The Helms plea and fine remained.[^hf-accountability] |
| Iran-Contra | Exogenous rupture | ✓ | The Hasenfus shootdown (October 5, 1986) exposed the Contra-resupply half and the *Ash-Shiraa* report (November 3, 1986) the Iran half; both broke from outside the oversight channel.[^hf-hasenfus][^hf6] |
| Iran-Contra | Demand posture | ✓ | The House and Senate select committees (H. Res. 12 and S. Res. 23) held joint hearings with subpoena power and obtained testimony under immunity orders.[^hf6][^hf-immunity] |
| Iran-Contra | Selective disclosure | ✓ | Senior officials held back information from Congress: the contemporaneous notes of Weinberger and Regan, and those dictated by Shultz, were withheld until 1991 and 1992, and Poindexter, North, and others shredded documents from November 21, 1986, as the Attorney General's inquiry began and before the Independent Counsel was appointed or the select committees were established.[^hf-ic-withheld] The previous edition scored this cell mixed on the ground that immunity bounded later prosecutions, which describes Congress's act and not the executive's disclosure. |
| Iran-Contra | Justification proliferation | ✓ | A doctrine of presidential discretion over notification was advanced in the Cooper opinion, NSDD-286, and the 1989 Bush letter to Senator Boren, and endorsed in the committees' minority report. NSDD-286 also narrowed that discretion while reserving it: it barred retroactive findings and, where prior notice was withheld, set notice to the committees within two working days unless the President directed otherwise.[^hf9] |
| Iran-Contra | Delayed compliance | ✓ | The select committees reported in November 1987 without the contemporaneous notes of Weinberger and Regan, and those dictated by Shultz, which were never produced to them. The notes reached the Independent Counsel only in 1991 and 1992, Weinberger's in late 1991; Regan's 1992 notes and the December 1992 discovery that Bush had failed to produce a diary are sequels. The Independent Counsel records that the limitation period had run on November 1986 activities before his office obtained its evidence.[^hf-ic-withheld] The previous edition scored this cell contrary because the committees convened promptly, which describes Congress and not the executive. |
| Iran-Contra | Executive self-definition | ✓ | The executive treated covert-action notification as a matter of presidential discretion and reserved constitutional authority to withhold notice beyond a few days.[^hf9][^hf-1991-act] |
| Iran-Contra | Majority declines an available remedy | ✗ | Remedies considered: immunity orders, a public report, and legislation, all used. The intelligence committees pressed notification legislation for almost four years, through one veto, to the 1991 Act; they did not obtain the 48-hour rule, and the constitutional reservation stood, which is the partial success of a remedy used, not a remedy declined.[^hf-1991-act] The sources read document no remedy that the committees or either chamber declined, so the cell is contrary, and Iran-Contra is the one historical case in which Congress used every remedy it considered. The December 1992 pardons are a presidential act and are not scored in this row. |
| Iran-Contra | Remedy attempted and defeated outside Congress | n/a | Initiator/request: no congressional act invoking the scored prosecutions is documented here; the Attorney General requested appointment of an independent counsel. Decision-maker: the Special Division appointed Walsh, who prosecuted. Requested result: investigation and, if warranted, prosecution. Observed disposition: convictions, reversals and pardons are retained in individual accountability below, rather than scored as a congressional remedy merely because an offence was against Congress.[^hf8] This n/a is a limit of the cited coding warrant, not a finding that no congressional referral ever occurred. |
| Iran-Contra | Informal negotiation | ✓ | The 1991 notification settlement was negotiated between the intelligence committees and two administrations, and the timing of the immunity orders was negotiated between the committees and the Independent Counsel in March 1987.[^hf-1991-act][^hf-immunity] |
| Iran-Contra | Limited individual accountability | ◑ | Mixed. The prosecutions, including those for offences against Congress, are scored here rather than as congressional invocations: McFarlane, Abrams and Fiers pleaded guilty to withholding information from Congress, and George was convicted of false statements and perjury before Congress. The Fernandez case, charging obstruction of the Tower Commission and false statements to government agencies, was dismissed after the Attorney General barred disclosure of classified evidence.[^hf7][^hf-cipa] Of fourteen persons charged, eleven were convicted and several convictions and pleas survived. North's conviction was vacated and Poindexter's reversed on use-immunity grounds. Bush pardoned four convicted defendants (McFarlane, Abrams, Fiers, George) and two awaiting trial (Weinberger, Clarridge). Pardons foreclosed or remitted criminal consequences; they did not expunge the convictions, pleas, or historical findings.[^hf8] |
| NSA bulk collection | Exogenous rupture | ✓ | The program's court order and Section 215 basis reached public view through Snowden's June 2013 disclosures; a May 2006 *USA Today* report had described the collection of call records. Both came from outside Congress.[^hf13] A December 2005 *New York Times* report, also from outside, concerned warrantless interception of international calls and e-mail under the same presidential program and did not disclose the call-records collection; the NSA then briefed the surveillance court's judges.[^hf-nyt-2005] Wyden and Udall raised alarms inside the channel in 2011 and 2012, within classification limits.[^hf11] |
| NSA bulk collection | Demand posture | ◑ | Wyden and Udall used public letters under secrecy constraints. S. Res. 400 also permitted subpoenas and a formal classified-information disclosure process; the letters were not the only authorized means of challenge.[^hf11][^hf10] |
| NSA bulk collection | Selective disclosure | ✓ | The legal interpretation was given to four committees and withheld from the public and, in the orders themselves, from other members; the first court opinion explaining it was issued on August 29, 2013.[^hf11][^hf13] |
| NSA bulk collection | Justification proliferation | ◑ | The evidence concerns collection authority, the classified reading of Section 215 that the Second Circuit later held the statute did not authorize, and not grounds for withholding; classification was the single withholding ground throughout. Scored mixed because the indicator as defined is only partly engaged.[^hf13] |
| NSA bulk collection | Delayed compliance | ✓ | In August 2009 DOJ and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence announced a process for releasing significant court opinions; in March 2012 Wyden and Udall wrote that not one redacted opinion had been released, and the first opinion on the program appeared after the 2013 disclosures.[^hf11][^hf13] |
| NSA bulk collection | Executive self-definition | ✓ | In 2001–07 briefing access required White House approval; from 2006 the government's classified applications, approved by court orders, supplied the statute's operative meaning until the August 2013 opinion and the later appellate rulings.[^hf10-nsa-oig][^hf13] |
| NSA bulk collection | Majority declines an available remedy | ◑ | Remedies considered: legislation and the S. Res. 400 disclosure procedure, which was not used. In 2008 Congress barred civil suits against assisting providers;[^hf-nyt-2005] it extended Section 215 in 2010 and 2011 while four committees held the orders and a classified summary had been offered to all members (in 2011 the House committee did not circulate it); after the 2013 disclosures it enacted the USA FREEDOM Act. Hayden's recollection that *"no one ever said that NSA should stop what it was doing"* concerns the 2001–07 briefings and is his inference, not proof of consent.[^hf13][^hf10-nsa-oig] |
| NSA bulk collection | Remedy attempted and defeated outside Congress | ? | Initiator/request: Sensenbrenner sought action on Clapper's testimony. Decision-maker: the Attorney General. Requested result: investigation and prosecution if warranted.
