Observed disposition: Holder acknowledged the letter and review of member-supplied material on April 8, 2014, but did not confirm an investigation. The record establishes a request, not its disposition or partial success; no charge is established.[^hf12] Private plaintiffs' litigation is outside this row.[^hf-215-litigation] |
| NSA bulk collection | Informal negotiation | ◑ | Terms of access were set by the executive and the court more than bargained; the Wyden-Udall letters are the trace of constrained bargaining.[^hf11] |
| NSA bulk collection | Limited individual accountability | ✓ | Holder acknowledged Sensenbrenner's letter and member-supplied material on April 8, 2014. That is a request for action, not proof of a formal committee referral or a confirmed investigation. No charge is established in the reviewed record.[^hf12] |
| CIA Interrogation Program | Exogenous rupture | ✓ | The December 2007 *New York Times* report of the destroyed interrogation videotapes preceded the committee's review, which began on December 11, 2007 after the CIA Director briefed it that day; the ninety-two-tape count surfaced in March 2009 litigation.[^hf-cia-tapes][^hf14] Committee access had widened only on September 6, 2006, the day the President publicly acknowledged the program, by which time 117 of the 119 known detainees had entered CIA custody: an executive choice, not a congressional discovery.[^hf14] |
| CIA Interrogation Program | Demand posture | ✓ | The SSCI authorized a study based on more than six million pages of CIA records (March 5, 2009), a strong demand that nonetheless ran through negotiated declassification of the CIA's own record rather than compelled public release.[^hf14] |
| CIA Interrogation Program | Selective disclosure | ✓ | Until September 6, 2006 the CIA restricted information to the committee's chairman and vice chairman; the White House withheld approximately 9,400 documents pending a determination on executive privilege; and only the Executive Summary (499 pages, released with the Findings and Conclusions as a 525-page document) was made public, on December 9, 2014, while the full report, more than 6,700 pages, stayed classified.[^hf14] |
| CIA Interrogation Program | Justification proliferation | ◑ | The evidence concerns interrogation authority more than withholding. The Office of Legal Counsel's January 15, 2009 memorandum for the files stated that the earlier opinions' categorical claim, that the Commander in Chief power precludes Congress from legislating on detention and interrogation, was not sustainable, while reserving as-applied constitutional objections.[^hf16] The withholding grounds were classification and a pending executive-privilege determination.[^hf14] |
| CIA Interrogation Program | Delayed compliance | ✓ | The committee approved the study in December 2012; the executive's declassification review then ran until the December 2014 release of the Executive Summary, and the full report was not released. The study period itself (March 2009 to December 2012) is the committee's time, not executive delay.[^hf14] |
| CIA Interrogation Program | Executive self-definition | ✓ | The White House withheld approximately 9,400 CIA documents pending an executive-privilege determination and did not answer the committee's three written requests of 2013, and the CIA disputed the committee's findings in its June 2013 response.[^hf14][^hf15] |
| CIA Interrogation Program | Majority declines an available remedy | ◑ | Remedies considered: a study, declassification, release of the full report, and legislation. The committee approved the study 9–6 and voted to send the Executive Summary to the President for declassification; it did not release the full report, which the D.C. Circuit held in 2016, as a sequel, to be a congressional record under the committee's control. Congress legislated in 2005 and, as a sequel, in 2015.[^hf14][^hf16][^hf-dta-mca] |
| CIA Interrogation Program | Remedy attempted and defeated outside Congress | n/a | Initiator/request: the CIA Inspector General referred possible misconduct by CIA personnel, and the acting general counsel filed a crimes report concerning committee staff. Decision-maker: DOJ. Requested result: criminal investigation. Observed disposition: no charges; DOJ reported insufficient evidence to warrant a criminal investigation. Both referrals were executive-origin; no congressional invocation is documented in this evidence. They remain counter-pressure and accountability evidence in the following rows.[^hf15] |
| CIA Interrogation Program | Informal negotiation | ✓ | The release proceeded through negotiation amid CIA counter-pressure (the staff-computer searches, the criminal referral, and declassification fights).[^hf15] |
| CIA Interrogation Program | Limited individual accountability | ✓ | Accountability was declined. No senior official was prosecuted; the Durham review closed with no charges; Margolis declined to adopt the Office of Professional Responsibility's misconduct findings against Yoo and Bybee, and the record reviewed shows no discipline; the CIA Inspector General referred possible CIA personnel misconduct, and the acting general counsel filed a crimes report against committee staff; both referrals ended without charges.[^hf15][^hf16] The Detainee Treatment Act of 2005 gave personnel who relied on authorized practices a defense, and the Military Commissions Act of 2006 redefined the war-crimes offense with effect from November 26, 1997.[^hf-dta-mca] |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Exogenous rupture | ◑ | The conduct first came to light outside Congress, when police arrested five men inside the Democratic National Committee offices on June 17, 1972. But the existence of the tapes, the records in dispute, was disclosed by the Senate committee's own questioning of Alexander Butterfield on July 16, 1973.[^tf-watergate] |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Demand posture | ✓ | After informal requests failed the committee issued two subpoenas in July 1973, the demand on which this column is scored. The Special Prosecutor's trial subpoena under Rule 17(c), of April 18, 1974, is recorded in the outcome rows.[^tf-watergate] |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Selective disclosure | ✓ | The President declined both committee subpoenas on July 25, 1973. On April 30, 1974, answering the House Judiciary Committee's subpoena, he released edited transcripts in place of the tapes; they were sent to that committee and published, not produced to the Senate committee, and they covered, with partial deletions, each of the five conversations it had subpoenaed, as the D.C. Circuit took judicial notice.[^tf-watergate][^tf-watergate-house] |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Justification proliferation | ◑ | One ground was asserted, a generalized executive privilege claimed to be absolute; the Supreme Court rejected its absoluteness.[^tf-watergate] |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Delayed compliance | ✓ | The committee never received the subpoenaed tapes from the President. When the D.C. Circuit ruled on May 23, 1974, ten months after the subpoenas, it treated the committee's need as merely cumulative because the House Judiciary Committee by then held copies. The Supreme Court's order of July 24, 1974 ran on the Special Prosecutor's subpoena, not the committee's.[^tf-watergate] |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Executive self-definition | ✓ | The President stated his own reading, that the privilege was his to define, and acted on it by refusing the committee's subpoenas and substituting edited transcripts. The courts' later determinations are recorded in the remedy and outcome rows: the D.C. Circuit decided the committee's subpoena under a presumption of privilege, and the Supreme Court held, on the Special Prosecutor's subpoena, that it is the province of the judiciary to say what the law is and that a generalized claim must yield to a demonstrated need for evidence in a pending criminal trial.[^tf-watergate] |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Majority declines an available remedy | ? | On the Senate committee's demand, the committee considered statutory contempt, the Senate's self-help (inherent contempt), and a suit. Its report says "it appeared unseemly" to proceed against the President by statutory contempt or "the self-help measures at the Senate's command" (S. Rep. No. 93-981, 1081–1082). These were considered alternatives whose legal availability against a sitting President was contested, so their non-use does not support a firm available-remedy mark. The committee used a suit, with a Senate authorization resolution and a special jurisdictional statute counted as parts of that route. The mark describes route choice among substitutes, not weak escalation. Impeachment remains outside this cell because the House Judiciary Committee's third article concerned that committee's own subpoenas.[^tf-watergate][^tf-watergate-house] |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Remedy attempted and defeated outside Congress | ✓ (the committee's own suit) | The committee's suit was dismissed, and on May 23, 1974 the D.C. Circuit affirmed: applying the presumption of privilege it had stated in *Nixon v. Sirica*, it held that the committee had not shown the tapes to be demonstrably critical to its functions. The tapes were compelled on the Special Prosecutor's trial subpoena, not the committee's.[^tf-watergate] |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Informal negotiation | ◑ | In October 1973 Senators Ervin and Baker met the President at his request. By the President's account, he proposed that Senator John Stennis listen to the tapes and verify a statement of their contents, and the two senators approved. The committee's final report states that they "agreed to no such compromise" and that the President "*unilaterally* offered certain materials to the committee"; the same paragraph says that the two senators understood him to be offering "verbatim transcripts of the conversations at issue, not mere 'summaries,'" that the committee "in all events, could pursue its lawsuit," and that the proposal never materialized and the suit continued. The mark rests on the codebook's first mixed category, terms the executive offered with limited bargaining, as the report's "unilaterally" describes them. The committee's earlier informal attempts were requests, not bargaining, and are not scored here.[^tf-watergate] |
| Watergate tapes (contrast) | Limited individual accountability | ◑ | The President resigned on August 9, 1974 and was pardoned on September 8, 1974; Haldeman, Ehrlichman, and Mitchell were convicted and sentenced to imprisonment, and their convictions were affirmed.[^tf-watergate] |

## Appendix C — Statutory Openings, Enforcement Routes, and Remedy Matrix

This appendix holds five tables that support the statutory analysis and the Recommendations. It does not count toward the main-text length. Page references are to the primary documents cited in the notes; "Letter #1" and "Letter #2" are DOJ's compliance letters of December 19, 2025 and January 30, 2026, and "ECF" numbers are docket entries in *Phang v. Blanche*.

**Table C.1. Statutory openings for executive self-definition in the Act[^app-c-openings]**

| Enacted feature | Who determines it | What the record shows |
|---|---|---|
| § 3: report due "Within 15 days of completion of the release" | The Attorney General, by declaring completion; no date certain | Completion declared January 30, 2026; report dated February 14, 2026 |
| § 3: report goes to "the House and Senate Committees on the Judiciary" | The statute | The committee that issued the August 5, 2025 subpoena and marked up contempt is the House Oversight Committee, which § 3 does not name |
| § 3(3): "politically exposed persons" | Undefined; applied by DOJ | The Section 3 Report (p. 3) states that the term "was not defined" in the Act and applies its own reading; its note 2 (p. 4) states that individuals "whose names were redacted for law-enforcement sensitive purposes are not included," although § 3(3) ends "with no redactions permitted under subsection (b)(1)" |
| § 2(c)(3): "covered information" withheld as classified to be declassified "to the maximum extent possible" | Undefined term; DOJ | Letter #2 (p. 4) states that no files are withheld or redacted on the classification ground |
| § 2(c)(4): "All decisions to classify any covered information after July 1, 2025" to be published and submitted to Congress | Undefined term; DOJ; the duty has its own trigger and does not depend on § 2(c)(1)(E) | The record reviewed here contains no published classification decision; the August 27 notice describes the duty as publishing "a justification for redactions and classification of records" |
| § 2(c)(2): no deadline, no named recipient beyond "Congress," and "redactions" but not withholdings | DOJ | Letter #1 (p. 3) promised a privilege log "as required under the Act"; the court later recorded DOJ's position: "Defendant states that there is no deadline for submission of the redaction information" (ECF 43, p. 6) |
| § 2(c)(1): "may withhold or redact the segregable portions of records that" | The Attorney General | Withholding is permissive even for victim-identifying material and child sexual abuse material, though distribution of child sexual abuse material is separately barred by 18 U.S.C. §§ 2252–2252A; the text requires no segregability finding, explanation, or reviewer |
| § 2(b)(1): a prohibition keyed to the basis of a redaction | DOJ | The subsection has no test, certification duty, or consequence, and it can be audited only if each redaction carries a stated reason; the Section 3 Report (p. 2) states that no record was withheld or redacted on those grounds |
| § 2(c)(1)(C): "narrowly tailored and temporary" | DOJ; no expiry or review | Not tested in the record reviewed |
| § 2(a): "searchable and downloadable format" | DOJ | Not assessed here against the format duty. After the project's own text extraction nine manifest PDFs still lacked a text layer (Appendix A.2), which does not by itself show a breach |
| No enforcement, definitions, appropriations, or severability section | | The January 21, 2026 *Maxwell* order (p. 4): "No provision of the EFTA vests this Court with that authority" |

**Table C.2. Bases for redaction or non-production that DOJ applied and § 2(c)(1) does not list[^app-c-bases]**

| Basis | Primary source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Deliberative-process, work-product, and attorney-client privileges | Section 3 Report, pp. 2–3; Letter #1, pp. 2–3; Letter #2, p. 4 (approximately 200,000 pages) | Contested; not adjudicated |
| Privacy Act | Letter #1, p. 2 ("as allowed under the Act and other applicable law. See Sec. 2(c)(1); see also 5 U.S.C. § 552a"); the review protocol as described in ECF 43, pp. 4, 10 | Reserved: "Whether the EFTA permits Defendant to redact information pursuant to the Privacy Act is not presently before the Court" (ECF 43, p. 10) |
| Names and email addresses of government personnel | The review protocol as described in ECF 43, p. 12; the March 21, 2025 training gave the same rule for personnel names (EFTA00172446, p. 5) | Show cause discharged, with no ruling on the statutory basis |
| "Law-enforcement sensitive purposes" | Section 3 Report, p. 4 n.2 | Not adjudicated |
| "Information the withholding of which is allowed under other applicable law" | Office of Information Policy crosswalk, p. 1, which lists this ground, citing the Privacy Act, in its column headed "EFTA Exemption(s)" and lists, under FOIA Exemption 3, the Bank Secrecy Act (31 U.S.C. § 5319), the Pen Register Act (18 U.S.C. § 3123(d)), 18 U.S.C. § 3509(d), section 6103 of the Internal Revenue Code, and Rule 6(e) | Not adjudicated; the Rule 6(e) entry sits beside the December 9 *Maxwell* holding |
| "Substantially similar" or duplicative records | DOJ's response as recounted in ECF 43, p. 5 | Rejected (ECF 43, p. 12) |
| Foreign-language records | Letter #2, p. 5; ECF 43, pp. 5, 14–16 | Rationale held "without merit" |
| Technically incompatible files | Letter #2, p. 5 | Not adjudicated |

**Table C.3. Congress's routes for enforcing a subpoena against an executive officer[^app-c-enforcement]**

| Route | Authority | Dependence | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Criminal contempt | 2 U.S.C. §§ 192, 194 | The certified contempt goes to "the appropriate United States attorney, whose duty it shall be to bring the matter before the grand jury" | The Office of Legal Counsel concluded in 1984 that a United States Attorney is not required to refer a contempt citation to a grand jury or otherwise to prosecute an executive official carrying out the President's instruction "in a factual context such as that presented by the December 16, 1982, contempt citation," and limited its conclusions to those circumstances; its 2024 opinion states the conclusion generally, as "longstanding Executive Branch precedent." The Department declined to act on the 2012 citation of Attorney General Holder and the 2024 citation of Attorney General Garland, each after a presidential privilege assertion; it prosecuted Peter Navarro, a former official for whom the courts found no invocation of privilege, and the D.C. Circuit affirmed his conviction on July 21, 2026, after the Department, under a new administration, withdrew from the appeal |
| Inherent contempt | *Anderson v. Dunn* (1821); *McGrain v. Daugherty* (1927); *Jurney v. MacCracken* (1935) | A majority of the House concerned, which tries and confines the contemnor itself; against an official who withholds on a presidential privilege assertion, the Office of Legal Counsel took the view in a 1984 footnote, and reaffirmed it on December 20, 2024, that Congress may not use the power | Long dormant; it does not appear that either House has used the power since the 1930s. A resolution to impose inherent-contempt fines on the Attorney General failed on the House floor on July 11, 2024, as the 2024 opinion records |
| Senate civil enforcement | 28 U.S.C. § 1365(a); 2 U.S.C. §§ 288b(b), 288d(a) | A Senate resolution directing Senate Legal Counsel to sue (2 U.S.C. § 288b(b)); the resolution is ordinarily debatable and exposed to a filibuster; if opponents sustain debate, cloture requires three-fifths of the Senators duly chosen and sworn (Senate Rule XXII), unless an applicable expedited procedure limits debate, and one directing suit in a committee's name must first be reported by that committee with four statements (§ 288d(c)) | By its terms § 1365 does not apply to a subpoena to an executive-branch officer or employee acting in an official capacity, unless the refusal rests on a personal privilege or objection and not on a governmental privilege. Senate Legal Counsel may sue "under any statute conferring jurisdiction" (2 U.S.C. § 288d(a)); whether a suit on another jurisdictional basis could reach an official-capacity executive subpoena is untested |
| House civil enforcement | No statute; authorization by the House (in the Lee text, a House resolution, with counsel appearing on the Speaker's authorization after consulting the Bipartisan Legal Advisory Group; under H. Res. 430 of the 116th Congress, a committee chair "when authorized by the Bipartisan Legal Advisory Group") | A majority-party gate at the authorization step | District courts have entertained such suits (*Miers*, 2008; *Holder*, 2013). In the Holder suit the court held in 2016 that the qualified privilege "invoked to shield material that the Department has already disclosed" was outweighed and ordered that material produced; the Department's appellate brief, as the district court later quoted it, said the Department "has complied in full," and in 2018 that court refused to vacate its orders as the parties' settlement required; the Committee's appeal was dismissed in 2019 on its unopposed motion, during settlement proceedings. The Judiciary Committee's 2024 suit against Attorney General Garland for audio recordings of the Special Counsel's interviews was argued in October 2024, stayed in January 2025, and dismissed by stipulation on May 21, 2025, after the recording's public release and without a ruling. The en banc D.C. Circuit held that a House committee has standing (*McGahn*, 2020); a panel then held that it lacks a cause of action, and that judgment was vacated when rehearing en banc was granted on October 15, 2020. The question is unsettled; a private recipient of two subpoenas in the Oversight Committee's Epstein investigation has asked a district court to declare that the Committee lacks any cause of action to enforce them against him (*Black*, filed September 3, 2026, pending on the docket read October 31, 2026) |
| House-passed statutory model | H.R. 4010, 115th Cong. (engrossed in House), proposing a new 28 U.S.C. § 1365a | Whatever authorization the underlying action requires: the bill presupposes an action "filed in a United States district court of competent jurisdiction" and supplies neither a cause of action nor jurisdiction | A docket clause to expedite "to the greatest possible extent" (hortatory) and, if the plaintiff requests it, a three-judge court reviewable only by direct appeal to the Supreme Court; monetary penalties "directly against the head of a Government agency" held to have willfully failed to comply, not payable from appropriated or other Government funds; a discretionary waiver (a privilege or other ground "may be determined to have been waived") for a record not covered by the timely privilege log that a new section 105 of the Revised Statutes would require, reaching grounds "whether statutory, common law, or otherwise." The bill does not amend § 1365(a), and the Code contains no § 1365a |
| Member-level suit | None | A court; no chamber vote | Individual members lack standing to sue over an institutional injury (*Raines v. Byrd*); the January 21, 2026 *Maxwell* order left the Act's co-sponsors free to file a separate suit and noted, without deciding, DOJ's argument that they would lack standing |

**Table C.4. Remedy matrix[^app-c-remedies]**

| Route | Who sues | Whom; waiver | What must be shown | Relief | Speed | What the route does not supply |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| APA, as in *Phang* | A person with informational or economic injury | The Attorney General in his official capacity; 5 U.S.C. § 702 waives immunity for relief other than money damages; whether FOIA is an adequate alternative remedy is contested | Agency action not in accordance with law, on an administrative record; the merits were treated as conceded at the preliminary injunction, and DOJ's October 1 motion contests them | Injunction and declaration | Complaint April 27, 2026; preliminary injunction June 25, 2026 | An EFTA-specific cause of action and EFTA-specific provisions for de novo review, agency burden, in camera inspection, and fees; conditional recovery under the Equal Access to Justice Act (EAJA), 28 U.S.C. § 2412(d), is distinct[^remedy-apa-gap] |
| Proposal 1 | A person denied information the amendment requires to be published, to compel the accounting, with findings naming the harms the duty prevents; then a person who identifies a logged record, or a victim | The agency or official; § 702 suffices for non-monetary relief | De novo review, with the burden on the agency | A schedule order, production, or correction; fees to be specified | A fixed accounting date | Secure court procedures (Proposal 2) |
| Proposal 3 | An identified victim or requester with concrete injury | The United States; requires an express money-damages waiver and a named source of payment | Liability and amount; intent, defenses, and caps to be defined | Compensatory or bounded statutory damages | After notice and an opportunity to cure | An incentive, unless the award is paid from the agency's own appropriations |
| Proposal 7 | Either House, or a committee it authorizes by resolution, under an express cause of action | An executive officer, under a jurisdictional grant that for the Senate amends the exclusion in 28 U.S.C. § 1365(a) | Validity of the subpoena; for a constitutionally based privilege, the committee's need weighed against the executive's interest | An order to comply; penalties on the model of H.R. 4010, for willful noncompliance only | A three-judge court with direct appeal, on request; the docket clause is hortatory | A willing majority at the authorization step, and in the Senate, if opponents sustain debate, a cloture margin unless an applicable expedited procedure limits debate; a waiver reaching constitutionally based privilege; a lower showing on the merits |
| EFTA II § 4(a)(1), State officers | A State attorney general, district attorney, or "other authorized State officer," for the State "or the residents thereof" | The Attorney General of the United States; no separate waiver clause; the express authorization of suit for "monetary relief" supports a waiver, with its scope requiring clarification[^efta-ii-memo] | Unlawful withholding, redaction, or delay | Declaratory, injunctive, and monetary relief, costs, and fees | The court "shall advance on the docket and expedite the disposition of a civil action filed under this subsection to the greatest extent practicable" (hortatory) | A State-held right for suits on behalf of residents (the State's own right is the § 4(c)(1) access for its own investigations and proceedings), clarity on the scope and adequacy of the monetary authorization, and a measure of damages |
| EFTA II § 4(a)(1) and (b)(3), victims | A "victim": directly harmed and meeting one of § 4(g)(1)(A)'s identification routes, including an affidavit alleging that harm, subject to § 4(g)(1)(B)'s exclusion and victimization exception | Same | Same | Same | The same hortatory clause (§ 4(b)(3)(B)) | An express procedure to determine direct harm and apply § 4(g)(1)(B)'s exclusion before access, specifying an independent decision-maker, burden and timing; the affidavit route alone supplies no such procedure |
| EFTA II § 4(e) | Any Member of Congress | The Attorney General of the United States | Non-compliance with a written request for unredacted access within 7 calendar days, or with a written notice of violation within 30 days | Declaration, injunction, mandamus, costs, and fees | Not stated | A settled answer to *Raines v. Byrd*: the access right is written as personal to each Member, a basis for standing the D.C. Circuit has not settled |
| EFTA II § 4(d), classified records | A State attorney general, district attorney, or "other authorized State officer" conducting an investigation or proceeding | The Attorney General of the United States, who "may not refuse, delay, or object to access ... on the grounds that the material is classified" | A request for "classified records described in section 2(a)" | "Full, unredacted review" in a secure facility under court-approved procedures | Not stated | An answer to the Article II objection the executive would raise (*Department of the Navy v. Egan*), and, on a literal reading, any records, since § 2(a) describes only unclassified records |

**Table C.5. Consequences that attach without a court[^app-c-consequences]**

| Obligation | Consequence that attaches without litigation |
|---|---|
| Enacted § 2(a) 30-day deadline | None |
| Enacted § 2(b)(1) prohibition | None |
| Enacted § 2(c)(2) justification | None |
| Enacted § 3 report | None; the trigger is the obligor's own declaration of completion |
| Proposal 1 | None; a right to sue |
| Proposal 2 | None; court review |
| Proposal 3 | None; adjudicated damages, then payment |
| Proposal 4 | Reporting of a refusal to Congress |
| Proposal 5 | Notice and reasons, which current law already requires in part |
| Proposal 6 | None |
| Proposal 7 | None; a right to sue, after authorization by the chamber |
| Devices named in the Recommendations and not yet designed | Lapse of a temporary or non-enumerated withholding that is not logged and renewed; a burden on the agency to justify each entry; sworn certification by a named official, on the model of the *Maxwell* protective order's requirement that the United States Attorney "personally certify" compliance; payment of any award from the agency's own appropriations; a rule that congressional silence does not ratify a compliance position |



## Appendix D — Intelligence-Network Underlay Assessment

Here, Words of Estimative Probability (WEP) denotes verbal probability terms; EFTA denotes the Epstein Files Transparency Act. Portable Document Format (PDF) page references identify pages in released electronic carriers.

A documented but unresolved question about an intelligence link is one of the two grounds for including EFTA in this paper. This appendix presents the evidence for a link and audits it. It retains documented contacts, instructions, investments, and attributed assessments, but withdraws the aggregate numeric/WEP calibration. The question is unresolved: the records do not establish that the Epstein-Maxwell enterprise was a service-directed intelligence operation. DOJ holds records bearing on it; the reviewed record does not show whether decisive records exist or who holds them. The other ground, executive custody, and the paper's EFTA measurements do not depend on the answer.

*Evidence bearing on the question.* The principal items are these; each is examined, with its limits and its ordinary explanations, under the criterion named.

- **A request to a former Russian official.** In July 2015 Epstein asked Sergey Belyakov, a former deputy minister with a Federal Security Service (FSB) academy education, for help concerning a woman, and Belyakov undertook an inquiry (Criterion 3). Senator Wyden's letter lists fifteen meetings with Russian officials and bank executives from 2015 to 2017 (Criteria 3 and 4).
- **An instruction to use Russian contacts.** In September 2015 Epstein directed counsel to have investigators "use russian contacts" (Criterion 2).
- **A partnership with a former Israeli prime minister.** Epstein's Southern Trust contributed to a partnership whose general partner Ehud Barak owned, and which invested in Carbyne, an Israeli emergency-response technology company (Criterion 7).
- **A source report held by the FBI.** An October 2020 FD-1023 records a confidential source's allegation that Epstein was "a co-opted Mossad Agent" (Criterion 3).
- **The Acosta remark.** Vicky Ward's 2019 column reports, from one unnamed former official, that Alexander Acosta had been told Epstein "belonged to intelligence"; Acosta denies making the assertion (Criterion 5).
- **An analyst's assessment.** John R. Schindler, a former National Security Agency counterintelligence officer, argues that Epstein did intelligence work and, from 2024, ties the Mega Group co-founded by Epstein's benefactor to a 1997 Israeli codename, citing unnamed officials in 2026 (Criterion 5).
- **A passport in another name.** Agents recovered an expired Austrian passport in another man's name bearing Epstein's photograph (Criterion 9).
- **Family history.** Robert Maxwell's reported intelligence ties, and Victor Ostrovsky's memoir allegation of a Mossad role (Criterion 11).

These items make a link a question the record raises. They do not answer it. None shows service direction, tasking, payment, or official acknowledgment, and several rest on one another (Table D.3).

*Alternative explanations and limits.* Criteria 1 to 11 give the ordinary explanation for each item. A 2025 *New York Times Magazine* investigation offers an ordinary account of Epstein's wealth and rise: "Abundant conspiracy theories hold that Epstein worked for spy services or ran a lucrative blackmail operation, but we found a more prosaic explanation for how he built a fortune."[^a-nyt-fortune] It describes expense-account abuse, inside deals, and a climb through Bear Stearns connections and personal introductions. Its subject is the fortune and his rise. It does not discuss the passport or find that no service relationship existed, so it bears on whether a service funded him and leaves the rest of the question open. Schindler's July 2025 guide also reports a public denial by former Israeli prime minister Naftali Bennett, that the "accusation that Jeffrey Epstein somehow worked for Israel or the Mossad running a blackmail ring is categorically and totally false."[^a-schindler-guide-2025] That is a denial by a former officeholder, not a finding.

### D.1 Methodology

The eleven headings below remain an organizational checklist, not a validated scoring instrument. The appendix assigns no score and applies no elevation rule.[^a-six-criteria-weighting] No empirically estimated likelihood ratios, base rates, or calibration study support conversion of these heterogeneous observations into a percentage or verbal probability. Intelligence Community Directive (ICD) 203 distinguishes the likelihood of an event or development from the analyst's confidence in the basis for that judgment; its separate source-quality standard addresses the credibility of underlying sources. Neither standard validates a numerical mapping, and none is used.[^a-icd203]

The relevant questions are actor, action, provenance, independence, and alternative explanation. A court or agency record can reliably establish that an allegation was recorded without corroborating its substance. Repeated productions of one email are one observation. Introductions to officials, private investment, and advice about publicity have ordinary explanations that must be evaluated before inferring tasking. Labels below describe the narrow predicate supported, not a score toward a service-operation conclusion.[^a-framework-literature]

**Table D.1. Evidence families and inference limits.**

| Evidence family | Dependence | Supported predicate | Missing inference |
|---|---|---|---|
| Introduction and calendar emails | Duplicate productions and replies share a thread | Arranged contacts | Meeting content, tasking, or intelligence purpose |
| Investment correspondence and bank questions | Overlapping business transaction | Financing structure and requested ownership confirmation | Service funding or operational use |
| Wolff/Thomas/Barrack correspondence | Quoted chains recur across carriers | Offers, advice, contact permission, statements about press inquiries | Executed compromise operation or changed target conduct |
| Schindler / Gertz / earlier MEGA reporting | Gertz quotes Schindler; older reports do not identify the codename with the group | Named analyst's interpretation and its genealogy | Independent official corroboration of the bridge |
| Family history, source reporting, institutional-looking email | Different provenance does not make the underlying allegations mutually corroborative | Association or recorded allegation | Operational continuity, authenticated service status |

### D.2 Bounded source findings

Bates identifiers below refer to the public United States Department of Justice (DOJ) document carriers.[^a-doj-pdf-url]

The eleven topics are numbered for reference; their titles describe the evidence under review, not thresholds or scores. "Not established" means the reviewed evidence does not establish the specified inference, not that the underlying proposition has been disproved.

#### Criterion 1 — Multi-counterparty facilitation (documented contact arrangements)

The records document an introduction or arrangement for at least two independent United States (U.S.) counterparties, Peter Thiel and Tom Pritzker. Epstein introduced Thiel to Sergey Belyakov in the July 1, 2015 message, "peter - sergey , sergey- peter." Thiel replied that it would be good to meet in San Francisco and copied his assistant; Belyakov subsequently requested timeslots for July 8 (EFTA00635218). For Pritzker, Epstein wrote "Churkin set 10th lunch" on December 29, 2016 (EFTA01057777); the staff calendar names Churkin and Tom Pritzker for lunch on January 10, 2017 (EFTA02200098). For Thiel, Epstein wrote "churkin confirmed friday" on October 4, 2016, and Thiel replied "Noon or 1pm on Friday?" (EFTA02448518); the October 7 staff message states, "It is for 3pp at 12:30! Peter Thiel and Vitaly Churkin will be coming" (EFTA02040190). These are a direct introduction and documented arrangements, not merely separate appointments with a common host.[^a-criterion1-records]

Public biographies describe Belyakov's Federal Security Service (FSB) academy education and former deputy-minister post (the Russian Government relieved him of it on August 6, 2014, before the 2015 contacts), and Churkin's United Nations (UN) ambassadorship.[^a-belyakov-bio][^a-belyakov-office][^a-churkin-bio] Those are biographical facts, not intelligence predicates. A career diplomat's ambassadorship is not an intelligence indicator; diplomatic office is not itself evidence of covert tasking. The searches behind this finding were not exhaustive, and duplicate productions were not deduplicated. This establishes at least two counterparties, not that no others exist; Thiel's two pairings count as one U.S. counterparty. No reviewed record states what was discussed at any of these meetings. The finding concerns facilitated contact and does not establish wrongdoing or intelligence activity by Thiel, Pritzker, or Belyakov. The May 21, 2018 Carbyne thread names Trae Stephens and Founders Fund and asks Epstein, "If you want to put in a word with Peter maybe :)", but refers to "Chris and Nicole" in the preceding introduction (EFTA02656963, pp. 1–3). The quoted chain identifies Chris Hopkins; it does not supply Nicole's surname. It is not counted as Epstein brokerage.[^a-efta-prefix] The Black/Barak calendar entries (EFTA00389511 and EFTA02046653) show common-node scheduling, not brokered contact; these calendar entries do not establish brokerage involving Leon Black or demonstrate wrongdoing by him or Stephens.

#### Criterion 2 — Private-dispute instruction (requested action documented; service direction not established)

Epstein's September 20, 2015 12:20 PM message directs Karp to have Nardello "use russian contacts ..." (EFTA02486513, p. 2). The September 21 page-1 header belongs to the outer message. The instruction is stronger evidence of requested action than an introduction, but neither execution, result, nor an intelligence purpose is established. The surrounding chain identifies Brad S. Karp as a partner at Paul, Weiss, Rifkind, Wharton & Garrison and concerns surveillance of a woman and the feasibility of a private case in London or New York. Karp reports that earlier surveillance had stopped and that no evidence had yet been found for the allegation being investigated; he discusses a London lawyer's advice about a possible criminal trial for blackmail. Epstein asks to speak with that lawyer. Correspondence with outside counsel about a private dispute is therefore the explanation supported by the thread itself. The limit on execution concerns the particular request for Russian contacts, not Karp's report of earlier surveillance. Nothing in this carrier identifies a state-service principal or distinguishes that requested action as intelligence tasking.[^a-karp-private-dispute] Wyden's letter identifies the woman in Epstein's July 2015 email to Belyakov, discussed under Criterion 3 (p. 13), treats the subject of an August 23, 2015 Paul, Weiss surveillance exchange (EFTA02488634) as "presumably" the same person (p. 14), and quotes this September 20 message as the same surveillance "a month later" (p. 16, citing EFTA02486513). The link between this thread and the woman is therefore the letter's, drawn from sequence; neither that passage of the letter nor the thread names her. The December 2018 Qatar security-company forward (EFTA02612143) records a request for further business details after discussion; it does not itself establish state-service tasking.

The November 8, 2017 Boies/Mossad messages (EFTA00964912 and EFTA00964979, p. 1 each) are questions Epstein asks Barak about Boies seeking former operatives for private investigations. Questions about another person's conduct are not Epstein's operational instructions or proof of what occurred. They therefore do not corroborate the Karp instruction as a second service-direction episode.

#### Criterion 3 — Contacts with foreign officials (contacts documented; operational relationship not established)

The records describe contact with Belyakov, Churkin, and Barak. EFTA02612143 also records Epstein's November 2018 introduction of Barak to a Qatari correspondent and Barak's December 2018 thanks for a meeting with "HH HBJ," apparently Hamad bin Jassim; that meeting was Barak's. Official positions and Belyakov's reported FSB academy education supply biographical context; they do not transform every interaction into an intelligence relationship. Wyden's March 20, 2026 letter describes a 2015 Epstein email to Belyakov at p. 12 and lists meeting rows at pp. 13–14.[^a-wyden-black] The meeting rows support the existence of arrangements, not their content. The email is different. On July 24, 2015, Epstein wrote to Belyakov "I need a favor" and asked "Suggestions?" about a woman he said was "attempting to blackmail a group of powerful biznessman in New York," and gave her hotel (EFTA01203506). The letter calls Belyakov then Russia's Deputy Minister of Economic Development, but the Russian Government had relieved him of that post nearly a year earlier.[^a-belyakov-office] Belyakov replied "give me a few days to get information about her" and, on July 27, that he was meeting "a guy who knows her/about her," asking for "any details about case you have mentioned" (EFTA00634422). Epstein answered "saying pwerful men taking advantage of women like her etc."; Belyakov wrote "Got it" and then "Text you tomorrow," where the email thread ends (EFTA00850284; EFTA00697997). This records a former Russian deputy minister undertaking an inquiry about a private individual at Epstein's request and receiving case details from him. It does not show what, if anything, Belyakov learned or passed back, or in what capacity he acted. "Favor" is Epstein's word; the thread does not establish service tasking. Wyden's letter heads the exchange "Epstein provided the Russian government with the location of women who were on your payroll for unknown reasons" and treats Belyakov's FSB academy education and former forum chairmanship as indicators of ties to Russian intelligence (p. 12); of Epstein's meetings with Russian officials generally, it asks whether he was working with them "to help silence victims of his trafficking scheme or protect other powerful figures in his orbit" (p. 14). Those are the letter's inferences, which these records do not establish.

The October 2020 Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) form FD-1023 records a confidential source's intelligence allegation; government custody authenticates it as a source report, not an FBI finding.[^a-fd1023] The released form identifies a numbered confidential source, contact by encrypted messaging on October 16, a report dated October 19, and first-level approval that day. The source claims to have shared calls and taken notes, but the Epstein paragraph also uses recollection and a conclusion that the source became convinced of an intelligence role. An ordinary explanation for this carrier is an agent recording a source's recollections, secondhand assertions, and interpretation of personal contacts, rather than establishing service tasking. The three released pages provide no reliability rating, demonstrated reporting track record, attached call notes, or independent corroboration of the Epstein allegation. References to previous reporting do not supply that reporting. These administrative and asserted-access markers do not distinguish the allegation from that ordinary explanation or establish its truth. The ambiguous machine-transcribed recording is not needed to establish any participant's identity or this contact predicate.[^a-barak-2013-recording]

#### Criterion 4 — Sustained temporal pattern (repeated contacts and transactions)

The cited meeting rows span 2015–2017,[^a-wyden-black] and the March 2019 Carbyne correspondence (EFTA02634615; EFTA01035810) extends the business record. EFTA01035810 transmits financial statements and a forecast under consent language. This establishes duration of correspondence and business dealings, not duration of a service operation. No Israeli registry filing is relied on, because no identified filing and page is available.

#### Criterion 5 — Credentialed intelligence-community source attribution (attributed assessment; not official corroboration)

The cited public interpretations are those of John R. Schindler, a former National Security Agency (NSA) counterintelligence officer and credentialed intelligence-studies analyst. His July 10, 2019 *Observer* piece (the paper was formerly the *New York Observer*) wrote that *"It appears that Jeffrey Epstein was involved in intelligence work, of some kind, for someone—and it probably wasn't American intelligence either,"* with Russian and Israeli services on a hedged suspect list.[^a-schindler-observer-2019] His January 6, 2024 *Top Secret Umbra* piece "MEGA, NSA, and the Ugly Truth About the Epstein Sexpionage Network" advanced an interpretive bridge from the 1997 NSA-intercepted Israeli "MEGA" intelligence codename to Leslie Wexner's 1991-cofounded Mega Group. That piece concluded that *"MEGA was viewed by Israel intelligence officials as a vehicle for espionage and influence operations in the United States"* and was *"co-founded by Jeffrey Epstein's billionaire benefactor."*[^a-schindler-mega-2024]

*Attribution limit.* Schindler's credentials identify the author of an assessment. They do not independently corroborate his underlying interpretation, supply a likelihood ratio, or fulfill a mandatory threshold in a probability rule. His publications from 2019 through 2026 are examined below as a source genealogy to distinguish source dependence from multiple independent confirmations.



The *foundational* publication is his July 10, 2019 *Observer* piece "It Sure Looks Like Jeffrey Epstein Was a Spy—But Whose?".[^a-schindler-observer-2019] That piece named Russian and Israeli services on a hedged suspect list without yet invoking the MEGA framing. It was keyed to Vicky Ward's opinion column[^a-ward-2019] reporting that U.S. Labor Secretary Alexander Acosta had been told Epstein *"belonged to intelligence,"* to what the piece called Acosta's "non-denial denial," and to Robert Maxwell's reported intelligence ties: a British counterintelligence assessment that he worked for the Soviet Committee for State Security (KGB), and what the piece called "pervasive allegations" that he worked for Mossad.

*Later record on the Ward account.* Ward's 2019 column attributes the remark to a single unnamed former senior White House official describing Acosta's Trump transition interview; Ward did not hear it from Acosta and cites no document.[^a-ward-2019] In a transcribed interview with the House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform on September 19, 2025, the questioner told Acosta that Steve Bannon had publicly stated that Acosta told him Epstein "belonged to intelligence." This appendix has not located the statement the questioner described. Acosta replied that he did not recall speaking to Bannon about Epstein and "never made that assertion," and said he has "no knowledge of his membership in the intelligence community."[^a-acosta-hogr-2025] He added that he had called the report untrue at his 2019 press conference ("I said, that's not truthful"), although his answer at that press conference, as transcribed the same day, was "I would hesitate to take this reporting as fact" and "I can't address it directly because of our guidelines," adding that "a lot of reporting is just going down rabbit holes."[^a-acosta-press-2019] The October 2020 FD-1023 discussed under Criterion 3 records a related recollection, that one of Epstein's defense lawyers told Acosta that Epstein "belonged to both U.S. and allied intelligence services"; it postdates the 2019 publication, and its independence from that account is not established in the released report, so it is not counted here as independent corroboration.[^a-fd1023] In July 2025, two months before the interview, Ward said she stood by the reporting and glossed the remark this way: "that's not as far-fetched an idea as it sounds ... You don't have to be a spy. You don't have to be on a government's payroll. But it does mean that you are a useful asset."[^a-ward-2025] In February 2026, without addressing the remark, she wrote that it is "unlikely Epstein was working for the CIA or Mossad, as conspiracy theories have suggested."[^a-ward-2026] The account therefore remains second-hand and unverified. Its subject denies it, in a transcribed interview that opened with a warning that knowingly false statements could be prosecuted; its author stands by her reporting without reading it as state employment; and no reviewed record establishes service status.

The *January 2024 formulation* is his January 6, 2024 *Top Secret Umbra* piece "MEGA, NSA, and the Ugly Truth About the Epstein Sexpionage Network".[^a-schindler-mega-2024] This is the earliest formulation of his MEGA-codename → Mega-Group interpretive bridge found in the Schindler writing reviewed here (the 2019 Observer piece and the eight 2020–2023 Top Secret Umbra Epstein posts reviewed for this appendix,[^a-umbra-posts] none of which advances the MEGA bridge; the August 2021 post links Webb's Mega Group article only under the words "conspiracy theorists who don't know much about real-world intelligence matters"). The piece block-quotes the May 1997 *Washington Post* NSA-intercept reporting by Nora Boustany and Brian Duffy.[^a-wapo-1997-05-07] As quoted by Schindler, that reporting describes an Israeli intelligence officer in Washington telling his superior in Tel Aviv, *"The ambassador wants me to go to MEGA to get a copy"* of a letter from Secretary of State Warren Christopher to Yasser Arafat; the superior rejected the request: *"This is not something we use MEGA for."* The piece also cites Lisa Miller's May 4, 1998 *Wall Street Journal* identification of the "Mega Group".[^a-wsj-1998-05-04] The Mega Group is described there as a 20-member club of wealthy Jewish American businessmen co-founded in 1991 by Charles Bronfman, Seagram co-chairman, and Les Wexner, the most notable client of Epstein's money-management business according to JPMorgan's client-review record (EFTA01480549).

The piece's closure formulation reads: *"We know that MEGA was viewed by Israel intelligence officials as a vehicle for espionage and influence operations in the United States. We know that it was co-founded by Jeffrey Epstein's billionaire benefactor. The rest remains speculation."*[^a-schindler-mega-2024] Note Schindler's own *"The rest remains speculation"* hedge.

The Bill Gertz column "Inside the Ring: Former NSA counterspy says Jeffrey Epstein was part of Israeli network," *Washington Times*, January 10, 2024 is textually derivative of Schindler. The column quotes Schindler's MEGA closure formulation, including "The rest remains speculation," and brings the attribution into mainstream-newspaper form, while saying in its own voice that in 1998 "The Wall Street Journal solved the mystery." It is documented as appearing four days after the Schindler January 6, 2024 piece. The textual dependence, not the four-day publication-precedence interval alone, is what establishes derivative status. Publication precedence alone would not establish derivative sourcing, since the 1997 *Washington Post* and 1998 *Wall Street Journal* primaries had been public for decades and could in principle have been independently bridged by any analyst. The four-day ordering supports the textual-derivation reading but does not by itself do the inferential work.[^a-gertz-mega-2024]

The *expanded substrate* is Schindler's February 2026 five-part *Top Secret Umbra* series "EXCLUSIVE: Epstein and Intelligence" (Parts I–V).[^a-schindler-series-2026] Part IV, dated February 20, 2026, advances a stronger Mega Group claim; the closure quoted above belongs to the January 2024 article, not Part IV.

*Framing-evolution arc.* The Schindler corpus exhibits a progression relevant to the source genealogy in Criterion 5. Stage (i) is the 2020-07-31 *Top Secret Umbra* "The Epstein Mystery Deepens",[^a-schindler-deepens-2020] the first Top Secret Umbra Schindler-Epstein piece, restating the 2019 *Observer* hedged-multi-service framing without yet invoking MEGA. Stage (ii) is the 2024-01-06 *Top Secret Umbra* "MEGA, NSA, and the Ugly Truth", Schindler's publication of the MEGA-codename → Mega-Group interpretive bridge and the formulation examined in Criterion 5. Stage (iii) is the 2026-02 five-part *Top Secret Umbra* series, Part IV, an expansion of the Mega Group argument, not the source of the January 2024 closure quotation. A six-year framing progression can reflect genuine evidentiary accumulation, analyst confirmation drift, or changed publication incentives in post-Epstein-arrest discourse. The reviewed sequence runs from a dismissal (2021) to an adoption resting on reports public since 1997 and 1998 (2024). A July 2025 guide re-quotes the "rest remains speculation" closure while saying the analysis "leaves little doubt that Israel and its friends constituted the secret force – though likely not the only one – looming behind Epstein's criminal *kompromat* operation."[^a-schindler-guide-2025] Part IV (2026) hardens the claim: the "speculation" hedge is gone from the codename bridge, the Israeli liaison explanation is called "untrue," and unnamed "senior Western intelligence officials" are cited, while Epstein's own involvement in creating the group is still called "unconfirmed, but … plausible."[^a-schindler-part-iv] No stage reviewed cites new documentary evidence for the codename-to-group identification. This paper relies on the documented source sequence as a citation genealogy, not as evidence of increasing truth without independent corroboration.

*Schindler-interpretive-bridge caveat.* The MEGA-codename → Mega-Group identification is Schindler's interpretive bridge within the credentialed-source chain used here, not an original identification: Whitney Webb raised that identification as a possibility on August 7, 2019.[^a-webb-mega-2019] The May 7 *Washington Post* report describes a disputed codename "Mega" through officials' accounts of an intercept, which led them to believe that Mega "may be someone in the U.S. government," without identifying it with any 1991-founded philanthropy circle.[^a-wapo-1997-05-07] Duffy and Boustany's follow-up reports surveillance of possible suspects, explicitly cautions that investigators had not determined that those individuals had acted improperly, and records alternative Israeli explanations, including a decoding or translation error involving "Elga," said to refer to the Central Intelligence Agency (CIA).[^a-wapo-1997-05-08] Gellman's May 17 archive article records Israel's formal account that "Mega" referred to the CIA's Israel-desk officer and an ordinary intelligence-liaison channel, and reports a U.S. official saying the administration "has accepted it as plausible," while the FBI had not told the White House its investigation was closed.[^a-wapo-1997-05-17] That is an ordinary explanation for the proposed request for a diplomatic letter. It remains an attributed explanation, not a confirmed identification. The contemporary reports record suspicion and an ongoing investigation, not a resolved identification of the codename; they do not establish its eventual outcome. Miller's 1998 report describes philanthropy, seminars, and shared projects as the activities of the Wexner-Bronfman-cofounded group; those supply an ordinary explanation for its meetings and investments.[^a-wsj-1998-05-04] None of these four articles identifies the codename with that group. None of them makes the bridge; Schindler is the credentialed analyst cited here, not its originator. The reviewed reporting supplies no evidence distinguishing a group-tasking interpretation from ordinary liaison and philanthropic activity. Similar names alone do not establish that connection.

*Bounded negative preserved.* The exact queries `"Mega Group"` and `MegaGroup`, with snippet review, found no identifiable Epstein-email hits in the full-text search (FTS) index searched September 23, 2026, including a secondary report (EFTA02856277).[^a-mega-fts-negative] This is a later search observation, separate from the August 30 corpus accounting. It cannot establish absence of differently worded references, unindexed records, or a concealed relationship.

#### Criterion 6 — Formal state-service documentation (not established)

The material reviewed for this appendix contains no declassified state-service personnel record, employment file, or official acknowledgment of formal intelligence-service status for either Epstein or Maxwell. Formal service status is not established; the appendix does not infer formal state-service status from the contact and attribution records in criteria 1–5.

##### Illustrative exhibit not establishing Criterion 6: the July 2010 ucia.gov display address

EFTA00736184, p. 1, preserves Epstein's forwarding to counsel of an email whose displayed address uses ucia.gov and whose signature presents the sender as Joint Military Attaché School (JMAS) faculty. The current federal .gov registry lists that domain under the CIA, a record that does not date the registration to this exhibit; institutional references identify JMAS as a Defense Intelligence Agency (DIA) school.[^a-ucia][^a-jmas] The released flattened record does not supply transport headers, authentication results, authoritative faculty identification, or the alleged attachment's contents. Domain authenticity does not authenticate this individual message or show that it originated on CIA infrastructure. Epstein's characterization of it as possibly misdirected classified material is his statement, not a classification determination. A forwarded subject does not rule out misdelivery. This exhibit establishes only the preserved display and forwarding, and does not establish service status.

#### Criterion 7 — Financial infrastructure (private financing documented; intelligence financing not established)

The SUM (E.B.) Limited Partnership financing is documented in the February 28 to March 14, 2019 email chain (EFTA02634615). That chain projects an increase from about 13% to about 26.33% of Carbyne, an Israeli emergency-response technology company, on a fully diluted basis after warrant exercise. Its $3.6 million Preferred-Right contribution table assigns $2.4 million (66.67%) to Southern Trust and $1.2 million (33.33%) to Montilla / NJF Capital (copied to Nicole Junkermann); those contribution shares are not established ownership-interest splits. The wire instructions name Bank Leumi LeIsrael, branch 832 (Pinkas); they were sent by Tomer Toor of Furth, Wilensky, Mizrachi, Knaani, a Tel Aviv law firm, and the funds were later confirmed received in Ergo's account. The partnership agreement (EFTA00810711) supplies the legal structure: it names Ergo (E.B. 2014) Ltd. as General Partner, and its § 12 treats Ehud Barak as the General Partner's sole owner and beneficiary, restricting the General Partner's authority if he becomes permanently disabled or, without the approval of the holders of a majority of the Preferred Rights, ceases to be the General Partner's sole owner and beneficiary. Epstein's March 14, 2019 “Yes” confirms the controlling-person statement, but is not the first primary admission: his signed August 19, 2015 letter (EFTA01110744) identifies him as president and sole shareholder. These document identifications reflect the substrate reviewed as of August 30, 2026.

These features also fit ordinary investment and reputational privacy: a vehicle, partnership, local bank, and counsel are not distinctive intelligence-financing indicators. Barak's February 11, 2016 statement that two executives were not fully aware of the partnership (EFTA02704699) does not establish deliberate compartmentalization, its cause, or a service purpose. Combining nondiagnostic features without base rates does not make the conjunction diagnostic. The financial structure is therefore retained as a documented business relationship; it does not establish intelligence financing.

EFTA01035810 preserves the financial-statement and forecast chain; EFTA01373148 records Deutsche Bank compliance seeking ownership confirmation. A bank's inquiry is distinct from an independently verified conclusion. The cited partnership agreement identifies Ergo as General Partner, while the signed August 19, 2015 letter establishes Epstein's stated Southern Trust role. No separate registry filing is relied on here. The United States Virgin Islands (USVI) complaint supplies allegations about the entity, not an adjudicated intelligence-financing finding.[^a-usvi-jpmc]

#### Criterion 8 — Media and influence correspondence (execution not established)

The correspondence supports the following actor/action predicates. It does not establish an executed compromise operation, a curated recruitment system, foreign-service tasking, or resulting change in Trump's conduct. Attempted influence and discussion of leverage are analytically distinct from demonstrated deployment.

**Table D.2. Media and influence correspondence.**

| Cluster | Documented actor and action | Limit |
|---|---|---|
| December 8, 2015; EFTA00839265 and EFTA00839268, p. 1 | Epstein offers Thomas photographs; Thomas separately asks whether he may pass to a political reporter a contact Epstein had sent him that afternoon; Epstein's reply, "Pass on midelfart wealthiest girl in Norway," names a different person and does not plainly authorize the requested hand-off | No instruction to distribute the photographs, no proof of publication or target response |
| December 15–16, 2015; EFTA02346409, p. 1 | Epstein asks for advice; Wolff proposes using a possible Trump denial for publicity or political benefit | Advice by Wolff, not an executed plan or tasking by a service |
| March 9, 2016; EFTA02469569, p. 1 | Epstein tells Barrack about press inquiries and his own nonresponse | Possible informational courtesy; no express threat, requested return, or demonstrated influence |
| December 1, 2018; EFTA01014745, p. 1 | Epstein and Wolff discuss a defensive media response | Self-protection and public relations are live explanations; no intelligence purpose shown |

The first thread's photograph offer and contact authorization must not be merged. Thomas asks about passing to a political reporter a contact Epstein had sent him; Epstein's reply, "Pass on midelfart wealthiest girl in Norway," names a different person and does not plainly authorize that hand-off. Calling it a photograph-dissemination directive would change the object of the permission. The House image carriers supply context for the contact reference, not proof that a photograph was distributed.[^a-house-oversight-7th]

The December 16 Wolff quotation preserves the comma after "for you": "You can hang him in a way that potentially generates a positive benefit for you, or, if it really looks like he could win, you could save him, generating a debt." This is Wolff's proposed strategy in reply to Epstein, not a record of execution. The committee's parallel email packet and staff memorandum are related carriers rather than independent proof that the strategy was used.[^a-house-3emails][^a-house-staff-memo]

The December 2018 quotations must likewise remain separate and chronological. In EFTA01014745, Epstein writes at 10:51 AM: "im aware. im thinking what would trump do". Wolff's 10:56 AM message begins "Claims are ludicrous and self-serving" and ends "this is all about Donald Trump." His 10:57 AM outer message separately says "...all about Donald Trump, the real villain." The outer Coordinated Universal Time (UTC) header is 15:57:27; the quoted inner times are displayed local times. The two Wolff messages are not one quotation, and Epstein's 10:51 message is not a reply to them.

Wolff's earlier 9:09 AM message reads: "Maybe we do agree to a big interview with you--Landon Thomas or something like that. But this has to be war gamed out." The words "or something like that" are retained; EFTA01014756 is a related carrier. This is documented planning of a media response, not evidence that Trump was the target of a foreign-service operation. The records do not show a subsequent publication or conduct change; secondary accounts of unsuccessful attempts to assemble material do not supply that missing execution evidence.[^a-intercept-thomas][^a-nyt-finaldays]

Criterion 8 therefore does not support a service-operation inference. The actor/action findings remain useful without converting a photograph offer, contact permission, courtesy message, or strategic discussion into proved compromise deployment.

#### Criterion 9 — Clandestine tradecraft (a passport in another name documented; operational use not established)

The cited burner-phone passages concern third parties. On November 29, 2016, Wolff tells Epstein about leaving messages on a burner phone and being collected at a different location by the other person's driver, adding "spy tradecraft!" (EFTA01060472, p. 1). This is Wolff's characterization of another person's arrangements, not evidence that Epstein operated that protocol. EFTA01741880, p. 1, preserves the November 28 statement about contacting the person through his wife; EFTA01787496 reproduces that quoted message inside a November 29 follow-up. The repeated quote is not a separate independent burner-phone episode.

EFTA02466645, p. 1, is a March 31, 2016 Epstein–Ruemmler discussion of reporting about 1Malaysia Development Berhad (1MDB) and legal theories concerning others' transactions. Its reference to burner phones does not document Epstein's use of one. The Barak incomplete-awareness statement is also not evidence of clandestine practice. These records therefore do not establish the subject's compartmentalized communications or counter-surveillance behavior.

*The Austrian passport.* This item does concern Epstein himself. Property the FBI recovered in July 2019, originally held in a safe at his residence, included an expired Austrian passport in another man's name with a photograph the agents identified as Epstein's (EFTA01301613, p. 1; EFTA00159104, pp. 2–3).[^a-passport-records] This appendix does not name that man: nothing reviewed shows that he knew of the document, consented, or did anything wrong. The booklet shows an issue date of May 21, 1982 (EFTA00021627, p. 3). It carries border marks from France, Spain, the United Kingdom, and Saudi Arabia dated 1982 to 1983 (EFTA00021627, pp. 4–8). An official mark dated January 12, 1987 ties it to another passport, and nothing reviewed identifies that passport's holder (EFTA00258505, p. 2).

The record holds competing accounts. Prosecutors said the stamps "reflect use of the passport," while recording that they did not know how it was procured or whether it was "genuine or fabricated," and argued that possession "gives rise to the inference the defendant knows how to obtain false travel documents." Defense counsel said it was acquired in the 1980s, "when hijackings were prevalent," and "only to be presented to potential kidnapers, hijackers or terrorists"; then that "a friend" gave it to him, that he "never used the document to travel internationally," and that the stamps predate his receipt of it. Those are counsel's assertions, and nothing reviewed verifies them or dates his receipt. The judge weighed the passport as a flight-risk factor and made no finding about intelligence work. In July 2025 an FBI agent wrote, "We don't have any additional documentation to show that he traveled under a different name" (EFTA01656330, p. 5). Austria's Justice Minister reported that prosecutors examined a suspicion of document falsification against Epstein, closed the matter on his death, and did not establish its purpose.[^a-passport-austria]

The passport therefore documents possession of a travel document in another name, with an ordinary explanation offered and unverified. It does not establish who procured or altered it, who made the recorded journeys, or why he kept it, and it does not identify a service, handler, tasking, or operational use. Repeated images of one booklet, and later summaries of counsel's account, are not independent evidence.

#### Criterion 10 — Independent foreign-government acknowledgment (not established)

None of the public sources reviewed here shows a foreign government acknowledging Epstein, Maxwell, or any associated entity as an asset of its intelligence service.

#### Criterion 11 — Family and business associations (continuity not established)

Three different strands require separation. The first is Robert Maxwell's alleged Mossad *sayan* role, asserted in Victor Ostrovsky's 1994 memoir. A *sayan* (Hebrew for "helper"; plural *sayanim*) is described in the memoir literature as a Jewish diaspora volunteer who provides logistical, financial, informational, or operational assistance to Israel's foreign intelligence service (Mossad) without being a formal officer of the service. Victor Ostrovsky and Claire Hoy, *By Way of Deception: The Making and Unmaking of a Mossad Officer* (St. Martin's Press, 1990), supplies the general definition and examples of roles. The Maxwell-specific claim belongs to Victor Ostrovsky, *The Other Side of Deception* (HarperCollins, 1994), which also gives the alleged Mossad-Kidon-kill account of November 4–5, 1991.[^a-ostrovsky-maxwell] The latter book explicitly calls Maxwell a "sayan on the grand scale" while distinguishing that status from being an agent himself; the allegation is more specific than a description of ordinary business contacts, but is still the memoir's account. The 1990 definition does not independently corroborate it. Ordinary family relationships and separate criminal or professional careers explain the associations below without requiring inherited service tasking. Neither the general definition nor the 1994 allegations about Robert establish tasking of Epstein or Ghislaine, or distinguish operational inheritance from those ordinary explanations. The cited passages of the FBI Foreign Counterintelligence file on Robert Maxwell (105-25063) and a bounded optical character recognition (OCR) search of it do not establish that Mossad role. Instead, Part 1 records a 1954 informant's statement that Maxwell claimed British intelligence service and missions; OCR across the eight-part FBI Vault release found no Mossad reference and only incidental, unrelated mentions of Israel. The cited pages are readable except for the informant's withheld identity, not heavily redacted.[^a-maxwell-fbi]

The second strand is Ghislaine Maxwell's documented participation in Epstein's criminal enterprise, *United States v. Maxwell*, No. 20-cr-330 (S.D.N.Y. June 29, 2022), ECF 696, *aff'd*, No. 22-1426 (2d Cir. Sept. 17, 2024). Judgment was entered on her convictions for conspiracy to transport minors (18 U.S.C. § 371), transportation of a minor (18 U.S.C. § 2423(a)), and sex trafficking of a minor (18 U.S.C. § 1591(a), (b)(2)); judgment was not entered on the jury's guilty verdicts on the other two conspiracy counts (Counts One and Five, each charged under 18 U.S.C. § 371), as multiplicitous. Those convictions do not establish continuation of Robert Maxwell's asserted intelligence operation.

The third strand is Whitney Webb's assertion that Christine Maxwell, Ghislaine's sister, co-founded Chiliad Inc., which none of the company, trade or federal records cited here states and this appendix has not verified. Chiliad claimed in its own press release that its technology was deployed across the FBI, while contemporaneous trade reporting identified Chiliad as a vendor for the FBI counterterrorism database; federal award records document nine FBI awards; and an Air Force Small Business Innovation Research (SBIR) award record carries the company's own abstract describing an FBI enterprise deployment. Those records do not verify that a specific Chiliad contract built the warehouse, and this appendix has not verified Webb's claims about Alan C. Wade's founding role and CIA post.[^a-chiliad-records]

Family association and a criminal conviction do not establish operational inheritance. This criterion does not establish continuity or service direction.


### D.3 Conclusion

**Table D.3. Bounded results by criterion.**

| Criterion | Bounded result |
|---|---|
| 1 | Multiple contact arrangements documented; meeting content and tasking unknown |
| 2 | A request in a private-dispute counsel thread; service tasking not established |
| 3 | Contacts with current and former officials documented; one request to a former Russian deputy minister, who undertook an inquiry, with outcome and capacity unknown; intelligence-operational relationships not established |
| 4 | Repeated correspondence and transactions over years; operational duration not established |
| 5 | Credentialed analyst's assessment documented; source genealogy limits independence |
| 6 | No formal service-status documentation established by the reviewed record |
| 7 | Private financing structure documented; service financing not established |
| 8 | Offers, contact permission, and strategic discussion; no executed service operation established |
| 9 | Cited third-party conduct does not establish subject tradecraft; possession of a passport in another name documented, with competing explanations; Epstein's use of it and its purpose unresolved |
| 10 | No independent foreign-government acknowledgment established |
| 11 | Family and business associations; operational continuity not established |

The aggregate numeric/WEP assessment is withdrawn. Confidence is strongest in the narrow documentary predicates, namely what identified messages say and what arrangements they record, and substantially weaker concerning execution, purpose, and service direction. That distinction cannot be converted into an aggregate probability from the present checklist. Repeated carriers and derivative commentary do not create independent corroboration.

The Schindler interpretation remains an attributed hypothesis, with the codename-to-group bridge unproved by the older primary reporting. The September 23 exact-query negative is bounded by its query and index. The contact and financial records do not impute wrongdoing to the named counterparties, and the records do not establish that the enterprise was an intelligence operation. The question is therefore one of two grounds for including EFTA, stated as unresolved. The other ground, executive custody and disclosure duties, and the paper's EFTA measurements stand whatever the answer.

- [Soror FV](https://sororfv.substack.com): Its public article pointed to the passport scan, the 1987 endorsement, the Austrian answers and custody records; every fact the paper states rests on the primaries.

Court record services:

- [CourtListener](https://www.courtlistener.com/): Its RECAP dockets gave access to the Black, Committee on the Judiciary and Phang filings the paper cites; the court records are the authority.

[^tt-committee-civil-motion]: House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform, markup of January 21, 2026. Amendment offered by Rep. Lee to the amendment in the nature of a substitute to the committee report recommending that the House find former President William J. Clinton in contempt, https://docs.house.gov/meetings/GO/GO00/20260121/118877/BILLS-119-WJCContemptReport-L000602-Amdt-3.pdf: it would have struck the report and substituted a resolution that the Committee "shall initiate judicial proceedings in any Federal court of competent jurisdiction" to seek declaratory judgments affirming the Attorney General's duty to comply with the August 5, 2025 subpoena, with the House Office of General Counsel representing the Committee "at the authorization of the Speaker," who "shall consult with the Bipartisan Legal Advisory Group." H. Rept. 119-468, Roll Call Votes, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CRPT-119hrpt468/html/CRPT-119hrpt468-pt1.htm: the Lee amendment failed 19–24 (Rollcall No. 1); an amendment by Rep. Min to authorize a declaratory-judgment suit against Clinton instead failed 19–25 (Rollcall No. 2); the report was ordered reported 34–8, with 2 voting present (Rollcall No. 3). The committee's roll-call sheet for the Lee amendment (PDF p. 22 of https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CRPT-119hrpt468/pdf/CRPT-119hrpt468-pt1.pdf) records every aye as a Democrat and every no as a Republican. Meeting transcript, https://docs.house.gov/meetings/GO/GO00/20260121/118877/HMKP-119-GO00-Transcript-20260121.pdf, printed pp. 48–49 (Lee describes the amendment as holding the Attorney General "in civil contempt") and 70 (vote announced, 19 to 24). These establish the proposal and outcome, not the truth of the underlying noncompliance allegation.
[^intro1]: Hughes-Ryan Amendment, Foreign Assistance Act of 1974, Pub. L. No. 93-559, § 32, 88 Stat. 1795, 1804 (adding § 662 to the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961), codified at 22 U.S.C. § 2422 (repealed 1991, reenacted at 50 U.S.C. §§ 3091–3093). Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Act of 1978 (FISA), Pub. L. No. 95-511. Intelligence Oversight Act of 1980, Pub. L. No. 96-450. Inspector General Act of 1978, Pub. L. No. 95-452. S. Res. 400, 94th Cong. (1976) (establishing the Senate Select Committee on Intelligence (SSCI)); H. Res. 658, 95th Cong. (1977) (establishing the House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence (HPSCI)). Privacy and Civil Liberties Oversight Board (PCLOB), 42 U.S.C. § 2000ee (Pub. L. No. 108-458, § 1061 (2004), as amended by Pub. L. No. 110-53, § 801(a) (2007)). USA FREEDOM Act of 2015, Pub. L. No. 114-23. National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2016, Pub. L. No. 114-92, § 1045 (codifying the McCain-Feinstein anti-torture provisions).
