No. 113-288 (2014), Foreword, p. 1 (the April 3, 2014 vote to send the Findings and Conclusions and the Executive Summary to the President for declassification; the full study "totals more than 6,700 pages, remains classified"). Sen. Dianne Feinstein, "CIA Detention and Interrogation Report," 160 *Cong. Rec.* S1487–S1491 (daily ed. March 11, 2014) (the CIA's search of the committee's computer network, and the acting general counsel's "crimes report with the Department of Justice concerning the committee staff's actions").

[^intro3]: Epstein Files Transparency Act, Pub. L. No. 119-38, 139 Stat. 656 (2025).

[^intro4]: Table 4 specifies eight process indicators: demand posture, selective disclosure, justification proliferation, delayed compliance, executive self-definition, majority declines an available remedy, remedy attempted and defeated outside Congress, and informal negotiation. Exogenous rupture and limited individual accountability are brackets, recorded but not counted among the process indicators, so Tables 4 and 7 each have ten rows.

[^intro-scope]: Two works on secrecy that bear on this scope choice are not engaged in this edition: Rahul Sagar, *Secrets and Leaks: The Dilemma of State Secrecy* (Princeton: Princeton University Press, 2013), and Michael P. Colaresi, *Democracy Declassified: The Secrecy Dilemma in National Security* (New York: Oxford University Press, 2014). The project does not hold them, and nothing is attributed to a work that has not been read; both are to be read before submission, Colaresi's comparative argument in particular because it bears on the limit stated in the text.

[^lit1]: For the classical-republican inheritance, see Polybius, *Histories* Book VI; John Adams, *A Defence of the Constitutions of Government of the United States of America* (1787–88). For the Federalist articulation, James Madison, *Federalist No. 51*; John Jay, *Federalist No. 64*; and Alexander Hamilton, *Federalist No. 70*, in *The Federalist Papers* (1788).

[^church-ii]: Frank Church, Letter of Transmittal, in Senate Select Committee to Study Governmental Operations with Respect to Intelligence Activities, *Final Report*, Book II: *Intelligence Activities and the Rights of Americans*, S. Rep. No. 94-755 (April 26, 1976), p. iii. The sentence omitted at the ellipsis reads: "Our experience as a nation has taught us that we must place our trust in laws, and not solely in men."

[^lit2]: The institutional history relied on here is L. Britt Snider, *The Agency and the Hill: CIA's Relationship with Congress, 1946–2004* (Washington: Center for the Study of Intelligence, 2008). General references, not held in full text for this edition: Frank J. Smist, Jr., *Congress Oversees the United States Intelligence Community, 1947–1994*, 2nd ed. (Knoxville: University of Tennessee Press, 1994); David M. Barrett, *The CIA and Congress: The Untold Story from Truman to Kennedy* (Lawrence: University Press of Kansas, 2005).

[^lit3]: Loch K. Johnson, "Accountability and America's Secret Foreign Policy: Keeping a Legislative Eye on the Central Intelligence Agency," *Foreign Policy Analysis* 1, no. 1 (2005): 99–120, at 111: "The repeated pattern of intelligence accountability since 1975 has been one of shock, intense oversight response, remedial legislation, then back to weak patrolling." The same page treats the scandals as fire alarms: "Over these 30 years, five major fire alarms have led to approximately six years of high-energy retrospective investigations by overseers qua fireman." Johnson's books *A Season of Inquiry Revisited* (2015) and *Spy Watching* (2018) are among the six books named in the limited-source paragraph; nothing is attributed to their text.

[^lit4]: Amy B. Zegart, "The Domestic Politics of Irrational Intelligence Oversight," *Political Science Quarterly* 126, no. 1 (2011): 1–25; Zegart, *Spies, Lies, and Algorithms: The History and Future of American Intelligence* (Princeton: Princeton University Press, 2022), ch. 8, pp. 198 (oversight driven by "information, incentives, and institutions," for reasons that have nothing to do with "which party was in power"), 212–15 (electoral incentives, "regardless of party or era"), and 216 (expertise). Zegart's *Eyes on Spies: Congress and the United States Intelligence Community* (Stanford: Hoover Institution Press, 2011) is held only as a summary reconstructed from secondary sources and is not relied on.

[^lit5]: Jennifer Kibbe, "Congressional Oversight of Intelligence: Is the Solution Part of the Problem?" *Intelligence and National Security* 25, no. 1 (2010): 24–49; Kathleen Clark, "Congressional Access to Intelligence Information: The Appearance of a Check on Executive Power," Legal Studies Research Paper No. 12-07-02 (July 2012), https://ssrn.com/abstract=2112081, p. 15 (the leaders' "silence came to be seen as endorsement"). See also Mary B. DeRosa, "Congressional Oversight of US Intelligence Activities," in *National Security Intelligence and Ethics*, ed. Seumas Miller, Mitt Regan, and Patrick F. Walsh (Abingdon: Routledge, 2022), 216–231.

[^lit6]: Mathew D. McCubbins and Thomas Schwartz, "Congressional Oversight Overlooked: Police Patrols versus Fire Alarms," *American Journal of Political Science* 28, no. 1 (1984): 165–179, at 166 (fire-alarm oversight as "a system of rules, procedures, and informal practices that enable individual citizens and organized interest groups to examine administrative decisions"). Zegart, "Domestic Politics," 7–8 and n. 30, observes that the authors never consider "a policy area like intelligence that is steeped in secrecy and has few interest groups on the scene."

[^lit7]: Michael J. Glennon, "National Security and Double Government," *Harvard National Security Journal* 5 (2014): 1–114, at 16–18 (the "Madisonian" institutions and the "Trumanite" network). The book of the same title (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2014) develops the argument and is not relied on here.

[^lit8]: Kathleen Clark, "Congress's Right to Counsel in Intelligence Oversight," *University of Illinois Law Review* 2011, no. 3: 915–60, at 935 (Part I.D, "Limited Notification as Inoculation"); Clark, "Congressional Access to Intelligence Information."

[^lit9]: Heidi Kitrosser, "Congressional Oversight of National Security Activities: Improving Information Funnels," *Cardozo Law Review* 29, no. 3 (2008): 1049, at 1050 (the "information funnel" approach, "funneling information only to discrete groups of people") and 1072 ("the near absence of public accountability for behavior in the information funneling process"). Kitrosser, *Reclaiming Accountability: Transparency, Executive Power, and the U.S. Constitution* (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2015), is further reading.

The project holds a summary of this book reconstructed from a review, the publisher's description, and academic reviews, and an Internet Archive lending-library copy protected by Adobe digital-rights management, which the project's tools cannot read and which it will not try to unlock. The rival stated in the text is this paper's formulation, and no argument or page is attributed to the book.

[^lit11]: Alfred Cumming, *Congress as a Consumer of Intelligence Information*, Congressional Research Service Report R40136, January 28, 2010, summary (the President "has the authority to restrict the flow of intelligence information to Congress") and pp. 1–4 (including the statutory "Gang of Eight" notifications, p. 4), https://www.everycrsreport.com/files/20100128_R40136_8fa60d28782e3e5beb9c290da190c2578ee2c81f.pdf; Clark, "Congressional Access to Intelligence Information," p. 15 (restricted briefings and silence "seen as endorsement").

[^lit-intel-secondary]: Kathryn Olmsted, "Reclaiming Executive Power: The Ford Administration's Response to the Intelligence Investigations," *Presidential Studies Quarterly* 26, no. 3 (1996): 725–737 (the executive's response to the 1975–76 Church and Pike investigations); James T. Currie, "Iran-Contra and Congressional Oversight of the CIA," *International Journal of Intelligence and CounterIntelligence* 11, no. 2 (1998): 185–210. Olmsted's book *Challenging the Secret Government: The Post-Watergate Investigations of the CIA and FBI* (Chapel Hill: University of North Carolina Press, 1996) covers the same investigations and is further reading.

[^tf2]: McCubbins and Schwartz consider the objection that fire-alarm oversight is biased and reply that "if the point is merely that fire-alarm oversight can be biased in various ways, then the same is true of police-patrol oversight" (McCubbins and Schwartz, "Congressional Oversight Overlooked," 172). The extension to classified domains is Zegart's ("Domestic Politics," 7–8 and n. 30); this paper applies it.

[^tf3]: Lawrence E. Walsh, *Final Report of the Independent Counsel for Iran/Contra Matters*, vol. 1 (Washington: U.S. Government Printing Office, 1993), Executive Summary p. xxi.

[^efta-intel-link]: The question is a reason for selecting the case, not a finding of service direction or a coded condition: it adds no positive code for Scope #2 in Table 2. A link, if present, may implicate national-security interests, but that possibility alone supplies no class (E) withholding ground: class (E) reaches only information that is properly classified (Table 8), and the Act requires the Attorney General to declassify such information to the maximum extent possible (§ 2(c)(3)). Reports of it predate the dispute over the Act: Vicky Ward's July 2019 column and the October 2020 FD-1023. Appendix D gives each item of evidence with its limits, among them Epstein's 2015 request to a former Russian deputy minister, who undertook an inquiry; his instruction that investigators "use russian contacts"; his financial partnership with Ehud Barak; and John R. Schindler's assessment. The custody ground and the measurements in this section do not depend on the answer.

[^tf4]: Domains with strong external accountability structures (organized professional communities; mandatory disclosure frameworks with structurally independent enforcement; domains in which the executive does not control operational architecture) fall outside our framework's primary scope. The paper does not test our framework against these domains; cross-domain extension is identified as a future-research direction.

[^imp-trust-asymmetry]: On non-uniform scandal effects on political trust: Marc Hetherington and Jason Husser, "How Trust Matters: The Changing Political Relevance of Political Trust," *American Journal of Political Science* 56, no. 2 (2012): 312–325 (post-9/11 pivot to the foreign-policy domain); Virginia Chanley, Thomas Rudolph, and Wendy Rahn, "The Origins and Consequences of Public Trust in Government," *Public Opinion Quarterly* 64, no. 3 (2000): 239–256 (branch-asymmetric scandal effects); Jack Citrin and Donald Green, "Presidential Leadership and the Resurgence of Trust in Government," *British Journal of Political Science* 16, no. 4 (1986): 431–453 (Reagan-era "Teflon"); and the post-Snowden Pew Research Center surveys and their retrospective review: Mary Madden, "Public Perceptions of Privacy and Security in the Post-Snowden Era," Pew Research Center, November 12, 2014, https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/2014/11/12/public-privacy-perceptions/; Lee Rainie and Mary Madden, "Americans' Privacy Strategies Post-Snowden," Pew Research Center, March 16, 2015, https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/2015/03/16/americans-privacy-strategies-post-snowden/; and Pew Research Center, "How Americans Have Viewed Government Surveillance and Privacy since Snowden Leaks," June 4, 2018, https://www.pewresearch.org/short-reads/2018/06/04/how-americans-have-viewed-government-surveillance-and-privacy-since-snowden-leaks/. Synthesis (the author's inference, not a finding of the cited works): scandal revelations are among several influences on trust, alongside economic and sociocultural drivers; whether their effect differs by frame (broad-victim privacy framing versus out-group national-security framing) is untested here.


[^tf-falsifiability]: Table 6 is the single statement of the observations that would count against the description. No cutoff is applied: the indicators were developed with the historical cases and are not independently validated. Marker percentages and the number of pre-enactment guidance documents are not compliance tests. Concrete access or policy gains, including negotiated concessions and judicial compulsion, count against complete absorption. These rules were written in the September 2026 revisions and were not preregistered.

[^tf-formalism]: The "bargaining" label proposes a self-reinforcing institutional pattern, not a formal non-cooperative game of the kind in Ariel Rubinstein, "Perfect Equilibrium in a Bargaining Model," *Econometrica* 50, no. 1 (1982): 97–109, or Vincent P. Crawford and Joel Sobel, "Strategic Information Transmission," *Econometrica* 50, no. 6 (1982): 1431–51. It describes an institutional pattern of the kind Knight and Pierson analyze (works cited at the end of this note): the structural conditions tend to reproduce the configuration, a proposition these exploratory cases illustrate but do not test, and not a claim that each interaction reaches a game-theoretic solution. The case record also does not supply the separate payoffs (executive reputational cost, congressional electoral cost, public-trust cost) a formal model would need. A formal model would yield predictions about how the pattern shifts as each payoff changes; that is left to future work. See Jack Knight, *Institutions and Social Conflict* (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1992); Paul Pierson, *Politics in Time* (Princeton: Princeton University Press, 2004).

[^meth1]: The five comparison dimensions are: (a) institutional configuration (committee-level or individual-member-level access); (b) the course of the bargain (bargaining and defiance in both chambers in 1975–76; investigation after exposure in 1986–91; constraint built into the program's access rules in 2001–15; investigation under counter-pressure against the committee itself in 2002–14; adjudication in the 1973–74 contrast); (c) party configuration (which party held each chamber and the presidency); (d) statutory framework; (e) documented outcome.

[^meth-design]: James Mahoney and Gary Goertz, "A Tale of Two Cultures: Contrasting Quantitative and Qualitative Research," *Political Analysis* 14, no. 3 (2006): 227–249. The comparison here is exploratory and claims neither a most-similar nor a most-different systems design; on the limits of those designs, see Adam Przeworski and Henry Teune, *The Logic of Comparative Social Inquiry* (New York: Wiley-Interscience, 1970), and Carsten Anckar, "On the Applicability of the Most Similar Systems Design and the Most Different Systems Design in Comparative Research," *International Journal of Social Research Methodology* 11, no. 5 (2008): 389–401.

[^meth2]: Three grounds support EFTA selection: enactment by a 427–1 House vote and Senate unanimous consent, enumerated redaction categories, and an empirically characterizable multi-million-page production. The searchable index is used for retrieval rather than as the population denominator; the manuscript's manifest population is defined separately. Enactment and statutory text sharpen rival tests but do not logically rule out every partisan or discretionary-withholding explanation.

[^meth-pt-collier]: David Collier, "Understanding Process Tracing," PS: Political Science & Politics 44, no. 4 (2011): 823–830. Used for the distinction between a documented observation and its discriminating force among explanations. This paper does not assign decisive evidentiary-test labels to the training or marker counts.

[^meth3]: Primary-source bases. Pike and Church, 1975–76: House Select Committee on Intelligence hearings, *U.S. Intelligence Agencies and Activities*, Parts 1–6 (1975–76); Senate Select Committee, *Final Report*, Books I–VI (1976), and hearings, vols. 1–7 (1975); the committee report as printed in the *Village Voice*, February 16, 1976; Snider, *Agency and the Hill*. Iran-Contra, 1986–91: Walsh, *Final Report*, vol. 1 (1993); *Report of the Congressional Committees Investigating the Iran-Contra Affair*, H. Rept. 100-433, S. Rept. 100-216 (1987); the Cooper opinion (December 17, 1986); NSDD-286 (October 15, 1987); the Bush–Boren letter (October 30, 1989). NSA bulk collection, 2001–15: the NSA Inspector General's working draft (2009); the Wyden–Udall letters of 2011 and 2012; the Department of Justice white paper (2013); the Privacy and Civil Liberties Oversight Board report (2014); the USA FREEDOM Act (2015); the appellate decisions. CIA Interrogation Program, 2002–14: the SSCI study's Foreword, Findings and Conclusions, and Executive Summary (released December 9, 2014); Senator Feinstein's floor statement of March 11, 2014. Watergate tapes, 1973–74: the judicial decisions, the President's statements, and the Senate committee's final report cited in the Watergate note to the framework.


[^hf1]: Seymour M. Hersh, "Huge C.I.A. Operation Reported in U.S. Against Antiwar Forces, Other Dissidents in Nixon Years," *New York Times*, December 22, 1974, p. 1. Hersh's report dealt with many of the subjects disclosed in the Schlesinger-ordered "Family Jewels" internal CIA self-audit and first revealed the compilation's existence; the CIA released the compilation on June 26, 2007. Snider, *Agency and the Hill*, 33, 234 n.40. Victor Marchetti and John D. Marks, *The CIA and the Cult of Intelligence* (New York: Knopf, 1974). For the Senate authorization, S. Res. 21, 94th Cong. (1975), agreed to January 27, 1975, by a vote of 82 to 4 (Snider, *Agency and the Hill*, 34; the resolution is printed as agreed to in Senate Select Committee to Study Governmental Operations with Respect to Intelligence Activities, *Rules of Procedure* (1975), appendix). For the House, the committee was established in February 1975 and reconstituted on July 17, 1975 after initial controversy over the Lucien Nedzi chairmanship (Snider, *Agency and the Hill*, 37–38), under H. Res. 591, 94th Cong. (see 122 Cong. Rec. 1632 (1976), Rep. Young: "House Resolution 591, in July 1975"; the resolution's own text is not held).

[^hf2]: On January 29, 1976 the House agreed to the committee amendment to H. Res. 982 by 246 to 124, with 62 not voting, and then to the resolution as amended (122 Cong. Rec. 1641, Roll No. 29). The amendment provides that the committee "shall not release any report containing materials, information, data, or subjects that presently bear security classification, unless and until such reports are published with appropriate security markings and distributed only to persons authorized to receive such classified information, or until the report has been certified by the President as not containing information which would adversely affect the intelligence activities of the CIA in foreign countries or the intelligence activities in foreign countries of any other department or agency of the federal government" (id. at 1632). The party totals are Snider's, not printed by the *Record*: "the House voted 246-124, with 127 Democrats joining 119 Republicans, to suppress the publication of the report" (*Agency and the Hill*, 39). The committee's later proceedings apply the condition: Representative McClory's motion asked that the report be "examined by a representative of the President of the United States for purposes of indicating classified information as materials required to be deleted from the report prior to publication in accordance with House Resolution 982": *U.S. Intelligence Agencies and Activities: Committee Proceedings*, Part 6 (1976), p. 2138. The findings nonetheless appeared through Daniel Schorr's leak to the *Village Voice*, which published them on February 16, 1976 (Snider, 39), and in book form as *CIA: The Pike Report* (Nottingham: Spokesman Books, 1977).

[^hf-church-cluster]: Snider, *Agency and the Hill*, 51 (S. Res. 400 passed May 19, 1976), 60 (Hughes-Ryan reporting reduced "from eight to two"); Intelligence Oversight Act of 1980, Pub. L. No. 96-450.

[^hf-schorr-leak]: House Committee on Standards of Official Conduct, *Report on Investigation Pursuant to H. Res. 1042 Concerning Unauthorized Publication of the Report of the Select Committee on Intelligence*, H. Rept. 94-1754 (Oct. 1, 1976), pp. 2, 42, recording Schorr's testimony that he gave his copy of the report to Clay Felker, who published it in the *Village Voice*.

[^hf3]: Senate Select Committee to Study Governmental Operations with Respect to Intelligence Activities, *Final Report*, S. Rep. No. 94-755, Book I: *Foreign and Military Intelligence* and Book II: *Intelligence Activities and the Rights of Americans* (both dated April 26, 1976), with Books III–VI (supplementary detailed staff reports, the report on the investigation of the Kennedy assassination, and supplementary reports on intelligence activities); *Intelligence Activities, Senate Resolution 21: Hearings Before the Select Committee*, 94th Cong., 1st Sess., vols. 1–7 (1975); *Alleged Assassination Plots Involving Foreign Leaders: An Interim Report* (November 20, 1975); Exec. Order No. 11905, *United States Foreign Intelligence Activities*, 41 Fed. Reg. 7703 (Feb. 19, 1976). The Senate Historical Office gives April 29, 1976 as the date the report was issued (71 days after the order); this paper uses the April 26, 1976 date printed on Books I and II (68 days).

[^hf5]: For the procedural confrontation, including the Kissinger contempt resolutions of November 14, 1975 and Antonin Scalia's Office of Legal Counsel appearance of November 20, 1975, see *U.S. Intelligence Agencies and Activities: Committee Proceedings, Hearings Before the Select Committee on Intelligence, House of Representatives, 94th Congress, 1st Sess., Part 4* (1975), 1383 ff. (meeting of November 14: resolutions of contempt) and 1413 ff. (meeting of November 20; Scalia prepared statement at 1456, stating at 1457 that the seven subpoenas were served on Friday, November 7 and returnable on Tuesday, November 11). For the Rogovin-Field exchange immediately preceding the suppression vote, see *U.S. Intelligence Agencies and Activities: Committee Proceedings, Hearings Before the Select Committee on Intelligence, House of Representatives, 94th Congress, 2d Sess., Part 6* (1976), 2127-2130. For the Rockefeller Commission as the executive-side parallel investigation, see *Report to the President by the Commission on CIA Activities Within the United States* (June 6, 1975).

[^hf6]: The Boland Amendment series is conventionally grouped in three phases: Boland I (Pub. L. No. 97-377, § 793, fiscal 1983 appropriations, effective December 21, 1982), Boland II (Pub. L. No. 98-473, § 8066, continuing appropriations for fiscal 1985, October 12, 1984), and Boland III (Intelligence Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 1986, Pub. L. No. 99-169, § 105, and fiscal 1986 continuing appropriations, Pub. L. No. 99-190, § 8050). *Al-Shira'*, November 3, 1986, pp. 24, 26, as translated by the Foreign Broadcast Information Service (relaying what "Hashemi's supporters" relate, "only to present the other side objectively": a secret visit to Tehran by "A secret U.S. envoy, Robert McFarlane," and "the sale of spare parts to Iran for its U.S.-made aircraft, tanks, radar, and other weapons"); *Report of the Congressional Committees Investigating the Iran-Contra Affair*, Preface, p. xv (the *Ash-Shiraa* report of November 3, 1986); Executive Summary, p. 10 (the Attorney General's November 25, 1986 press conference); and p. 399 ("The Administration has asserted that Boland II did not apply to the NSC staff"; the committees found those "post hoc arguments ... not persuasive"). Separate Senate and House select committees were established by S. Res. 23 on January 6 and H. Res. 12 on January 7, 1987; they conducted joint hearings and issued a joint report.

[^hf-hasenfus]: On the Nicaragua-side rupture that preceded *Ash-Shiraa*: a C-123 cargo plane carrying military supplies to the Contras was shot down over southern Nicaragua on October 5, 1986, and its captured surviving crew member, Eugene Hasenfus, publicly stated that the flight was a CIA-assisted weapons-resupply operation. Doyle McManus, "Downed Plane Not Ours, Shultz Says," *Los Angeles Times*, October 8, 1986 (the crash, the C-123 and the U.S. denials); Julia Preston, "Captured American Says CIA Ran Supply Missions," *Washington Post*, October 10, 1986, p. A1, Central Intelligence Agency copy (Hasenfus "said today he has been part of an extensive air resupply operation for the Nicaraguan rebels run from a Salvadoran Air Force base by CIA employes," at a press conference called by the Nicaraguan military, in a "wooden statement that seemed to have been rehearsed"; Abrams said there was "no relation at all" between the CIA and those aiding the resupply); "Shot Down on 10th Flight, Captured American Says: Asserts CIA Assisted in Operation," Associated Press / *Los Angeles Times*, October 9, 1986 (held in part). He spoke in Sandinista custody. The Hasenfus disclosure and the *Ash-Shiraa* report a month later were the two external ruptures that exposed the Contra and Iran halves of the operation and spurred the congressional inquiry.

[^hf7]: *United States v. North*, 910 F.2d 843 (D.C. Cir. 1990) (convictions vacated and remanded for a *Kastigar* hearing); *United States v. Poindexter*, 951 F.2d 369, 379 (D.C. Cir. 1991) (convictions reversed; the majority also held that the term "corruptly" in 18 U.S.C. § 1505 was "too vague to provide constitutionally adequate notice that it prohibits lying to the Congress"). Both turned on the rule that the prosecution must prove its trial evidence independent of the immunized testimony, *Kastigar v. United States*, 406 U.S. 441 (1972). Judge Gesell dismissed the North case on September 16, 1991, on the Independent Counsel's motion: Walsh, *Final Report*, vol. 1, p. xxiv.

[^hf8]: Walsh, *Final Report*, vol. 1, "Summary of Prosecutions," printed pp. xxiii–xxv: fourteen charged, eleven convicted, two convictions overturned; two defendants pardoned before trial. The December 24, 1992 pardons covered convicted defendants McFarlane, Abrams, Fiers and George, and pretrial defendants Weinberger and Clarridge. Pardons do not erase the historical pleas, convictions, or report findings. On the appointment, p. xiii: after Attorney General Meese sought an independent counsel, the court's Special Division appointed Walsh on December 19, 1986.

[^hf9]: Charles J. Cooper, "The President's Compliance with the 'Timely Notification' Requirement of Section 501(b) of the National Security Act," 10 Op. O.L.C. 159 (1986) (memorandum opinion for the Attorney General, December 17, 1986); Ronald Reagan, National Security Decision Directive 286, "Approval and Review of Special Activities," October 15, 1987, §§ II.A.1 ("No Finding may retroactively authorize or sanction a special activity"), II.G.1, and II.G.4 (where notice is delayed, "Unless the President otherwise directs, not later than two working days after the President signs a Finding or associated MON, if any, the Intelligence Committees shall be notified") (declassified extract, Ronald Reagan Presidential Library, https://www.reaganlibrary.gov/public/archives/reference/scanned-nsdds/nsdd286.pdf); Minority Report, in *Report of the Congressional Committees Investigating the Iran-Contra Affair*, 431–633; President George H. W. Bush to Senator David L. Boren, Chairman, Senate Select Committee on Intelligence, October 30, 1989 (copy in the holdings of the National Security Archive, George Washington University); Office of the Vice President, "Vice President's Remarks to the Traveling Press," Air Force Two, en route to Muscat, Oman, December 20, 2005, https://georgewbush-whitehouse.archives.gov/news/releases/2005/12/20051220-9.html. Zivotofsky v. Kerry, 576 U.S. 1, 10–16, 20–22 (2015), declined the broad sole-organ claim attributed to Curtiss-Wright; recognition exclusivity rests on the Reception Clause and constitutional structure. It did not narrow a general sole-organ doctrine to a recognition exception. The continuity argument is structural and bounded by the three-document citation audit, not proof that the minority report caused later OLC doctrine.

[^hf9-olc-audit]: The directly searchable audit universe is three primary DOJ texts: John Yoo, The President's Constitutional Authority to Conduct Military Operations Against Terrorists and Nations Supporting Them, September 25, 2001 (SHA256 f7270cafc41315ca30a27e4efe75714063f276482412719cc025fe94ed3cce59); Yoo and Robert Delahunty, Authority for Use of Military Force To Combat Terrorist Activities Within the United States, October 23, 2001 (6506ab6baf596c3f2f43f74876db662753d082003d3f86cac161ba0c73cce700); Yoo, Applicability of 18 U.S.C. § 4001(a) to Military Detention of United States Citizens, June 27, 2002 (7289c7561653c94cde2ef81001a37ed65945d2222244cfdfef3f98e41d89703b). Hashes identify the held text extractions, available on request. Whole-text case-insensitive searches for Cooper, Boren, NSDD[- ]286, Cheney, Iran[- ]Contra, minority report/views, 100-433 and 100-216 were checked in context. The September 25 Cheney hit concerns 1991 Gulf War remarks and its Iran-Contra hit cites Koh; neither is a citation to the notification chain. No chain citation was found in this bounded universe. It does not cover other opinions, redacted text, unsearchable material, or indirect borrowing.

[^hf10]: For the architectural framework: S. Res. 400, 94th Cong. (1976), agreed to May 19, 1976, by a vote of 72 to 22 (Snider, *Agency and the Hill*, 51); and Espionage Act criminal liability under 18 U.S.C. §§ 793–798.

[^hf10-nsa-oig]: NSA Office of the Inspector General, *ST-09-0002 Working Draft: Report on the President's Surveillance Program*, March 24, 2009, published by *The Guardian* on June 27, 2013: p. 24 (for members of Congress, the court, providers' outside counsel, and the NSA Inspector General, "General Hayden had to obtain approval from the White House"); p. 25 ("Between 25 October 2001 and 17 January 2007," Hayden "conducted over 49 briefings to members of Congress or their staff," and "during the many PSP-related briefings he gave to members of Congress, no one ever said that NSA should stop what it was doing"). The roster of the first briefings is reconstructed from pp. 23–25; Hayden's recollection is his inference, not a finding of congressional consent.

[^hf11]: Sen. Ron Wyden and Sen. Mark Udall to Attorney General Eric Holder, September 21, 2011, p. 1 (the government "is relying on secret interpretations of surveillance authorities"); Wyden and Udall to Holder, March 15, 2012, p. 2 (the opinions are "so highly classified that most members of Congress do not have any staff who are cleared to read them"; the Department of Justice and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence wrote to Senators Rockefeller and Wyden in August 2009 to announce a process for releasing significant opinions; "Not a single redacted opinion has been released"). The August 2009 letter itself was not opened for this edition.

[^hf12]: SSCI, Worldwide Threats hearing, March 12, 2013, printed p. 66 (Clapper exchange). House Judiciary Committee, *United States Department of Justice*, Serial No. 113-76, April 8, 2014, printed pp. 14–15 (PDF pp. 18–19), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-113hhrg87497/pdf/CHRG-113hhrg87497.pdf: Holder acknowledges Sensenbrenner's letter, says member material is being reviewed, and declines to confirm an investigation. This does not establish a formal SSCI referral, a confirmed criminal investigation, or a charge. The general five-year limitation period for non-capital federal offences is 18 U.S.C. § 3282(a); for testimony given on March 12, 2013 it ran in March 2018, and the record reviewed here contains no charge.

[^hf13]: Leslie Cauley, "NSA has massive database of Americans' phone calls," *USA Today*, posted May 10, 2006, updated May 11, 2006, https://usatoday30.usatoday.com/news/washington/2006-05-10-nsa_x.htm; Glenn Greenwald, "NSA Collecting Phone Records of Millions of Verizon Customers Daily," *The Guardian*, June 5, 2013. Privacy and Civil Liberties Oversight Board, *Report on the Telephone Records Program Conducted under Section 215 of the USA PATRIOT Act and on the Operations of the Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Court*, January 23, 2014, pp. 9 (presidential authorizations from late 2001 through early 2006; the first Section 215 order in May 2006; the June 5, 2013 *Guardian* article; the August 29, 2013 opinion), 96–98 (the 2010 and 2011 extensions; the order and application given to the intelligence and judiciary committees of both houses; other members "prohibited from reading" them; the briefing paper not made available to all House members in 2011), and 193 (later official releases). Administration White Paper, *Bulk Collection of Telephony Metadata Under Section 215 of the USA PATRIOT Act*, August 9, 2013, p. 18 ("By December 2008, all four committees had received the initial application and primary order"). USA FREEDOM Act of 2015, Pub. L. No. 114-23, §§ 103 (prohibition on bulk collection of tangible things) and 109(a) (effective "180 days after the date of the enactment"); House Roll Call 224, 114th Cong., 1st Sess. (May 13, 2015), H.R. 2048 passed 338–88, with Republicans 196–47. The post-2013 litigation is described in the note on the appellate decisions.

[^hf-nyt-2005]: James Risen and Eric Lichtblau, "Bush Lets U.S. Spy on Callers Without Courts," *New York Times*, December 16, 2005 (under a presidential order, the agency "has monitored the international telephone calls and international e-mail messages of hundreds, perhaps thousands, of people inside the United States without warrants"); the report did not describe the collection of call records, which *USA Today* reported in May 2006. NSA Inspector General, *Working Draft*, p. 37 ("In response to a New York Times 'warrantless wiretapping' story published in December 2005, General Alexander briefed all FISC members on the PSP on 9 January 2006"). FISA Amendments Act of 2008, Pub. L. No. 110-261, § 201, 122 Stat. 2436, 2467–69 (July 10, 2008) (new FISA § 802(a): "a civil action may not lie or be maintained in a Federal or State court against any person for providing assistance to an element of the intelligence community, and shall be promptly dismissed, if the Attorney General certifies," among other grounds, assistance with an activity "authorized by the President during the period beginning on September 11, 2001, and ending on January 17, 2007").

[^hf14]: SSCI, *Committee Study*, S. Rep. No. 113-288: Foreword, pp. 1 (the April 3, 2014 vote; the full study "totals more than 6,700 pages, remains classified"), 4 (the Department of Justice investigation "expanded by Attorney General Holder in August 2009," which kept CIA employees and contractors from appearing before committee staff), and 5 (staff "reviewed the more than six million pages of CIA materials" from early 2009 to late 2012); Findings and Conclusions, pp. 5–6 (the CIA "resisted efforts by Vice Chairman John D. Rockefeller IV, to investigate the program, including by refusing in 2006 to provide requested documents to the full Committee") and 6 (the CIA restricted access beyond the chairman and vice chairman "until September 6, 2006, the day the president publicly acknowledged the program, by which time 117 of the 119 known detainees had already entered CIA custody"); Executive Summary, p. 8 (review begun December 11, 2007 after a briefing that day; Terms of Reference approved March 5, 2009 "in a vote of 14 to 1"; a report "exceeding 6,700 pages, including approximately 38,000 footnotes"; approval on December 13, 2012 "by a bipartisan vote of 9-6"), p. 9 n. (approximately 9,400 CIA documents withheld by the White House pending an executive-privilege determination, requested in writing on January 3, May 22, and December 19, 2013), and p. 225 ("The White House did not respond to the requests"). The Executive Summary runs to 499 pages; with the Foreword and the Findings and Conclusions the released document is 525 pages. It was released December 9, 2014. *American Civil Liberties Union v. Central Intelligence Agency*, 823 F.3d 655 (D.C. Cir. 2016), affirmed dismissal of a Freedom of Information Act suit for the full report on the ground that it remained a congressional record.

[^hf-cia-tapes]: The external rupture for the CIA Interrogation Program inquiry: Mark Mazzetti, "C.I.A. Destroyed 2 Tapes Showing Interrogations," *New York Times*, December 7, 2007 (dateline Washington, Dec. 6), revealing that the CIA had destroyed videotapes of detainee interrogations, a destruction ordered in 2005 by Jose A. Rodriguez Jr., who headed the clandestine service throughout that year, as Deputy Director of Operations until October 2005 and then as director of the National Clandestine Service (Declaration of Jose Rodriguez, *Salim v. Mitchell*, No. 2:15-cv-00286 (E.D. Wash.), Dkt. 175, ¶¶ 6–7, https://www.aclu.org/sites/default/files/field_document/175._declarationj_of_jose_rodriguez.pdf; the article calls him head of the Directorate of Operations). The specific count of ninety-two destroyed tapes did not appear in the 2007 reporting; it first surfaced publicly in a Department of Justice litigation disclosure, the government's March 2, 2009 letter to the court ("Ninety-two videotapes were destroyed"), *ACLU v. Department of Defense*, No. 04 Civ. 4151 (AKH) (S.D.N.Y.), endorsed and docketed March 3, 2009, Dkt. 330, in the American Civil Liberties Union's Freedom of Information Act suit seeking records of the treatment of prisoners in U.S. custody abroad; see American Civil Liberties Union, "CIA Destroyed 92 Interrogation Tapes," press release, March 2, 2009. The revelation and the ensuing DOJ preliminary review preceded the Senate Intelligence Committee's March 5, 2009 authorization of its detention-and-interrogation study.

[^hf15]: Feinstein, 160 *Cong. Rec.* S1487–S1491 (documents that "were no longer accessible" in May 2010; the January 2014 search of the committee's network, of which she was informed on January 15, 2014; the Inspector General's referral of the CIA's activities to the Department of Justice and the acting general counsel's crimes report concerning committee staff; the June 2013 CIA response to the study). Carrie Johnson, "No Criminal Charges In Senate-CIA Spat, Justice Department Says," NPR, July 10, 2014.

[^hf16]: Department of Justice, "Statement of Attorney General Eric Holder on Closure of Investigation into the Interrogation of Certain Detainees," August 30, 2012 (the two remaining criminal investigations closed without charges). Office of Professional Responsibility, *Investigation into the Office of Legal Counsel's Memoranda Concerning Issues Relating to the Central Intelligence Agency's Use of "Enhanced Interrogation Techniques" on Suspected Terrorists*, July 29, 2009, released February 19, 2010 (professional-misconduct findings against John Yoo and Jay Bybee); Memorandum for the Attorney General from David Margolis, January 5, 2010 (declining to adopt those findings). This paper paraphrases rather than quotes both documents. Steven G. Bradbury, Memorandum for the Files, "Status of Certain OLC Opinions Issued in the Aftermath of the Terrorist Attacks of September 11, 2001," January 15, 2009, p. 4 (the "sweeping assertions" that the Commander in Chief authority "categorically precludes Congress from enacting any legislation concerning the detention, interrogation, prosecution" of enemy combatants are not OLC's position, while a law "constitutional in general may still raise serious constitutional issues if applied in particular circumstances"), https://www.justice.gov/sites/default/files/opa/legacy/2009/03/09/memostatusolcopinions01152009.pdf. National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2016, Pub. L. No. 114-92, § 1045.

[^hf-dta-mca]: Detainee Treatment Act of 2005, Pub. L. No. 109-148, div. A, title X, 119 Stat. 2739–40 (December 30, 2005), §§ 1003(a) ("No individual in the custody or under the physical control of the United States Government, regardless of nationality or physical location, shall be subject to cruel, inhuman, or degrading treatment or punishment") and 1004(a) (in a civil action or prosecution arising from authorized interrogation practices, a defense where the agent "did not know that the practices were unlawful and a person of ordinary sense and understanding would not know the practices were unlawful"). Military Commissions Act of 2006, Pub. L. No. 109-366, § 6(b), 120 Stat. 2600, 2633–35 (October 17, 2006) (replacing the War Crimes Act's offense for conduct "which constitutes a violation of common Article 3," added by the Foreign Operations, Export Financing, and Related Programs Appropriations Act, 1998, Pub. L. No. 105-118, § 583, 111 Stat. 2386, 2436 (November 26, 1997), with enumerated "grave breach" offenses; the amendments, with one exception for later conduct, "shall take effect as of November 26, 1997"). National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2016, Pub. L. No. 114-92, § 1045, is the sequel.

[^hf-dta-response]: SSCI, *Committee Study*, Findings and Conclusions, p. 16 ("In late 2005 and in 2006, the Detainee Treatment Act and then the U.S. Supreme Court decision in Hamdan v. Rumsfeld caused the CIA to again temporarily suspend the use of its enhanced interrogation techniques"), and Executive Summary, p. 164 and n. ("A classified legal opinion from OLC concluding that the use of the CIA's six enhanced interrogation techniques proposed for use on Rahim ... did not violate applicable laws was issued on July 20, 2007"; the opinion applied the War Crimes Act, the Detainee Treatment Act, and Common Article 3). *Hamdan v. Rumsfeld*, 548 U.S. 557 (2006).

[^hf-accountability]: The accountability outcomes for the four cases. Pike/Church: Richard Helms pleaded nolo contendere in 1977 to two misdemeanor counts under 2 U.S.C. § 192 of failing to testify fully before the Senate Foreign Relations Committee regarding CIA activity in Chile, and received a suspended sentence and a $2,000 fine; see Office of Legal Counsel, "Richard Helms' Eligibility Under 5 U.S.C. § 8314 to Receive an Annuity or Retired Pay," 1 Op. O.L.C. 252 (1977) (plea of nolo contendere to two counts under 2 U.S.C. § 192), and "Helms Fined $2,000, Term Suspended," *Washington Post*, November 5, 1977; W. Mark Felt and Edward S. Miller were convicted on November 6, 1980 of conspiracy to violate citizens' constitutional rights through 1972–1973 warrantless entries and fined by Judge William B. Bryant on December 15, 1980 (UPI, "Fines, no jail terms in FBI case," Dec. 15, 1980; see also the pretrial rulings in *United States v. Felt*, 502 F. Supp. 71 (D.D.C. 1980), and the indictment described in *United States v. Gray*, 502 F. Supp. 150 (D.D.C. 1980)), then pardoned while their appeal was pending, Ronald Reagan, "Statement on Granting Pardons to W. Mark Felt and Edward S. Miller," April 15, 1981. Iran-Contra: the *North* conviction was vacated and the *Poindexter* conviction reversed on use-immunity grounds, and McFarlane's guilty plea remained in the historical record after his December 24, 1992 pardon, as documented in the Iran-Contra case narrative and its sources above. NSA bulk collection: Holder acknowledged member requests concerning Clapper; no charge is established in the reviewed record, per the NSA case narrative above. CIA Interrogation Program: the Durham review closed with no charges, the OPR professional-misconduct finding was reduced by Margolis, with no discipline shown in the record reviewed, and both CIA-personnel and committee-staff conduct were referred in the computer-search dispute, per the CIA Interrogation Program case narrative above.

[^tt1]: Epstein Files Transparency Act, Pub. L. No. 119-38, 139 Stat. 656 (2025). The House passed H.R. 4405 on November 18, 2025 by 427–1 (House Roll Call 289, 119th Cong., 1st sess.), and the Senate passed it by unanimous consent (Congress.gov, H.R. 4405, All Actions: "Received in the Senate, read twice, considered, read the third time, and passed, under the order of 11/18/2025, without amendment by Unanimous Consent. (consideration: CR S8211)", that is, 171 Cong. Rec. S8211 (daily ed. Nov. 19, 2025)); President Trump signed it on November 19, 2025. Unanimous consent records the absence of objection, not a counted vote.

[^tt-fr-aug2026]: Department of Justice, “Report Under Public Law 119-38,” August 27, 2026, 91 FR 55356–55362, FR Doc. 2026-17533, signed August 21 by Associate Attorney General Stanley E. Woodward Jr. https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/08/27/2026-17533/report-under-public-law-119-38. The appendix republishes the Section 3 Report’s categories, redaction summary and privileges, without itemized per-redaction entries or Bates numbers. The September 16 order, ECF 43, pp. 17–18, discharged publication show cause while reserving content adequacy; this paper does not state a final judicial holding against the notice.

[^tt2]: Compliance status from DOJ EFTA Compliance Letters #1 (December 19, 2025), https://www.justice.gov/opa/media/1434851/dl, and #2 (January 30, 2026), https://www.justice.gov/opa/media/1426091/dl, with later dated developments noted below. Letter #2 (pp. 1–2) reports "more than 6 million pages... identified as potentially responsive" and "over 3 million responsive pages... nearly 3.5 million pages released" cumulatively; the 6 million is the identified pool, the 3.5 million the released total combined with prior releases (Letter #2 does not itemize them; Letter #1, p. 5, separately lists over 33,000 pages produced to Congress on August 22, 2025). Letter #1 (p. 6) reports that the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Southern District of New York produced 3.6 million records. The December 19, 2025 statutory deadline (§ 2(a)) was missed; the cited production sequence ran through January 30, 2026; February 14 was the report date; the § 2(c)(2) Federal Register per-redaction publication was absent at the May 27, 2026 check (federalregister.gov API: zero matches; DOJ had posted a Justice.gov "EFTA-FOIA Crosswalk" instead). DOJ’s August 27, 2026 notice, “Report Under Public Law 119-38” (FR Doc. 2026-17533), republished the Section 3 Report without record-level entries. The September 16 order, ECF 43, pp. 17–18, discharged publication show cause while reserving content adequacy; this paper does not state a final judicial holding against the notice.

[^tt3]: DOJ, Section 3 Report, February 14, 2026, pp. 2–3 ("Summary of Redactions Made and Legal Basis"), https://www.justice.gov/opa/media/1434856/dl. The authorities it cites are Federal Rule of Evidence 501; *Trammel v. United States*, 445 U.S. 40, 47 (1980); *FBI v. Fazaga*, 595 U.S. 344, 355 (2022); *Bassett v. United States*, 137 U.S. 496, 505–06 (1890); and *Oklahoma v. Castro-Huerta*, 597 U.S. 629, 642 (2022). In *Fazaga* the Court stated the clear-statement rule for the state-secrets privilege, applying it "[r]egardless of whether the state secrets privilege is rooted only in the common law (as respondents argue) or also in the Constitution (as the Government argues)" and citing the general presumption against abrogating common-law principles, *Norfolk Redevelopment and Housing Authority v. Chesapeake & Potomac Telephone Co. of Va.*, 464 U.S. 30, 35 (1983) (slip op. at 9), which is the strongest ground for extending the rule to ordinary privileges; the Act's answers are its express coverage of internal charging communications (§ 2(a)(7)) and its enumerated withholdings. The Court said its decision "addresses only the narrow question whether §1806(f) displaces the state secrets privilege" (slip op. at 13). The approximately 200,000-page privilege figure comes from the January 30 letter (p. 4) and is not a disjoint withholding total. *NLRB v. Sears, Roebuck & Co.*, 421 U.S. 132 (1975), *Hickman v. Taylor*, 329 U.S. 495 (1947), and *Upjohn Co. v. United States*, 449 U.S. 383 (1981), are the author's background on the privileges, not authorities DOJ cites. The August 27, 2026 Federal Register notice republished the Section 3 Report "pursuant to Section 2 of the Act," and DOJ "claims that this satisfies section 2(c)(2)" (*Phang*, ECF 43, p. 17).

[^nm-truth-interim]: New Mexico Survivors' Truth Commission, *Interim Report* (August 5, 2026), https://www.nmlegis.gov/handouts/HISC%20080526%20Item%201%20Interim%20Report.pdf, pp. 12–14 and 21, and “Disclaimer & Disclosures,” p. 5. House Resolution 1 was approved February 16, 2026, 62–0; it established the Commission with a remit concerning Epstein, his co-conspirators, and his facilitators in New Mexico, granted witness-compulsion and subpoena powers, and the report records use of HR-1 subpoenas while expressly offering no final findings. The resolution's text creates "a special committee" and does not use the name; the Legislature's bill page records passage, 62–0, on February 16. New Mexico's Attorney General has separately sued DOJ under the Administrative Procedure Act over its refusal of his *Touhy* requests for investigative records for a state criminal investigation, *New Mexico v. U.S. Department of Justice*, No. 1:26-cv-02762 (D.D.C. filed Aug. 5, 2026); the allegations are the complaint's, and DOJ's motion to dismiss or for summary judgment (Oct. 12, 2026), which argues that the denial is committed to agency discretion and was not arbitrary, is pending as of the docket read October 31, 2026.


[^tt-released-count]: Released-page count measured directly from the DOJ EFTA production manifests for Data Sets 1 through 12, as counted through Bates EFTA02731789: 1,378,393 Bates-EFTA PDF documents summing to 2,729,256 pages (three documents carry no recorded page count). An independent public mirror (epstein-data.com v5.2 concordance index) counts 1,380,964 EFTA-Bates documents and 2,731,789 pages; the release and content status of the 2,571 additional identifiers is unresolved, because each has a start-page image path and the sparse metadata does not distinguish them from matched entries (the project's October 12, 2026 audit, correcting its earlier reconciliation record). Bates-EFTA records that DOJ published above EFTA02731789 (Appendix A.1) are outside these counts. DOJ's January 30, 2026 Compliance Letter #2 (p. 2) reports releasing "over 3 million" responsive pages and "nearly 3.5 million" combined with prior productions; the February 14, 2026 Section 3 Report states no count. The gap between the claimed and measured totals is unreconciled in the compliance record.

[^tt-media-crosswalk]: First accounting, the project's June 2026 transcription run: file-status record (SHA256 1bbc0d28731a01622bf33411eb2b9151be8362a131b2b83c9dc704940d5ff587), columns status, duration_sec, and transcript_chars: 557 files, 546 ok (471 with empty and 75 with nonempty transcripts) and 11 no-audio; the ok durations total 446.938 hours, of which 20.710 have nonempty output. Second accounting, regenerated June 15, 2026: 3,175 restored media files from Data Sets 9 through 11, of which 513 have transcript text, 906 have an audio stream with no speech recognized, and 1,756 have no audio stream. These are file-level processing counts, not listening-based or whole-media negative findings. Local records, available on request.

[^tt-glomar-cycles]: The released agency Glomar correspondence documents three request cycles in which Epstein, through counsel, sought records about himself. A CIA Privacy Act response of July 29, 2011 to attorney Martin G. Weinberg (EFTA01089565) reported no records of an "open or otherwise acknowledged Agency affiliation" and declined to confirm or deny any "classified connection to the CIA" under Executive Order 13526 Section 3.6(a), referencing a prior 1999 cycle (acceptance letter P-1999-02450, November 5, 1999). A 2014 NSA cycle on FOIA Case 76098 comprises attorney Darren K. Indyke's appeal of March 4, 2014 (EFTA00583099), which quotes and describes an initial denial of January 17, 2014 that the production does not itself carry under a separate Bates; the NSA's acknowledgment of that appeal, March 25, 2014 (EFTA02726324, with a second, differently redacted copy at EFTA00605791); and the NSA appeal response of July 30, 2014 (EFTA00606101), which continued to neither confirm nor deny intelligence material on Epstein and named the Section 215 and Section 702 collection programs. A Glomar response is applied categorically and is by design uninformative as to whether responsive records exist, so the correspondence evidences the requests and the agencies' withholding posture, not any intelligence relationship.

[^tt-julie-brown]: Julie K. Brown, "Perversion of Justice," *Miami Herald*, November 28, 2018, as cited by Department of Justice, Office of Professional Responsibility, report of November 2020 on the Southern District of Florida's resolution of its 2006–2008 investigation of Jeffrey Epstein, Executive Summary, p. iii n.3; OPR found that "The Miami Herald report led to public outrage and media scrutiny of the government's actions" and records the Southern District of New York indictment of July 2, 2019 (p. iv).

[^tt-binder-etp]: The pre-EFTA executive offer sequence. On February 27, 2025 the Department of Justice released what it called the "first phase" of declassified Epstein files, distributing binders labeled "The Epstein Files: Phase 1" to about fifteen online influencers at the White House; Kerry Breen, "Right-wing influencers get binders labeled 'The Epstein Files,' but downplay revelations," CBS News, February 27, 2025 (fifteen attendees; the Department's statement that the first phase "largely contains documents that have been previously leaked"). Office of the Attorney General, "Attorney General Pamela Bondi Releases First Phase of Declassified Epstein Files," February 27, 2025; Attorney General Bondi letter to FBI Director Kash Patel, February 27, 2025, demanding the "full and complete Epstein files" with "no withholdings or limitations." The FBI's internal Epstein Transparency Project processing guidance (training dated March 21, 2025) was provided by the FBI directly to DOJ on March 23, 2025; the operational summary at EFTA00172473 states it was provided to all FBI personnel participating in the redaction project. An undated, unsigned Department of Justice and FBI memorandum made public in July 2025, https://www.justice.gov/opa/media/1407001/dl, stated that the review "revealed no incriminating 'client list'" and that "no further disclosure would be appropriate or warranted"; see also Associated Press, "A look at how the Epstein files dogged Pam Bondi's time as attorney general," PBS NewsHour ("July 2025: The walkback"; "an unsigned memo"). EFTA (Public Law 119-38) followed on November 19, 2025.

[^tt5]: DOJ EFTA production, EFTA00172446, PDF p. 1 (March 21, 2025 training date), p. 6 ("Do not add any exemptions; just redaction boxes", without a period). EFTA00172329 is a second scan of the same training, not independent evidence. The instruction concerns the earlier processing workflow. DOJ's January 30, 2026 letter describes updated protocols and FAQs; neither the training nor released box-only pages proves that the old instruction governed post-enactment review. "EFTA" followed by eight digits is the Bates number DOJ stamped on each document of the production; each document is posted in the Department's Epstein Library at an address of the form https://www.justice.gov/epstein/files/DataSet%2012/EFTA02858481.pdf, with the data-set number and Bates number varying.

[^tt-gj-unsealing-gambit]: The grand-jury-unsealing gesture. On July 18, 2025 the Government moved to unseal the grand jury transcripts in both *United States v. Epstein* (S.D.N.Y. 19 Cr. 490, Dkt. 61) and *United States v. Maxwell* (S.D.N.Y. 20 Cr. 330, Dkt. 785), the motion bearing the signature block of Attorney General Pamela Bondi and Deputy Attorney General Todd Blanche (signed /s/ by Blanche); U.S. Attorney Jay Clayton signed the Government's July 29 supplemental memorandum (Maxwell Dkt. 796) (the Main-Justice leadership appearing personally on a district-court filing is itself notable). Judge Engelmayer denied the Maxwell motion on August 11, 2025, holding that the Second Circuit's narrow *In re Craig* "special circumstances" exception to grand-jury secrecy did not reach summary-witness testimony largely duplicative of the trial record, and that granting it "would bloat the 'special circumstances' doctrine" (*United States v. Maxwell*, 794 F. Supp. 3d 215, 225–29 (S.D.N.Y. 2025), as the January 21, 2026 order in that case summarizes it at p. 5; the copy cited bears Bates DOJ-OGR-00015133–163, the prefix of DOJ's August 22, 2025 production to the House Oversight Committee). The parallel Southern District of Florida motion had already been denied on July 23, 2025 on the ground that no Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 6(e) exception applied and that the Eleventh Circuit does not recognize the special-circumstances doctrine (*In re Grand Jury 05-02 (WPB) & 07-103 (WPB)*, No. 9:25-mc-80920-RLR, Dkt. 4 (S.D. Fla. July 23, 2025)). The proposed unsealing also kept covered material covered: the Government's July 29 supplemental brief proposed redacting uncharged third parties (Maxwell Dkt. 796 at 7), which victims' counsel attacked as a proposal that "smacks of a cover up" (McCawley/Farmer letter, Aug. 5, 2025, Bates DOJ-OGR-00015103–107), while separately-counseled third parties whom the Department had told on August 1 that they were "referenced in some capacity" in the materials, but declined to tell in what context, moved to keep the transcripts sealed entirely (Binder & Schwartz letter, Aug. 5, 2025, Bates DOJ-OGR-00015118–122). Later reporting indicates the futility was anticipated: per Maggie Haberman and Jonathan Swan, "Inside the White House Freakout Over the Epstein Files," *New York Times Magazine*, June 10, 2026, at a July 17, 2025 White House meeting the Deputy Attorney General laid out the unsealing petition as an option and predicted the courts would refuse, which would shift blame for withholding onto the judges while making the administration appear to favor release. That last point is reported intent, not part of the court record, and is treated here only as consistent with the litigated outcome the record establishes.

[^tt-clark-extension-public-inoculation]: Clark, "Congressional Access to Intelligence Information," pp. 14–15 (Part IV, "Critique of the Current System: Limited Access as Inoculation"), describes executive-imposed access restrictions and limits on consultation, with congressional silence converted into apparent endorsement; she does not identify a signed classified-access agreement as the instrument. Clark's account supports restricted consultation and silence converted into apparent endorsement; it does not address motive behind a later public release. This paper proposes, as an untested hypothesis, an extension to a public audience: that a large partial release may saturate attention on some dimensions while others remain unresolved. No intent is claimed. The hypothesis predicts that, after the release, coverage density and survey attention on the unresolved dimensions will fall relative to the resolved ones; 2026–27 coverage counts and polling could test it.

[^tt-maxwell-asymmetry]: ABC News, "Ghislaine Maxwell invokes 5th Amendment in closed-door House Oversight deposition," February 9, 2026, https://abcnews.com/Politics/maxwell-expected-invoke-amendment-closed-virtual-house-oversight/story?id=129991066 (a "closed-door virtual deposition" in which she "repeatedly invoked" the privilege). DOJ, Compliance Letter #1, December 19, 2025, p. 5 (release on August 22, 2025 of the transcript and audio of "the Deputy Attorney General's interview of Ghislaine Maxwell"). Rep. Robert Garcia to Bureau of Prisons Director Marshall, August 1, 2025, https://oversightdemocrats.house.gov/imo/media/doc/2025-08-01-rg-to-bop-director-marshall-re-maxwell.pdf, p. 1 (transfer from Tallahassee to Federal Prison Camp Bryan, "a facility with far fewer restrictions and security measures"); Garcia to Warden Tanisha Hall, October 30, 2025, https://oversightdemocrats.house.gov/imo/media/doc/2025-10-30.garcia-to-fpc-bryan-warden-re-maxwell.pdf, p. 1 (such transfers for sex offenders "are at odds with BOP policy"). Rep. Ro Khanna to Chairman James Comer, February 8, 2026, https://khanna.house.gov/sites/evo-subsites/khanna.house.gov/files/evo-media-document/rep-khanna-letter-to-chair-comer-re-maxwell-deposition-88.pdf, p. 1 (a blanket invocation "appears inconsistent" with her not invoking the privilege when she met the Deputy Attorney General "to discuss substantially similar subject matter"). Rep. Jamie Raskin to President Trump, November 9, 2025, https://democrats-judiciary.house.gov/sites/evo-subsites/democrats-judiciary.house.gov/files/evo-media-document/2025-11-09-raskin-to-trump-wh-re-maxwell.pdf (whistleblower information about her treatment at Federal Prison Camp Bryan and a "Commutation Application"). Twelve senators to the DOJ Office of the Inspector General, December 24, 2025, https://www.blumenthal.senate.gov/imo/media/doc/20251224lettertodojoigonepsteinreleasefinal.pdf, p. 1 (requesting "an audit of the Department's compliance with the statute"). The letters record allegations, not findings, and waiver of the privilege is generally assessed proceeding by proceeding, so the contrast is not itself evidence of waiver.

[^tt-epstein-npa]: Non-prosecution agreement between the United States Attorney's Office for the Southern District of Florida and Jeffrey Epstein, signed September 24, 2007, p. 5 (the United States "will not institute any criminal charges against any potential co-conspirators of Epstein, including but not limited to" four named women). Office of Professional Responsibility, report of November 2020, Executive Summary, pp. i–iv: on June 30, 2008 Epstein pleaded guilty in state court to the pending indictment for felony solicitation of prostitution and, under the agreement, to an information charging procurement of minors to engage in prostitution; he "actually served less than 13 months," with work release from October 2008; and on July 12, 2019 Acosta submitted his resignation. *Doe 1 v. United States*, 359 F. Supp. 3d 1201 (S.D. Fla. 2019) (Crime Victims' Rights Act); *United States v. Maxwell*, 118 F.4th 256 (2d Cir. 2024) ("the NPA with USAO-SDFL does not bind USAO-SDNY"). The Senate confirmed Acosta as Secretary of Labor on April 27, 2017, 60–38 (Roll Call Vote 116, 115th Cong., 1st Sess.).

[^tt-subpoena-roster]: Selected dated appearances, not a complete roster or uniform-denial dataset: Maxwell, February 9, 2026 (Fifth Amendment invocation); Bondi, May 29 (unsworn transcribed interview); Gates, June 10 (transcribed interview). House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform, Bill Gates interview transcript, title page and introductory procedure, https://oversight.house.gov/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/Bill-Gates-Transcript.pdf; Committee release, https://oversight.house.gov/release/oversight-committee-releases-gates-and-groff-transcripts/. The former thirteen-subpoena/eight-appearance aggregation and description of Gates as still scheduled are withdrawn.

[^imp1]: *Trump v. Mazars USA, LLP*, 591 U.S. ___ (2020), slip op. at 2 (an "ongoing relationship that the Framers intended to feature both rivalry and reciprocity"), 7 (such disputes "have been hashed out in the 'hurly-burly, the give-and-take of the political process'"), and 10–11 ("longstanding practice"); *AT&T*, 567 F.2d 121, 127 (Leventhal, J.) ("an implicit constitutional mandate to seek optimal accommodation through a realistic evaluation of the needs of the conflicting branches"). See also Clark, "Congressional Access to Intelligence Information"; Kitrosser, "Congressional Oversight of National Security Activities."

[^imp2]: Marc J. Hetherington, "The Political Relevance of Political Trust," *American Political Science Review* 92, no. 4 (1998): 791–808; Hetherington, *Why Trust Matters: Declining Political Trust and the Demise of American Liberalism* (Princeton: Princeton University Press, 2005); Virginia A. Chanley, Thomas J. Rudolph, and Wendy M. Rahn, "The Origins and Consequences of Public Trust in Government: A Time Series Analysis," *Public Opinion Quarterly* 64, no. 3 (2000): 239–256; Jack Citrin and Donald Philip Green, "Presidential Leadership and the Resurgence of Trust in Government," *British Journal of Political Science* 16, no. 4 (1986): 431–453; Marc J. Hetherington and Jason A. Husser, "How Trust Matters: The Changing Political Relevance of Political Trust," *American Journal of Political Science* 56, no. 2 (2012): 312–325. For the post-Snowden Pew panel data, see Pew Research Center, "Public Perceptions of Privacy and Security in the Post-Snowden Era" (2014). For the 1964 (77%) and 2025 (17%) trust-in-government endpoints, see Pew Research Center, "Public Trust in Government" longitudinal series (1958–present).

[^imp3]: Steven Levitsky and Daniel Ziblatt, *How Democracies Die* (New York: Crown, 2018); Yascha Mounk, *The People vs. Democracy: Why Our Freedom Is in Danger and How to Save It* (Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 2018).

[^imp-enforcement]: The police-patrol / fire-alarm distinction is from McCubbins and Schwartz, "Congressional Oversight Overlooked," 166.

[^tt-pt-table]: The six-row source-to-inference audit is printed in "The cumulative reading" in this paper. Its exact observations, rivals, and missing links replace the earlier unpublished diagnostic table; no inaccessible supplementary table is required to inspect the argument.

[^tt-rival-partisan]: Douglas L. Kriner and Eric Schickler, *Investigating the President: Congressional Checks on Presidential Power* (Princeton: Princeton University Press, 2016), finding that House investigative activity directed at the executive is concentrated in divided government and that the effect intensifies with polarization, a result the authors distinguish from aggregate Senate investigative volume (ch. 1, p. 18); their measured mechanism is erosion of presidential public approval through publicity, not direct compulsion. Daryl J. Levinson and Richard H. Pildes, "Separation of Parties, Not Powers," *Harvard Law Review* 119 (2006): 2311–2386, at 2312 ("The practical distinction between party-divided and party-unified government thus rivals, and often dominates, the constitutional distinction between the branches"). David R. Mayhew, *Divided We Govern*, 2nd ed. (New Haven: Yale University Press, 2005), is further reading.

[^tt-rival-intel]: Zegart, *Spies, Lies, and Algorithms*, 198 (oversight has been difficult "for reasons that have nothing to do with who liked whom or which party was in power"). Fights over notification also fell under both configurations (Table 2). After Iran-Contra the intelligence committees pressed notice legislation against two administrations under an opposition Congress, an impasse that "lasted for almost four years" before the 1991 compromise (Snider, *Agency and the Hill*, 67). In the CIA case the agency left Chairman Graham's requests for information unanswered in 2002, under an opposition Senate, and resisted the inquiry of the minority vice chairman, Rockefeller, a Democrat, including by refusing documents to the full committee in 2006, under a same-party Senate (S. Rep. No. 113-288, Findings and Conclusions, 5–6; U.S. Senate, "Senators from West Virginia," https://www.senate.gov/states/WV/senators.htm). Further reading, not relied on for any claim: Linda L. Fowler, *Watchdogs on the Hill: The Decline of Congressional Oversight of U.S. Foreign Relations* (Princeton: Princeton University Press, 2015); Genevieve Lester, *When Should State Secrets Stay Secret? Accountability, Democratic Governance, and Intelligence* (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 2015).

[^efta-ii-state]: Epstein Files Transparency Act II, H.R. 9694 and S. 5005, 119th Cong., 2d Sess. (2026) (introduced July 15, 2026), § 2: the Attorney General "may not invoke any common law privilege, including the deliberative process privilege, attorney-client privilege, attorney work-product privilege, or law enforcement privilege"; § 2(c)(1)(E) struck; "All redactions" replaced by "Each redaction"; and a new § 4, with actions by State officers and victims (§ 4(a)(1), (b)(3)), including "monetary relief"; unredacted access for a State officer's own investigation or judicial proceeding (§ 4(c)(1): the Attorney General "shall make available for full, unredacted access to, and copies of," records permitted to be withheld or redacted under § 2(c)(1)); and access and an action for Members (§ 4(e)). On parens patriae suits against the United States, *Massachusetts v. Mellon*, 262 U.S. 447, 485–86 (1923); *Alfred L. Snapp & Son, Inc. v. Puerto Rico ex rel. Barez*, 458 U.S. 592 (1982), n.16; *Massachusetts v. EPA*, 549 U.S. 497 (2007) (distinguishing a State's assertion of "its rights under federal law"). Section 4(d) requires "full, unredacted review" by State officers of "classified records described in section 2(a)" under court-approved procedures and bars refusal "on the grounds that the material is classified" (§ 4(d)(1), (3)); on a literal reading § 2(a) describes only unclassified records, and § 4(d)(5) allows public release of classified information only "in accordance with section 2(c)(3)," the provision whose trigger the bills remove. Against § 4(d) the executive would invoke *Department of the Navy v. Egan*, 484 U.S. 518, 527 (1988) (the President's "authority to classify and control access to information bearing on national security ... flows primarily from this constitutional investment of power in the President"), and would meet its qualifier, "unless Congress specifically has provided otherwise" (id. at 530). On Member standing, *Raines*, 521 U.S. 811. The bill writes the Member's right as personal: "Notwithstanding section 2(c) or any other provision of law, upon the written request of any Member of Congress," the Attorney General shall within seven days "provide such Member with full, unredacted access" (§ 4(e)(1)(A)). Whether such a right supports standing is unsettled in the D.C. Circuit. *Maloney v. Murphy*, 984 F.3d 50 (D.C. Cir. 2020), held that members requesting information under 5 U.S.C. § 2954 had standing, but after the plaintiffs voluntarily dismissed their suit the Supreme Court vacated that judgment with instructions to dismiss, *Carnahan v. Maloney*, No. 22-425 (June 26, 2023). In *Neguse v. U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement*, No. 26-5072 (D.C. Cir. May 8, 2026), the panel denied a stay of a ruling that, in a concurring statement's description, held the Members likely to have standing because an appropriations rider "gave them an individual right" of access; that statement would hold the ruling contrary to *Raines*, citing 521 U.S. at 820 n.3.

[^adverse-users]: The EFTA II bills' access routes would reach victim material. A State attorney general, district attorney, or "other authorized State officer" conducting an investigation or proceeding would receive "full, unredacted access to, and copies of," records the Act permits to be withheld under § 2(c)(1) (H.R. 9694, § 4(c)(1)), subject to "any protective order entered by a court to protect the personally identifiable information of victims" (§ 4(c)(2)) and to filing under seal unless a court finds public disclosure lawful and non-identifying (§ 4(c)(3)), but production "shall not be limited to in camera review," and no protective order may prohibit the officer "from possessing or using the records as necessary for an investigation or judicial proceeding" (§ 4(c)(4)). A Member's access applies "Notwithstanding section 2(c) or any other provision of law" (§ 4(e)(1)(A)). Child sexual abuse material is described in the Act itself as "prohibited under 18 U.S.C. 2252–2252A" (§ 2(c)(1)(B)), statutes that bar its distribution whatever the permissive "may" in § 2(c)(1). On its text, § 4(e)(1)(A)'s "any other provision of law" would displace those statutes for a Member's access, and § 4(e) sets none of the handling conditions that § 4(c) and § 4(d) impose, such as "a secure facility" (§ 4(d)(2)) or "any protective order entered by a court" (§ 4(c)(2)).

[^env-citizen-suit]: Clean Air Act § 304, 42 U.S.C. § 7604; Endangered Species Act § 11(g), 16 U.S.C. § 1540(g); Clean Water Act § 505, 33 U.S.C. § 1365; Resource Conservation and Recovery Act § 7002, 42 U.S.C. § 6972; Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act § 310, 42 U.S.C. § 9659. These provisions authorize citizen enforcement subject to their respective jurisdictional and notice conditions; suits to compel non-discretionary agency duties supply the closest analogy for Proposal 1's access-review remedy. Private initiation does not exclude DOJ participation in federal litigation under 28 U.S.C. § 516. A citizen-suit provision does not dispense with a concrete injury, *Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife*, 504 U.S. 555 (1992). Proposal 1 contains no damages component; Proposal 3 separately identifies the waiver and payment authority that damages would require.

[^vaughn]: *Vaughn v. Rosen*, 484 F.2d 820 (D.C. Cir. 1973), establishes the modern Freedom-of-Information Act practice of requiring an agency claiming exemption to produce an itemized index correlating specific portions of withheld material to specific exemptions and supplying segregability analysis subject to adversarial review. Proposal 2 applies the *Vaughn* architecture to the proposed EFTA access-review scheme (extension to other statutes is reserved for later design), with protected review by the district court and magistrate assistance only through a valid 28 U.S.C. § 636 referral. Agency custody of records and DOJ participation in litigation remain dependencies; the analogy does not create an autonomous review institution.

[^prv-action]: Analogies only: 15 U.S.C. § 78p(b) permits shareholder enforcement of short-swing-profit recovery; FCRA § 1681n permits actual or statutory damages for willful violations, while § 1681o concerns actual damages for negligent violations. Privacy Act remedies appear in 5 U.S.C. § 552a(g); a plaintiff "must prove some actual damages to qualify for a minimum statutory award," *Doe v. Chao*, 540 U.S. 614, 616 (2004), and the Act "does not unequivocally authorize an award of damages for mental or emotional distress," *FAA v. Cooper*, 566 U.S. 284, 304 (2012). Judgments against the United States are ordinarily paid from the permanent appropriation in 31 U.S.C. § 1304(a); for FOIA fee awards Congress barred payment from the Judgment Fund and required payment "only from funds annually appropriated" to the agency, OPEN Government Act of 2007, Pub. L. No. 110-175, § 4(b). Section 1983 concerns action under color of state law; § 1985 creates distinct conspiracy remedies. These mechanisms do not create an EFTA damages waiver. DOJ may still conduct federal defensive litigation under 28 U.S.C. § 516.

[^sox]: Sarbanes-Oxley Act of 2002, Pub. L. No. 107-204, 116 Stat. 745; § 404 (internal-controls auditing); § 101 et seq. (Public Company Accounting Oversight Board). The SOX/PCAOB architecture supplies a limited analogy for compliance auditing and reporting; 15 U.S.C. § 7211(h) routes the Board's annual report through the SEC to Congress. Proposal 4 would require its own appointment, access, preservation, budget, and enforcement provisions for executive-agency records. The analogy establishes neither automatic independence from executive supervision nor access to federal secrets. The Comptroller General is an officer of the legislative branch, *Bowsher v. Synar*, 478 U.S. 714 (1986); his civil action for records is barred when the President or the Director of the Office of Management and Budget certifies that a record could be withheld under 5 U.S.C. § 552(b)(5) or (7) and that disclosure "reasonably could be expected to impair substantially the operations of the Government," 31 U.S.C. § 716(d)(1)(C). When the Comptroller General sued the Vice President for energy task-force records, the suit was dismissed because he "does not have the personal, concrete, and particularized injury required under Article III standing doctrine, either himself or as the agent of Congress," *Walker v. Cheney*, 230 F. Supp. 2d 51, 53 (D.D.C. 2002). The Attorney General "may prohibit the Inspector General from carrying out or completing any audit or investigation" concerning specified sensitive information, 5 U.S.C. § 413(a).

[^ig-wb]: 5 U.S.C. §§ 403 (written reasons for removal of an inspector general, communicated to both Houses not later than 30 days before removal) and 416(b)–(g); 50 U.S.C. §§ 3033(k)(5)(F) and 3517(d)(5)(F) (urgent-concern actions "shall not be subject to judicial review"); § 3234 (reprisal protections; under § 3234(f) "The President shall provide for the enforcement of this section"); § 3341(j) (security-clearance reprisal; § 3341(j)(6) denies judicial review); § 3236 (external review panel). *Free Enterprise Fund v. PCAOB*, 561 U.S. 477 (2010), and *Seila Law LLC v. CFPB*, 591 U.S. 197 (2020), must now be read with *Trump v. Slaughter* (2026); an IG for-cause shield is a contested proposal, not a safe single-layer exception.

[^tt-chain-reaction-withholding]: For the March 2026 additional interview-summary release, see NPR, "Justice Department posts more Epstein files related to accusations about Trump" (npr.org, March 5, 2026), and James Hill and Peter Charalambous, "Epstein files: DOJ releases previously withheld FBI reports about sex abuse allegation against Trump" (ABC News, March 6, 2026). The related notes dispute is addressed in *Phang*, ECF 16, pp. 35–36, and ECF 43, pp. 12–13, with the latter ordering further in-camera submissions; those orders are not the source for the March release. Wyden's February 25 and March 18, 2026 correspondence concerning the DEA memorandum (EFTA00173953) records a demand and an allegation of interference; DOJ disputed that account. A disputed allegation is not an adjudicated obstruction finding. The separate Treasury example is dated February 14, 2024: Senate Finance minority staff, Looking the Other Way, August 4, 2026, PDF pp. 5–6. The report also describes later EFTA disclosure of SARs and bank records. Controlled access in 2024 therefore does not establish permanent public unavailability or prove the same later outcome in a different agency.

[^tt-bondi-format]: House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform, Transcribed Interview of Pamela Bondi, May 29, 2026, https://oversight.house.gov/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/Final-Bondi-Transcript.pdf: PDF/printed p. 27 (Blanche supervised review), p. 28 (six-million-page collection), p. 29 (missed deadline and more than 500 attorneys). These are Bondi's descriptions, not independent audit results. The introductory procedural statement identifies a transcribed interview rather than a sworn deposition. Liability for a false statement under 18 U.S.C. § 1001 differs from sworn perjury under § 1621; the availability of either requires its elements and prosecutorial action, not merely an inaccurate answer.
