[^tt-53-pages]: Stephen Fowler, "DOJ removed, withheld Epstein files related to accusations about Trump," NPR, February 24, 2026, https://www.npr.org/2026/02/24/nx-s1-5723968/epstein-files-trump-accusation-maxwell (by NPR's review of serial numbers, "53 pages of interview documents and notes missing from the public Epstein database"; the Department's position that unpublished documents "are privileged, are duplicates or relate to an ongoing federal investigation"; and Ranking Member Garcia's statement that "the DOJ appears to have illegally withheld FBI interviews with this survivor"). For the March 2026 releases, see NPR, "Justice Department posts more Epstein files related to accusations about Trump," March 5, 2026, and James Hill and Peter Charalambous, "Epstein files: DOJ releases previously withheld FBI reports about sex abuse allegation against Trump," ABC News, March 6, 2026. The point is the compliance posture, not the truth of the underlying allegations: the release followed external pressure and was not compelled by contempt, an appropriations rider, or a court order.

[^tt-massie]: 172 *Cong. Rec.* H2266–H2267 (daily ed. February 24, 2026), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CREC-2026-02-24/html/CREC-2026-02-24-pt1-PgH2266.htm (Rep. Massie: "I will name them right here," naming Leon Black, Jes Staley, and Leslie Wexner and asking why they were not being investigated). U.S. Const. art. I, § 6, cl. 1; *Gravel v. United States*, 408 U.S. 606, 625 (1972) (private republication "was in no way essential to the deliberations of the Senate"); *Hutchinson v. Proxmire*, 443 U.S. 111 (1979) (no immunity for republication of a speech first delivered in Congress). A member's naming is an allegation, not a legal disposition.

[^tt-four-cases]: The four inherited record or redaction groupings: Bureau of Prisons records withheld under FOIA Exemption 7(A) on the basis of *United States v. Tartaglione*, No. 16-cr-832 (KMK) (S.D.N.Y.), in *The New York Times Co. v. Federal Bureau of Prisons*, No. 20 Civ. 833 (PAE) (S.D.N.Y.), as the government's letter of April 28, 2021 (ECF 52) records (EFTA00085218, p. 1); the *United States v. Maxwell* trial record (sealed under Second Circuit rules, *Lugosch v. Pyramid Co. of Onondaga*); FBI investigative files (Privacy Act subsection (j)(2)); and files stamped "FOIA Exempt / SBU."

[^meth-scope]: Scope is assigned from executive custody and institutional constraints before the outcome. Later successful access does not remove a case from scope. The four historical episodes were used to develop the eight process indicators, and the Pike and Church profiles are separated in Appendix B; the number of marks in a column is a description, not a test, and no cutoff decides inclusion. Accountability, policy, institutions, and access remain separate outcomes.

[^tbl5a-pike]: Pike and Church share a historical episode but differ in chamber, mandate, leadership, procedure, and demand posture. Appendix B separates their eight-indicator profiles. The combined column is an episode-level union, not a controlled comparison or evidence of identical outcomes.

[^a32-whitebox-provenance]: The adjudicated examples were described as inherited FBI FOIA-style records, with exemption annotations and earlier production stamps. That provenance inference is limited to the inspected pages, not every white box or the entire EFTA workflow. The March 2025 training cannot establish post-enactment continuity. The final calibration accounting is 220 unique candidates, three unclear exclusions, 217 evaluable labels; the separate point estimate remains imprecise.

[^a-doccount]: The twelve DOJ data-set manifests list 1,378,394 PDF rows, which collapse to 1,378,393 unique Bates documents: one Data Set 8 row (EFTA00022173 through EFTA00022175) is duplicated at two paths with the same SHA256. An independent public concordance (epstein-data.com) counts 1,380,964; the release and content status of the 2,571 additional identifiers is unresolved, because each has a start-page image path and the sparse metadata does not distinguish them from matched entries (the project's October 12, 2026 audit, correcting its earlier reconciliation record). The sum of known PDF page counts is 2,729,256 after deduplicating the duplicate three-page row; three documents (EFTA00645624, EFTA01175426, EFTA01220934) have no recorded count. This defines the analyzed manifest population, not a reconciled census of every released page. The further Bates-EFTA files described in A.1 are not included in these counts.

[^wyden-bofa-aml]: Senate Finance Committee minority staff, Looking the Other Way: How Wall Street Banks enabled Jeffrey Epstein’s sex trafficking, August 4, 2026, PDF pp. 4–6, https://www.finance.senate.gov/imo/media/doc/wyden_wall_street_epstein_report.pdf. The staff's conclusion that Bank of America likely violated AML law is an attributed legislative assessment, not an adjudication or proof that prosecutors made a formal declination. The report describes Treasury access at controlled facilities on February 14, 2024; later public SAR releases mean controlled access then is not a claim that those records remain permanently unavailable. Cited here as a further instance of detected conduct without enforcement: the staff report calls "[t]he lack of enforcement action against Wall Street banks and their employees regarding their handling of Epstein's accounts" a miscarriage of justice (printed p. 65).

[^es-corpus-guidance]: Alan ElSheshai, *EFTA Document-Count Reconciliation*, June 28, 2026, content version 2796439c2001, “Counts,” “Pairwise deltas,” and “Verdict”, unpublished supplement held with the paper’s review record, available on request. Analysis of the twelve DOJ EFTA dataset manifests, the full-corpus classification table, and the public epstein-data.com concordance: deduplicating manifest PDF rows by Bates range and file hash and comparing the concordance-only identifiers produced 1,378,393 unique released documents. The count is read with this paper's August 30, 2026 measurement cutoff. Dated instruction and interval: DOJ/FBI, Epstein Project Processing Guidance, EFTA00172446, March 21, 2025, instruction "Do not add any exemptions; just redaction boxes"; the eight-month interval runs to EFTA's November 19, 2025 enactment.

[^constraint-access]: Clark, "Congress's Right to Counsel," pp. 917 (Rockefeller unable to consult staff or counsel), 935 ("Limited Notification as Inoculation"), and 959–60 (conclusion), https://www.illinoislawreview.org/wp-content/ilr-content/articles/2011/3/Clark.pdf; Cumming, *Congress as a Consumer*, summary and pp. 1–2, especially p. 2 n.5 (members' access to intelligence is "by virtue of their elected positions"; members are not subject to background checks, "nor are they issued security clearances").

[^constraint-enforcement]: 2 U.S.C. §§ 192, 194 (criminal contempt, certified to "the appropriate United States attorney, whose duty it shall be to bring the matter before the grand jury"); 18 U.S.C. §§ 1001, 1621; 28 U.S.C. §§ 516, 547(1). Office of Legal Counsel, "Prosecution for Contempt of Congress of an Executive Branch Official Who Has Asserted a Claim of Executive Privilege," 8 Op. O.L.C. 101 (1984), https://www.justice.gov/file/150521/dl, 102 (a United States Attorney "is not required to refer a contempt citation in these circumstances to a grand jury or otherwise to prosecute an Executive Branch official who is carrying out the President's instruction in a factual context such as that presented by the December 16, 1982, contempt citation"; "our conclusions are limited to the unique circumstances that gave rise to these questions in late 1982 and early 1983"), 140 n.42 (the same conclusion "would apply to any attempt by Congress to utilize its inherent 'civil' contempt powers to arrest, bring to trial, and punish an executive official who asserted a Presidential claim of executive privilege"). Office of Legal Counsel, "Whether Congress May Use Inherent Contempt to Punish Executive Branch Officials Who Withhold Subpoenaed Materials Based on a Presidential Assertion of Executive Privilege," December 20, 2024, https://www.justice.gov/olc/media/1382626/dl, slip op. at 1 (the resolution for daily fines on the Attorney General "was brought to the House floor but failed to pass," citing 170 Cong. Rec. H4609 (daily ed. July 11, 2024)), 1–2 ("Congress cannot use its inherent contempt power to arrest, imprison, or otherwise punish an Executive Branch official for complying with the President's assertion of executive privilege"), 3 (under "longstanding Executive Branch precedent, the criminal contempt of Congress statute does not apply, and could not constitutionally be applied, to Executive Branch officials who do not disclose materials to Congress based on a presidential assertion of privilege," citing 8 Op. O.L.C. at 129; the recordings subpoenaed from the Attorney General were "the audio recordings of the Special Counsel's interviews"), and 4 (the Department told the Speaker in June 2024 that it "would not bring the matter before a grand jury or take any other action to prosecute the Attorney General"; the Department's letter itself was not opened for this edition). Todd Garvey, *Congressional Subpoenas: Enforcing Executive Branch Compliance*, Congressional Research Service Report R45653, March 27, 2019, p. 9 (on the 2012 citation of Attorney General Holder, "The DOJ shortly thereafter informed the Speaker that it would not take any action on the criminal contempt citation"). *United States v. Navarro*, No. 24-3006 (D.C. Cir. July 21, 2026), https://media.cadc.uscourts.gov/opinions/docs/2026/07/24-3006-2184223.pdf, slip op. at 2 ("the district court concluded that no such invocation of privilege had occurred"), 14–15 (after a change of administration "the government is deemed to have withdrawn from any further participation in this appeal"), and 37 ("we affirm the district court's judgment"), reh'g denied (D.C. Cir. Aug. 28, 2026), petition for cert. filed, No. 26-474 (U.S. Oct. 6, 2026). Independent-counsel statute: enacted in the Ethics in Government Act of 1978 and lapsed in December 1992 (PBS *Frontline*, "A Brief History of the Independent Counsel Law," May 1998); reauthorized "for an additional 5 years" by the Independent Counsel Reauthorization Act of 1994, Pub. L. No. 103-270, 108 Stat. 732 (approved June 30, 1994), it expired June 30, 1999. Inherent contempt: *Anderson v. Dunn*, 19 U.S. (6 Wheat.) 204 (1821); *McGrain v. Daugherty*, 273 U.S. 135 (1927); *Jurney v. MacCracken*, 294 U.S. 125 (1935); Garvey, CRS Report R45653, p. 16 (it "does not appear that either house has exercised its inherent contempt power to enforce subpoenas ... since the 1930s"). Civil enforcement: 28 U.S.C. § 1365(a) (the section "shall not apply" to a subpoena to an executive officer or employee acting in an official capacity, unless the refusal rests on "a personal privilege or objection"); *Committee on the Judiciary v. Miers*, 558 F. Supp. 2d 53 (D.D.C. 2008); *Committee on Oversight and Government Reform v. Holder*, 979 F. Supp. 2d 1 (D.D.C. 2013) ("The motion to dismiss will be denied"); *Committee on Oversight and Government Reform v. Lynch*, 156 F. Supp. 3d 101, 115 (D.D.C. 2016) (the qualified privilege "invoked to shield material that the Department has already disclosed" outweighed, and "the records must be produced"); D.C. Cir. No. 16-5078, joint report of April 2, 2019 ("abeyance status is settlement proceedings ongoing") and clerk's order of May 14, 2019 ("granting motion to dismiss case voluntarily"); *Committee on Oversight and Government Reform v. Sessions*, No. 12-cv-1332 (D.D.C. October 22, 2018) (memorandum opinion and order), 2 (the parties' settlement was effective only on vacatur of the 2014 and 2016 orders, and "the Court will deny the parties' motion") and 8 (quoting the Department's appellate brief: "the Department has complied in full"); *Committee on the Judiciary v. Garland*, No. 1:24-cv-01911 (D.D.C.), complaint of July 1, 2024, minute entry of October 28, 2024 (cross-motions argued and "taken under advisement"), minute order of January 27, 2025 (case stayed), minute order of May 19, 2025 ("In light of the public release of the audio recording at issue in this case, the parties must show cause by May 21, 2025 as to why the case should not be dismissed as moot"), and stipulation of dismissal, ECF 31, May 21, 2025; 2 U.S.C. §§ 288b(b) (Senate Legal Counsel sues "only when directed to do so by the adoption of a resolution by the Senate") and 288d(a) (suit "under any statute conferring jurisdiction on any court of the United States (including section 1365 of title 28)"); *Committee on the Judiciary v. McGahn*, 968 F.3d 755 (D.C. Cir. 2020) (en banc) (standing); *Committee on the Judiciary v. McGahn*, 973 F.3d 121 (D.C. Cir. 2020) (no cause of action), judgment vacated on the grant of rehearing en banc (order of October 15, 2020). *Black v. House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform*, No. 1:26-cv-03084 (D.D.C. filed September 3, 2026), complaint ¶ 84 ("Congress has not authorized House committees to bring civil actions to enforce subpoenas against private citizens") and prayer for relief (a) (a declaration that the Committee "lacks any statutory, constitutional, equitable, or implied cause of action to enforce the Document Subpoena or Deposition Subpoena against Mr. Black in federal court"); the docket read October 31, 2026 shows no ruling (last filing September 9, 2026).

[^constraint-redaction]: Cumming, *Congress as a Consumer*, summary and pp. 1–2 (executive control over intelligence flow); Executive Order 13526 §§ 1.3 and 3.1–3.3 (executive classification and declassification authority), 3 C.F.R. 298 (2010 Comp.), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2010-title3-vol1/pdf/CFR-2010-title3-vol1-eo13526.pdf.

[^efta-crosswalk]: U.S. Department of Justice, Office of Information Policy, *EFTA-FOIA Crosswalk*, pp. 1–2, April 30, 2026, https://www.justice.gov/oip/media/1438931/dl. The table contains no Section 2(c)(1)(E) row. The phrases "law-enforcement sensitive purposes" and "substantially similar" are not in the crosswalk: Section 3 Report, p. 4 n.2 (individuals "whose names were redacted for law-enforcement sensitive purposes are not included"); *Phang v. Blanche*, ECF 43, pp. 4–5 (the Department's position that the handwritten interview notes are "substantially similar to" the typewritten reports).

[^phang-oig-sequence]: *Phang v. [^phang-orders]: *Phang v. Blanche*, No. 1:26-cv-01417 (D.D.C.), Judge Emmet G. Sullivan: ECF 15, June 25, 2026 (preliminary-injunction order), https://clearinghouse-umich-production.s3.amazonaws.com/media/doc/172046.pdf; ECF 16, June 25, 2026 (memorandum opinion), pp. 17–18 (jurisdictional arguments), 36 (merits arguments treated as conceded, citing Local Civil Rule 7(b)), and 46, https://clearinghouse-umich-production.s3.amazonaws.com/media/doc/172048.pdf; ECF 42, September 16, 2026, https://cases.justia.com/federal/district-courts/district-of-columbia/dcdce/1:2026cv01417/291779/42/0.pdf; ECF 43, September 16, 2026, pp. 2 (merits conceded), 4 and 10 (the "EFTA First Level Review Protocol," cited "(Jan. 4, 2026)" although, by the order's own account at p. 11, attached to the December 19 letter; whether the Act permits Privacy Act redactions "is not presently before the Court"), 12 (the protocol "requires the redactions of government personnel and their email addresses"), 13 (appeal No. 26-5299; stay denied), 15 (contempt premature), 16, and 17, https://cases.justia.com/federal/district-courts/district-of-columbia/dcdce/1:2026cv01417/291779/43/0.pdf. A further appeal, No. 26-5334, was docketed in the D.C. Circuit on September 21, 2026 (CourtListener docket). DOJ filed a protocol dated January 4, 2026 on the docket on September 24 (ECF 50-1, "Attorney Review Protocol for Epstein Files"; p. 8 lists Privacy Act withholdings, with exceptions for Senate-confirmed officials, public affairs staff, and two prosecuted prison guards); its title differs from the order's, ECF 59 calls it the January 4 protocol, and whether it is the text attached to the December 19 letter (ECF 50-2) is not established. Later entries: ECF 49, September 21, 2026; ECF 52, October 1, 2026; ECF 57, October 9, 2026; ECF 59, October 16, 2026; and the D.C. Circuit's per curiam order of October 28, 2026, Nos. 26-5299, 26-5334. Dockets read October 31, 2026; the joint notice ordered by ECF 49 has not been seen. Court pagination is used; these are separately dated updates, not a refreshed corpus run.

[^efta-interactions]: *United States v. Maxwell*, No. 20-cr-330 (S.D.N.Y.), Opinion and Order of December 9, 2025 (ECF 820), cited in *Phang* (ECF 16, p. 30) as 811 F. Supp. 3d 667: pp. 14 (§ 2(a) is "strikingly broad"; the enumerated exceptions), 15–16 (the later-in-time and specific-over-general canons: "Both canons apply here with force"), 16 (§ 2(b)(1)), 17 (Rule 6(e) overridden "in the very limited context of the Maxwell and Epstein grand jury materials"), 19 n.11, and 21 (the United States Attorney to "personally certify" compliance with § 2(c)(1)(A) before release), https://www.nysd.uscourts.gov/sites/default/files/2025-12/PAE%20Maxwell%20Opinion%202025.12.09%20%28As%20Docketed%29.pdf; Opinion and Order of January 21, 2026 (ECF 844), pp. 1 (motion by Representatives Massie and Khanna as amici), 4 ("No provision of the EFTA vests this Court with that authority"), and 7 and n.2 (without prejudice to a separate suit; the Representatives are "at liberty to pursue oversight of DOJ via the tools available to Congress"; DOJ's standing argument noted), https://www.nysd.uscourts.gov/sites/default/files/2026-01/Maxwell%2020cr330%20-%20Opinion%20%26%20Order%201.21.26.pdf. The December order concerns the covered Maxwell materials, not categorical repeal of grand-jury secrecy. 5 U.S.C. § 301; 18 U.S.C. § 3509.

[^wild-cvra]: In re Wild, 994 F.3d 1244 (11th Cir. 2021) (en banc), opinion introduction and concluding holding, https://media.ca11.uscourts.gov/opinions/pub/files/201913843.enb.pdf: no freestanding CVRA enforcement action before federal criminal proceedings commence. The contrary district-court finding must be read with this later en banc disposition.

[^privilege-manifest]: Author's privilege-subcluster classification manifest, run_timestamp 2026-06-24T02:44:50Z, input_tsv_sha256 75c83cb396e0d4c6efdc379ad261b27be69eba702b291346a3a9c314771b8a89, subcluster_counts. Local classification run manifest, available on request. The four counts sum to 444,298; 435,453/444,298 rounds to 98.01%. This replaces stale summary prose.

[^magistrate-review]: 28 U.S.C. § 636(b)(1)(A)–(C), (b)(2) (a magistrate judge may serve "as a special master pursuant to the applicable provisions of this title and the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure"), and (c)(1), https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/28/636; Fed. R. Civ. P. 53 (appointment and compensation of masters). A district judge may refer authorized matters and must review properly objected-to dispositive recommendations de novo; consent civil jurisdiction is a separate statutory route. The proposal does not presume that compulsory final adjudication can be assigned to a magistrate without those conditions.

[^remedy-design]: 5 U.S.C. § 702, https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/5/702 (waiver for relief other than money damages); 28 U.S.C. § 516; *TransUnion LLC v. Ramirez*, 594 U.S. 413 (2021), slip op. at 26 (distinguishing public-disclosure laws; no "downstream consequences" from failing to receive the information); *Trump v. CASA, Inc.*, No. 24A884 (June 27, 2025), syllabus ("Universal injunctions likely exceed the equitable authority that Congress has given to federal courts") and slip op. at 11 n.10, https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/24pdf/24a884_8n59.pdf; *Raines v. Byrd*, 521 U.S. 811 (1997). These are design constraints, not assurances that any proposed plaintiff or remedy will survive.

[^slaughter]: *Trump v. Slaughter*, No. 25-332, 609 U.S. ___ (June 29, 2026), slip op. at 21 ("If Congress wishes to establish independent agencies to assist it with its functions, it may do so") and 27–28 (Part III-B; "Nor do we determine the fate of officials not before us"), https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/25pdf/25-332_new_geil.pdf. The reasoning places for-cause inspector-general protection in doubt without deciding it; application requires an office-specific analysis.

[^lit-accommodation]: Harold Hongju Koh, "Why the President (Almost) Always Wins in Foreign Affairs: Lessons of the Iran-Contra Affair," *Yale Law Journal* 97 (1988): 1255–1342, at 1258 (the answer lay in "a combination of three institutional factors: executive initiative, congressional acquiescence, and judicial tolerance") and Part II, 1291–1317. On negotiated accommodation over information: *United States v. AT&T*, 567 F.2d 121 (D.C. Cir. 1977); Peter M. Shane, "Legal Disagreement and Negotiation in a Government of Laws: The Case of Executive Privilege Claims Against Congress," *Minnesota Law Review* 71 (1987): 461–542, at 514–15 n.189 ("a very large number of information demands are being handled amicably between the branches," although "The available data, however, are insufficient" to show the disputed cases comparable) and 515 (the branches "found room for accommodation without documented cost to the public interest"); Neal Devins, "Congressional-Executive Information Access Disputes: A Modest Proposal—Do Nothing," *Administrative Law Review* 48 (1996): 109–137, at 110 ("Congressional committees are routinely able to get what they desire from agencies through direct requests and negotiation").

[^tf-expertise]: Zegart, *Spies, Lies, and Algorithms*, 216: "Of the 535 members serving in the 116th Congress, only eighteen ever worked in an intelligence agency."

[^tf-leaks]: On leaks as a channel the executive condemns in principle and tolerates in practice, see David E. Pozen, "The Leaky Leviathan: Why the Government Condemns and Condones Unlawful Disclosures of Information," *Harvard Law Review* 127 (2013): 512–635, at 513 ("There is a dramatic disconnect between the way our laws and our leaders condemn leaking in the abstract and the way they condone it in practice").

[^tf-watergate]: *United States v. Nixon*, 418 U.S. 683 (1974) (decided July 24, 1974): 688 (the Rule 17(c) subpoena of April 18, 1974; on April 30 "the President publicly released edited transcripts of 43 conversations; portions of 20 conversations subject to subpoena in the present case were included"), 694–95 n.8 and 695–96 (the Special Prosecutor regulation, 38 Fed. Reg. 30739, as amended by 38 Fed. Reg. 32805, under which he "will not be removed from his duties except for extraordinary improprieties on his part"; "So long as this regulation is extant it has the force of law," though "it is theoretically possible for the Attorney General to amend or revoke the regulation"), 703 (quoting *Marbury v. Madison* that "it is emphatically the province and duty of the judicial department to say what the law is"), 706 ("Absent a claim of need to protect military, diplomatic, or sensitive national security secrets"), 708 ("a presumptive privilege for Presidential communications"), 710 (the President "does not place his claim of privilege on the ground they are military or diplomatic secrets"), 712 n.19 ("We are not here concerned with the balance between the President's generalized interest in confidentiality and the need for relevant evidence in civil litigation, nor with that between the confidentiality interest and congressional demands for information, nor with the President's interest in preserving state secrets"), and 713 ("The generalized assertion of privilege must yield to the demonstrated, specific need for evidence in a pending criminal trial"). *Senate Select Committee on Presidential Campaign Activities v. Nixon*, 498 F.2d 725 (D.C. Cir. 1974) (decided May 23, 1974): 726–27 (Butterfield's testimony of July 16, 1973; the committee "attempted informally to obtain certain tapes"; the President's letter of July 25, 1973 declining both subpoenas), 729–30 (the doctrines of *Nixon v. Sirica* "at least by analogy" controlling; presidential conversations "presumptively privileged"), 731 (the committee's showing must be that the tapes are "demonstrably critical to the responsible fulfillment of the Committee's functions"), 732 (the Judiciary Committee "now has in its possession copies of each of the tapes subpoenaed by the Select Committee," so the committee's need is "merely cumulative"), and 733 (the need is "too attenuated and too tangential to its functions"). The committee sued, its final report says, because "it appeared unseemly to attempt enforcement by initiating statutory contempt proceedings against the President (see 2 U.S.C. 192) or by employing the self-help measures at the Senate's command" (Senate Select Committee on Presidential Campaign Activities, *Final Report*, S. Rep. No. 93-981 (1974), 1081). S. Res. 194, 93d Cong. (1973), affirming that the committee was authorized to subpoena and sue the President, was submitted on November 2 and agreed to on November 7, 1973 (119 Cong. Rec. 35714, 36094–95; S. Rep. No. 93-981, 1082); *Senate Select Committee*, 498 F.2d at 727, dates its passage to November 2, the day it was submitted. The jurisdictional statute is Pub. L. No. 93-190 (1973). *Nixon v. Sirica*, 487 F.2d 700, 717 (D.C. Cir. 1973) (per curiam) (presidential conversations "are presumptively privileged"). On the October 1973 Stennis proposal: Richard Nixon, "Statement Announcing Procedures for Providing Information From Presidential Tape Recordings," October 19, 1973 ("Chairman Sam Ervin and Vice Chairman Howard Baker of the Senate Select Committee have agreed to this procedure"; Senator Stennis would "verify that the statement I am preparing is full and accurate"), The American Presidency Project, https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/statement-announcing-procedures-for-providing-information-from-presidential-tape; and "The President's News Conference," October 26, 1973 ("Senator Baker, Senator Ervin approved of the proposition"), The American Presidency Project, https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/the-presidents-news-conference-84; against which the committee's own report states that the two senators met the President "at the President's behest," that "Senators Ervin and Baker agreed to no such compromise," that the President "*unilaterally* offered certain materials to the committee" (emphasis in original), that "it was clearly understood by Senators Ervin and Baker that the committee, in all events, could pursue its lawsuit," that "these two Senators understood that the President was offering the committee verbatim transcripts of the conversations at issue, not mere 'summaries,'" and that "the 'Stennis Compromise' never materialized and the suit continued" (S. Rep. No. 93-981, 1081); National Archives, "Watergate and the Constitution," chronology, https://www.archives.gov/education/lessons/watergate-constitution/chronology.html (October 20, 1973: "Archibald Cox refuses to accept the Stennis compromise"). *United States v. Haldeman*, 559 F.2d 31 (D.C. Cir. 1976) (the arrests of June 17, 1972; sentences of imprisonment; the convictions of Haldeman, Ehrlichman, and Mitchell affirmed). Gerald R. Ford, Proclamation 4311, Granting Pardon to Richard Nixon, September 8, 1974 (reciting the resignation of August 9, 1974). Sequel: Ethics in Government Act of 1978, Pub. L. No. 95-521, 92 Stat. 1824, title VI (a special prosecutor appointed by a division of the court) and title VII (Senate Legal Counsel; a new 28 U.S.C. § 1364 for Senate civil actions, which "shall not apply to an action to enforce ... any subpena or order issued to an officer or employee of the Federal Government acting within his official capacity," 92 Stat. 1879); Senate Historical Office, "Select Committee on Presidential Campaign Activities," https://www.senate.gov/about/powers-procedures/investigations/watergate.htm (the committee's report recommended "the establishment of a permanent special prosecutor, and the creation of a permanent congressional legal service"; "In 1974, Congress overrode a presidential veto and revised the Freedom of Information Act"). The contrast is scored on the Senate committee's own demand and coded from these decisions and records, not from the committees' full hearing records.

[^tf-watergate-insulation]: The insulation followed a breach. On October 20, 1973 "the first Special Prosecutor was dismissed for not agreeing to the so-called 'Stennis Compromise,'" and the Attorney General and the Deputy Attorney General resigned (S. Rep. No. 93-981, 1081). The regulation the Court applied was then issued as Order No. 551-73, dated November 2, 1973 and signed by Robert H. Bork as Acting Attorney General (38 Fed. Reg. 30738–39, November 7, 1973), whom the Court calls "the author of the regulation" (*United States v. Nixon*, 418 U.S. at 695 n.9). It already contained the undertaking, quoted at 694–95 n.8, not to remove the Special Prosecutor without the President's first consulting congressional leaders; Order No. 554-73 of November 19, 1973 (38 Fed. Reg. 32805, November 28, 1973) extended that undertaking to any limit on his jurisdiction.

[^tf-watergate-house]: The Senate committee's subpoena sought "'original electronic tapes' of five conversations between the President and his former Counsel, John W. Dean, III" (*Senate Select Committee*, 498 F.2d at 726). On April 29, 1974 the President announced "my answer to the House Judiciary Committee's subpoena for additional Watergate tapes" (Richard Nixon, "Address to the Nation Announcing Answer to the House Judiciary Committee Subpoena for Additional Presidential Tape Recordings," April 29, 1974, The American Presidency Project, https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/address-the-nation-announcing-answer-the-house-judiciary-committee-subpoena-for-additional). In the House committee's debate a member spoke of "the edited transcripts sent to us by the White House" (House Committee on the Judiciary, *Debate on Articles of Impeachment*, 93d Cong., 2d Sess. (1974), 73); the President "released to the public partial, unauthenticated transcripts of these five conversations, among others" (S. Rep. No. 93-981, 1082); and "By order of May 2, 1974," the D.C. Circuit "took judicial notice of the President's public release of transcripts, with partial deletions, of each of the tapes at issue here" (498 F.2d at 732). The House committee's third article of impeachment charged that the President "has failed without lawful cause or excuse to produce papers and things as directed by duly authorized subpenas issued by the Committee on the Judiciary of the House of Representatives on April 11, 1974, May 15, 1974, May 30, 1974, and June 24, 1974" (*Debate on Articles of Impeachment*, 449), and the committee adopted it by 21 votes to 17 (id., 489).

[^tf-watergate-charges]: *United States v. Nixon*, 418 U.S. at 687 (on March 1, 1974 a grand jury "returned an indictment charging seven named individuals"); *Haldeman*, 559 F.2d 31 (the convictions of Haldeman, Ehrlichman, and Mitchell affirmed).

[^hf-church-defiance]: *Intelligence Activities, Senate Resolution 21: Hearings Before the Select Committee to Study Governmental Operations with Respect to Intelligence Activities*, 94th Cong., 1st Sess., vol. 5 (1975), pp. 51 (Senator Tower: "President Ford has personally and specifically requested of the committee that it be kept secret"), 57 (hearing of November 6, 1975; the committee's vote "of seven to three that the information should be made public"), and 58 (first inquiries "last May"; no response "until early September"). Snider, *Agency and the Hill*, 276 (the committee published the assassination report over the administration's objection after "presenting the issue to a secret session of the full Senate and noting an 'absence of disapproval'"; Snider dates the release to December 1975, while the report itself is dated November 20, 1975).

[^hf-pike-bargain]: *U.S. Intelligence Agencies and Activities: Committee Proceedings, Hearings Before the Select Committee on Intelligence, House of Representatives*, 94th Cong., 1st Sess., Part 4 (1975), pp. 1275–76 (Director Colby's letter of September 30, 1975, setting the conditions, under which the committee "would reserve its right to submit the matter to judicial determination"; Chairman Pike: "essentially the conditions which Mr. McClory and I discussed with the President") and 1286 (October 1: "The motion is agreed to by a vote of 10 to 3"; on the House floor Representative Broomfield later gave "a 9 to 3 majority," 122 Cong. Rec. 1640 (1976)); *U.S. Intelligence Agencies and Activities: Risks and Control of Foreign Intelligence*, Part 5 (1975), pp. 1601 (December 2: substantial compliance on the 40 Committee subpoena after members examined the material at the Executive Office Building) and 1771–72 (December 10: Pike, "It was, on the other hand, a compromise. We did not get everything we wanted, but we got more than they wanted to give us," and "substantial compliance on this subpena and the issue is moot"). Snider, *Agency and the Hill*, 200 (on September 18, 1975 Ford advised that the executive would provide no more classified materials, testimony, or interviews; on September 26, "at a White House meeting with Pike and the leadership of the House," the committee agreed that the President "would be the final arbiter") and 201 (Pike "contended his agreement did not extend to the committee's final report").

[^hf-ic-withheld]: Walsh, *Final Report*, vol. 1, pp. xvii ("Contemporaneous notes of Regan and Weinberger, and those dictated by Shultz, were withheld until they were obtained by Independent Counsel in 1991 and 1992"), xviii ("Regan in 1992 provided Independent Counsel with copies of notes"; in December 1992 the office learned that Bush "had failed to produce a diary"; "The statute of limitations had run on November 1986 activities before OIC obtained its evidence"), xix (of the Hill and Platt notes of Shultz's meetings, "The most revealing of these notes were not provided to any Iran/contra investigation until 1990 and 1991"), xx (Weinberger's notes, "which he deliberately withheld from Congress and the OIC until they were discovered by Independent Counsel in late 1991"), 24 (Meese "gathered his team around mid-day on November 21, 1986"; North "returned to his offices in the early evening of November 21 and shredded stacks of memoranda and messages"), and 25 (the FBI's criminal investigation began November 26, 1986; Independent Counsel was appointed on December 19, 1986; the select committees were established on January 6 and 7, 1987). *Report of the Congressional Committees Investigating the Iran-Contra Affair*, letter of transmittal of November 17, 1987, and Preface, p. xvi (NSC staff "shredded relevant contemporaneous documents in the fall of 1986"), and Executive Summary, p. 10 (after learning "that the President had authorized the Attorney General to gather the relevant facts," North and Poindexter "shredded and altered official documents on November 21, 1986, and later that weekend"). The President's review board reported on February 26, 1987: *Report of the President's Special Review Board*, John Tower, Chairman (Washington: Government Printing Office, 1987), as listed among the sources of the congressional report.

[^hf-immunity]: 18 U.S.C. §§ 6002, 6005 (immunity orders on the application of a congressional committee). Walsh, *Final Report*, vol. 1, p. 33 ("A compromise was reached between OIC and the Committees in March 1987, setting the timing for immunity grants for North and Poindexter"). *Report of the Congressional Committees Investigating the Iran-Contra Affair*, Appendix, p. 687 (the June 15, 1987 order compelling North to testify); the report cites North's public testimony on dates from July 7 to July 14, 1987 and Poindexter's from July 15 to July 21, 1987.

[^hf-1991-act]: Snider, *Agency and the Hill*, 66–68 (the recommendations of notice "no later than 48 hours" after a finding, written findings, and no retroactive findings; the two administrations' position: "While they were willing to compromise on the subsidiary issues (such as requiring findings to be in writing and the prohibition of retroactive findings) they would not accept a strict 48-hour time limit"; "The impasse lasted for almost four years"; legislation that "on one occasion, was vetoed"; the 1991 compromise, in which the "timely notice" language remained in the law and the conference report recorded the President's commitment to notify "within a few days") and 68 ("The Creation of a Statutory Inspector General for the CIA: 1989"). Intelligence Authorization Act, Fiscal Year 1991, Pub. L. No. 102-88, title VI, § 602(a)(2), 105 Stat. 442 (1991), codified at 50 U.S.C. § 3093 (a finding "shall be in writing" and "may not authorize or sanction a covert action ... which already has occurred"; statutory definition of covert action in § 3093(e)). President George H. W. Bush to Senator David Boren, October 30, 1989 ("I anticipate that notice will be provided within a few days. Any withholding beyond this period would be based upon my assertion of the authorities granted this office by the Constitution."). NSDD-286, October 15, 1987 ("No Finding may retroactively authorize or sanction a special activity").

[^hf-215-litigation]: *ACLU v. Clapper*, 785 F.3d 787, 826 (2d Cir. 2015) (the program "exceeds the scope of what Congress has authorized"; constitutional claims not reached); *Klayman v. Obama*, 800 F.3d 559 (D.C. Cir. 2015) (per curiam) (preliminary injunction vacated; Judge Williams found no substantial likelihood of standing, a view Judge Sentelle shared in partial dissent, and Judge Brown found bare standing but no substantial likelihood that the plaintiffs' own records were collected); *United States v. Moalin*, 973 F.3d 977, 996 (9th Cir. 2020) (the collection "exceeded the scope of Congress's authorization" in 50 U.S.C. § 1861), and id. at 984 (the government "may have violated the Fourth Amendment," but suppression was not warranted).

[^hf-cipa]: Walsh, *Final Report*, vol. 1, pp. xxi (the classification discretion "gives the attorney general the power to block almost any potentially embarrassing prosecution that requires the declassification of information"), xxiii (one case "dismissed when the Bush Administration declined to declassify information necessary for trial"), xxiv–xxv (Fernandez was indicted on June 20, 1988 for "obstructing the inquiry of the Tower Commission and making false statements to government agencies"; Judge Hilton dismissed the case on November 24, 1989, "after Attorney General Richard Thornburgh blocked the disclosure of classified information ruled relevant to the defense"), and 37 (on January 13, 1989 Judge Gesell dismissed "the central conspiracy and theft charges against North because the Reagan Administration refused to declassify certain information deemed relevant to these charges").

[^tt-discharge]: Office of Representative Thomas Massie, "Massie-Khanna 'Epstein Files Transparency Act' Passes House of Representatives," press release, November 18, 2025, https://massie.house.gov/news/documentsingle.aspx?DocumentID=395780 (the bill "received a vote after Rep. Massie successfully gathered 218 signatures on a discharge petition to force the legislation onto the House floor for consideration"); 171 *Cong. Rec.* H4725–H4733 (daily ed. November 18, 2025).

[^tt-guidance-docs]: A phrase search of the project's full-text index for "just redaction boxes" returns nine documents: seven copies of a two-page guidance sheet (EFTA00161485, EFTA00161592, EFTA00163142, EFTA00163144, EFTA01649097, EFTA01655727, EFTA01655729) and two scans of the March 21, 2025 training deck (EFTA00172329, EFTA00172446). Recount in the author's recomputation record of September 30, 2026, content version 1dc567730c04, available on request.

[^efta-log]: DOJ, Compliance Letter #1, December 19, 2025, pp. 3 ("A privilege log will be produced in due course as required under the Act") and 4 (the Department "will provide an explanation for any redacted and withheld materials as part of this production"). Blanche*, ECF 16, June 25, 2026, pp. 36 (the Attorney General "does not respond substantively" to the merits arguments, which are treated as conceded) and 46 ("The Act required the production of the covered documents and the redaction log by December 19, 2025"). [^tt-member-access]: DOJ, Compliance Letter #2, January 30, 2026, p. 4 (arrangements "made through the leadership of the House and Senate Committees on the Judiciary" and "subject to customary and appropriate confidentiality undertakings"); Section 3 Report, p. 3 ("certain members of Congress have come to the Department to inspect unredacted materials"); *Phang v. Blanche*, ECF 43, September 16, 2026, pp. 5 (the Department's position that the court "should not supplant Congress's acceptance of [its] explanation") and 16 (no authority "that Congressional silence in response to the January 30, 2026 letter functions as an implied amendment to the EFTA").

[^remedy-apa-gap]: *Phang v. Blanche*, ECF 16, pp. 17–18 (the Attorney General's arguments that the Act creates no private right of action, that the plaintiff lacks Article III standing, and that the APA waiver does not apply because FOIA is an adequate remedy) and 22 (informational injury, citing *FEC v. Akins*, 524 U.S. 11 (1998), and *Public Citizen v. U.S. Department of Justice*, 491 U.S. 440 (1989)); ECF 42 (administrative record); ECF 43, p. 10 (in camera review under the court's "inherent authority to review . . . material ex parte, in camera as part of its judicial review function"); appeals Nos. 26-5299 and 26-5334 (D.C. Cir.). Compare 5 U.S.C. § 552(a)(4)(B) (the court "shall determine the matter de novo," may examine records "in camera," and "the burden is on the agency to sustain its action") and (a)(4)(E)(ii) (a complainant "substantially prevailed" through "a voluntary or unilateral change in position by the agency"). The APA supplies no general fee entitlement, but eligible prevailing parties may recover under the Equal Access to Justice Act (EAJA), 28 U.S.C. § 2412(d)(1)(A), (2)(B), unless the Government's position was substantially justified or special circumstances make an award unjust. That conditional recovery is not an EFTA-specific fee provision. Unlike FOIA's express voluntary-disclosure provision, § 2412(d) does not expressly count a voluntary or unilateral agency change as substantially prevailing; the proposed FOIA-style provision would specify that catalyst route.

[^remedy-youngstown]: *Youngstown Sheet & Tube Co. v. Sawyer*, 343 U.S. 579, 637–38 (1952) (Jackson, J., concurring), as applied in *Zivotofsky*, 576 U.S. at 10 (in the third category the President's "asserted power must be both 'exclusive' and 'conclusive' on the issue"); *Trump v. United States*, 603 U.S. 593, 620–21 (2024) (the President's "exclusive authority over the investigative and prosecutorial functions of the Justice Department and its officials"). For a statute regulating control of presidential records the Court asks whether it "prevents the Executive Branch from accomplishing its constitutionally assigned functions" and only then whether "that impact is justified by an overriding need to promote objectives within the constitutional authority of Congress," *Nixon v. Administrator of General Services*, 433 U.S. 425, 443 (1977). Presidential communications carry "a presumptive privilege," *United States v. Nixon*, 418 U.S. at 708; applying the presumption of *Nixon v. Sirica*, the D.C. Circuit held on May 23, 1974 that the Senate committee had not shown the tapes "demonstrably critical to the responsible fulfillment of the Committee's functions," *Senate Select Committee*, 498 F.2d at 730–31. The Court has since distinguished those decisions as involving "Oval Office communications over which the President asserted executive privilege," declined "to transplant that protection root and branch to cases involving nonprivileged, private information," and set out "special considerations" for a congressional subpoena of the President's personal information, *Mazars*, slip op. at 13, 18–20. For the Department's deliberations, a district court held that "the qualified privilege invoked to shield material that the Department has already disclosed has been outweighed by a legitimate need that the Department does not dispute," *Lynch*, 156 F. Supp. 3d at 115; the Committee's appeal was held in abeyance while "settlement proceedings" continued and was dismissed on its unopposed motion on May 14, 2019 (D.C. Cir. No. 16-5078).

[^remedy-civil-enforcement]: Congressional Subpoena Compliance and Enforcement Act of 2017, H.R. 4010, 115th Cong. (engrossed in the House; passed October 23, 2017), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/BILLS-115hr4010eh/html/BILLS-115hr4010eh.htm, proposing 28 U.S.C. § 1365a: rules for "any civil action brought by the United States House of Representatives, the United States Senate, or a committee or subcommittee thereof," which "shall be filed in a United States district court of competent jurisdiction" ((a)(1)); a duty "to advance on the docket and to expedite to the greatest possible extent the disposition of any such action and appeal" ((a)(2)), a clause of the kind Table C.4 calls hortatory; a three-judge court if "expressly requested by the plaintiff in the initial pleading," which "shall be reviewable only by appeal directly to the Supreme Court," with notice of appeal "within 10 days" ((a)(3)); penalties "directly against the head of a Government agency or a component thereof held to have willfully failed to comply," with no appropriated or other Government funds to be used to pay them ((b)); and a privilege or other ground "(whether statutory, common law, or otherwise)" that "may be determined to have been waived" for a record not covered by a timely privilege log ((c)), the log that a new section 105 of the Revised Statutes would require of a recipient withholding a record, which "shall provide a log" stating "An express assertion and description of the legal basis asserted for withholding the record" (bill § 3). A statute confining that waiver would also have to say who decides, and at what stage, whether a ground is constitutionally based (for example, only on the President's personal assertion, subject to a judicial determination); whether an untimely log forfeits that characterization; and what standard governs statutory and common-law objections, the grounds a statute can fully govern. The bill grants neither a cause of action nor jurisdiction and does not amend 28 U.S.C. § 1365(a). A Senate suit requires "the adoption of a resolution by the Senate," 2 U.S.C. § 288b(b). Neither § 288b nor § 288d limits that resolution's debate. It is ordinarily debatable and exposed to a filibuster; if opponents sustain debate, cloture requires "three-fifths of the Senators duly chosen and sworn" (Standing Rules of the Senate, Rule XXII ¶ 2, S. Doc. No. 113-18 (2013), 16); a resolution directing suit "in the name of a committee or subcommittee" is out of order unless that committee reports it with four statements (§ 288d(c)). Privilege of consideration alone does not limit debate. An expedited procedure would need to specify treatment of the motion to proceed, debate on the resolution, amendments, and procedural appeals. On the merits and the courts' preference for settlement: Garvey, CRS Report R45653, pp. 22–23 (lower courts "have suggested that judicial intervention in such disputes 'should be delayed until all possibilities for settlement have been exhausted'"; a committee may overcome the presumptive privilege by showing "the subpoenaed evidence is demonstrably critical to the responsible fulfillment of the Committee's function"); *United States v. Nixon*, 418 U.S. at 708, 712 n.19. For the House authorization models, the Lee text of January 21, 2026 and H. Res. 430, 116th Cong. (2019) (a committee chair, "when authorized by the Bipartisan Legal Advisory Group," may initiate judicial proceedings). On penalties for an official following a presidential instruction, 8 Op. O.L.C. at 137 (Appendix C, Table C.3) (objecting to "inflicting punishment on an individual who failed to produce them"). The same opinion supports the civil route: Congress "could obtain a judicial resolution of the underlying privilege claim and vindicate its asserted right to obtain any documents by a civil action for enforcement of a congressional subpoena," prevailing "if it could establish that its need for the records outweighed the Executive's interest in preserving confidentiality" (id. at 137), and "there is little doubt that, at the very least, Congress may authorize civil enforcement of its subpoenas and grant jurisdiction to the courts to entertain such cases" (id. at 137 n.36). The Department later contested such suits: in *Miers* the executive moved to dismiss "on the grounds that the Committee lacks standing and a proper cause of action, that disputes of this kind are non-justiciable" (memorandum opinion of July 31, 2008, 1–2); in *Holder* the Attorney General took "the position that a dispute between the legislative and executive branches must be resolved through negotiation and accommodation, and that the judiciary may not, or at least, should not, get involved" (memorandum opinion, 1); and in the *Holder* appeal, "In the event the Circuit decided to hear the matter on the merits, the Department also reasserted the jurisdictional arguments based on standing and separation of powers" (*Sessions*, opinion of October 22, 2018, 9).

[^efta-ii-memo]: Alan ElSheshai, *Assessment of the Epstein Files Transparency Act II: H.R. 9694 / S. 5005* (EFTA II Memo, version 2.8.7, September 20, 2026; DisclosureWorks Publications, 2026-10 edition), M-3 ("The monetary remedies have no sovereign-immunity waiver") and M-9 (the parens patriae route), https://publications.disclosureworks.ai/legislation/efta-ii-memo/2026-10/. The absence of a separate waiver clause does not alone establish that conclusion. *Department of Agriculture Rural Development Rural Housing Service v. Kirtz*, 601 U.S. 42 (2024), slip op. at 5–6, recognizes waiver through an express cause of action against government; *FAA v. Cooper*, 566 U.S. 284, 291 (2012), requires discernible statutory scope, not magic words. H.R. 9694 § 4(a)(1) names the Attorney General of the United States as defendant and expressly authorizes monetary relief. That combination supports reading it as a waiver, but leaves the defendant's capacity, recoverable loss and payment source insufficiently specified. The drafting criticism is the scope and adequacy of that authorization, not the necessity of a separate immunity-stripping formula; a damages measure remains absent.

[^a-icd203]: United States, Office of the Director of National Intelligence, *Analytic Standards*, Intelligence Community Directive 203, January 2, 2015, as amended, § D.6.e(1)–(2), pp. 4–5 in the current eight-page PDF, https://archive.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-203.pdf. Standard (2) distinguishes "the likelihood of occurrence of an event or development, and the analyst's confidence in the basis for this judgment." Standard (1) separately addresses source quality and credibility. This directive does not validate a checklist or a numerical mapping; this appendix uses its checklist only to organize findings and applies no numerical mapping.

[^a-efta-prefix]: Throughout this paper, "EFTA" is the Bates prefix for the U.S. Department of Justice EFTA Disclosures production; a parenthetical Bates number (e.g. EFTA02656963) is the primary-document citation for the item it follows. Each Bates is cited inline at its first mention only; later references to the same document do not repeat the citation.

[^a-six-criteria-weighting]: This appendix applies no weighting or probability-elevation rule. The eleven headings organize bounded source findings; they do not add up to an intelligence-operation probability. Ordinary financial structures, third-party conduct, and familial association are not independent evidence of an intelligence operation.

[^a-barak-2013-recording]: EFTA01621008 is a 3-hour-27-minute media carrier with machine transcription. This paper has no auditable timecodes or secure speaker identification for it, and does not rely on it to confirm Summers's identity, a February 14, 2013 recording date, or particular utterances.

[^a-fd1023]: FBI, *Confidential Human Source Reporting Document*, form FD-1023, October 19, 2020, EFTA00090314–EFTA00090316, pp. 1–3 (FBI case 804I-LA-3315657-INTELPRODS). The form records a confidential human source's account that Epstein "trained as a spy under" Ehud Barak and was "a co-opted Mossad Agent." An FD-1023 documents an uncorroborated source's reporting, not an FBI finding or endorsement; it does not establish formal intelligence-service status, and it is cited only as an official-government record of an uncorroborated source allegation, without adopting the allegation or inferring an intelligence-operational character. The report answers a handling agent's question about "improper domestic or foreign influence over the electoral process in the U.S.," and its Epstein paragraph sits among other uncorroborated assertions about named people; the recollection about Acosta is at p. 1. The p. 1 header records a numbered confidential source, October 16 contact by encrypted messaging, and an October 19 report; p. 3 records submission and first-level approval. Neither is a reliability evaluation. The source claims call access and note-taking but the notes and referenced prior reports are not supplied in these three pages. The carrier gives no source track record, reliability grade, independent validation of the Epstein claims, or basis for assessing the source's motives. Because its assessor is an anonymous source rather than a named, credentialed intelligence-community analyst, it is not official or independent analyst corroboration of Criterion 5.

[^a-intercept-thomas]: The non-publication is independently documented in The Intercept, "Epstein Gave NY Times Journalist Tips About Trump. Why Did They Never Get Reported?", November 14, 2025, https://theintercept.com/2025/11/14/epstein-new-york-times-trump/, which reports that the Trump-related material Epstein supplied to New York Times reporter Landon Thomas Jr. never appeared in print.

[^a-criterion1-records]: U.S. Department of Justice, EFTA production: EFTA00635218, July 1–6, 2015 introduction and replies (Data Set 9); EFTA01057777, December 29, 2016 Epstein-to-Pritzker email (Data Set 9); EFTA02200098, January 9, 2017 message with January 10 calendar (Data Set 10); EFTA02448518, October 4–5, 2016 Epstein–Thiel exchange (Data Set 11); EFTA02040190, October 7, 2016 lunch arrangements (Data Set 10). The calendar supplies a scheduled lunch, not a record of its discussion or independent proof of attendance. The searches were not exhaustive and the productions were not deduplicated; those limits do not affect the distinct identities of the two U.S. counterparties.

Software: Parallax EFTA full-text-search utility over the EFTA indexed corpus; phrase query `"Mega Group"` and exact-token query `MegaGroup`, each with `--limit 25 --json`: six document hits and, on snippet review, zero identifiable Epstein emails (one web-search capture, one unrelated OCR phrase, one Nu Skin article, one fashion article, one Wexner biographical clipping, and one 151-page secondary report, EFTA02856277); `MegaGroup` returned zero hits. These are Ostrovsky's memoir claims, not official findings. The 1990 definition passage contains no Maxwell attribution, and a case-insensitive Maxwell search of the book's full text returned no hits. This is a bounded text-layer negative, not proof against extraction omissions.
